Writing `'l1.end'` as the start of `l2` makes the two lines share a point, so the corners stay closed. `horizontal` and `vertical` keep the sides square, `fix('l1.start')` pins the corner at the origin, and two dimensions size it: `l1` is `w` and `l2` is `d`.\n\nHole `h1` has its diameter from `hole_d` and its centre placed by two dimensions, 12 mm from the left edge and half the depth up. The compile reports 1 DOF left, `h2:center.x`, as a `sketch.underConstrained` warning, and the sketch inspector lists it under *Still free*. Dragging on the sheet solves only the cluster that holds `h2`, from where it is now, so nothing else moves.\n\nTo fix the last DOF, add a dimension for `h2` from the right edge, for example `.dim({ h: ['h2.center', 'l2.start'] }, 'edge')`, and press Run: the status becomes fully constrained, 0 DOF.\n\n## Where next\n\n[A-01](lab:preset/A-01) uses a rectangle helper instead, which constrains itself. Hole h2 matches h1's size and height,\n// but its x position is left free on purpose: open the sketch from the\n// stage's Sketch menu and drag it along the plate.\nconst plate = j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .line('l1', [0, 0], [80, 0])\n  .line('l2', 'l1.end', [80, 40])\n  .line('l3', 'l2.end', [0, 40])\n  .line('l4', 'l3.end', 'l1.start')\n  .circle('h1', 2, { at: [12, 20] })\n  .circle('h2', 2, { at: [60, 20] })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('l1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('l1'), j.c.horizontal('l3'),\n    j.c.vertical('l2'), j.c.vertical('l4'),\n    j.c.equal('h2', 'h1'),\n  )\n  .dim('l1', 'w')\n  .dim('l2', 'd')\n  .dim('h1', 'hole_d')\n  .dim({ h: ['l1.start', 'h1.center'] }, 'edge')\n  .dim({ v: ['l1.start', 'h1.center'] }, 'd / 2')\n  .dim({ v: ['h1.center', 'h2.center'] }, 0)\n\nreturn j.part('plate')\n  .params({ w: mm(80), d: mm(40), t: mm(4), hole_d: mm(4.5),\n    edge: mm(12) })\n  .expose(['w', 'd', 'hole_d'], {\n    labels: { w: 'width', d: 'depth', hole_d: 'hole diameter' },\n    ranges: { w: [mm(40), mm(160)], d: [mm(20), mm(80)],\n      hole_d: [mm(2), mm(8)] },\n  })\n  .extrude(plate.profiles(), 't', { id: 'body' })\n"}},{"id":"A-06","title":"Extrude and revolve","group":"Foundations","about":"A plate with four holes and a ring revolved on it: Cavalieri and Pappus as volumes you can check.","view":"axo","overlays":"shadows,grid,dims","files":{"notes.md":"# Extrude and revolve\n\nA plate 80 × 50 × 4 mm with four M3 clearance holes, and a ring revolved on top of it. The two features have closed-form volumes, so the kernel's answer can be checked by hand: an extrude is its profile's area times its height (Cavalieri), a revolve is its section's area times the distance its centroid travels (Pappus).\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the plate's underside](lab:select/body/start), then [look from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side). Circles inside a closed outline are holes of the profile, so one extrude makes the plate with its holes already cut:\n\n$$V = (80 \\cdot 50 - 4 (1 - \\pi/4) 4^2 - 4 \\pi 1.7^2) \\cdot 4$$\n\n## The revolve\n\nThe section is a 3 × 8 mm rectangle on the XZ plane whose centroid sits `ring_r` from the Z axis. give holeComp as a length, taken at the size that matters."},"defaultFragments":["measure","profile"]},"files":{"measured.json":"{\n  \"source\": \"example: Prusa MK4, 0.4 mm nozzle, generic PLA, 0.2 mm layers\",\n  \"pin\": 5.97,\n  \"holes\": [5.93, 6.03, 6.13, 6.22, 6.33, 6.43, 6.52, 6.63, 6.72, 6.83],\n  \"outcomes\": [\"no\", \"press\", \"snug\", \"slide\", \"slide\",\n    \"free\", \"free\", \"free\", \"free\", \"free\"]\n}\n","notes.md":"# Fit coupon\n\nA strip of ten holes for a Ø6 mm pin, with radial gaps from 0.05 mm to 0.50 mm in 0.05 mm steps, ten loose pins printed beside it, and a proof block. You print it once per printer and material, measure it, type the numbers into `measured.json`, and `j.fit.calibrate` turns them into the fit profile every other preset sizes its holes from. The idea it teaches: clearances are measured, not guessed.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan) to see the holes grow from left to right. [Select the slide hole of the proof block](lab:select/slide/bore[1]) and read its size, then [pretend your pin measured 6.05 mm](lab:param/pin=6.05) and watch all three proof holes grow by 0.08 mm.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole', pin, range, steps })` makes the plate and the pins. It is modelled raw: no fit class and no hole compensation, so a hole modelled at 6.30 mm shows exactly what your printer does with 6.30 mm. The holes sit at a pitch of pin + 2 × largest gap + 4 mm = 11 mm. The script adds a 6 mm strip behind the plate and engraves the first and last gap in hundredths (`5` and `50`), 0.6 mm deep.\n\nThe pins stand 8 mm tall, higher than the 4 mm plate, so the label cut gives its extent as `{ two: [mm(0), mm(0.6)] }`: 0.6 mm down from the top face and nothing up. A plain `mm(0.6)` would cut upwards, into air.\n\n## What to measure\n\n1. Measure a pin across two directions with callipers and write the mean as `pin` (the example has 5.97 mm).\n2. Measure each hole, with the callipers' inside jaws or with drill shanks, and write the ten sizes into `holes`, smallest gap first.\n3. Push a pin into each hole by hand and write one word per hole into `outcomes`: `press` (goes in only with a clamp or a tap), `snug` (goes in by hand and holds), `slide` (moves freely, no play), `free` (drops through and rattles), or `no` (does not go in).\n\n## From measurements to a profile\n\n`j.fit.calibrate` fits a line through the holes, measured = a × nominal + b, and takes each class from the first hole with that outcome: the gap is (hole − pin) / 2. With the example numbers that is a = 0.996 and b = −0.145 mm, and press 0.03 mm, snug 0.08 mm, slide 0.125 mm, free 0.23 mm. The script turns the line into a hole compensation of 0.171 mm, (T − b) / a − T at the pin size, and builds the profile with `j.fit.profile('my-printer', { classes, holeComp, xyComp })`.\n\nThe print job carries that profile, so the proof block's holes come out at Ø6.20 mm (press), Ø6.39 mm (slide) and Ø6.60 mm (free). Print it with your next job: when a coupon pin presses, slides and drops as named, the profile is right.\n\n> **Careful.** `measured.json` ships with numbers from one printer. Replace them with yours before you trust a fit.\n\n## Printing it\n\nFlat on the bed, no supports, about 12 g of PLA and 33 min. Print at the layer height and in the material you will use for real parts: the gaps depend on both.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [C-04 Print-in-place clearance](lab:preset/C-04): the gap for parts printed already assembled.\n- [C-05 Hole sizes](lab:preset/C-05): more points for the hole line, vertical and horizontal.\n- [D-04 Battery holder](lab:preset/D-04): pockets sized with `j.fit.hole`.\n","script.js":"// C-01 · Fit coupon: ten holes in 0.05 mm steps, ten loose pins,\n// a proof block.\n// Notice that the coupon is j.fit.coupon, and that measured.json becomes the\n// fit profile of this printer through j.fit.calibrate.\n\n// The coupon: Ø6 mm pins, radial gaps 0.05 to 0.50 mm in ten steps. It is\n// modelled raw (no fit classes, no hole compensation): it measures the printer.\nconst pin = mm(6), lo = mm(0.05), hi = mm(0.5), steps = 10\nconst coupon = j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole', pin, range: [lo, hi], steps })\nconst { gaps } = coupon.meta.sets.coupon\n\n// j.fit.coupon spaces the holes at pin + 2 × largest gap + 4 mm on a plate\n// pin + 8 mm deep and 4 mm thick; the labels go on a strip behind it.\nconst pitch = pin.value + 2 * hi.value + 4, depth = pin.value + 8\nconst strip = j.region.rect(pitch * steps, 6, { center: false, at: [0, depth] })\n// The smallest and the largest gap are labelled in hundredths of a\n// millimetre (5 = 0.05 mm); the holes between step evenly. Text is costly to\n// cut (every glyph curve is a face), so two labels, not ten.\nconst label = i => j.region.text(String(Math.round(gaps[i] * 100)), {\n  size: mm(3), at: [pitch * (i + 0.5), depth + 3],\n})\nconst labels = label(0).union(label(steps - 1))\nconst top = j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(4))\nconst labelled = coupon\n  .extrude(strip, mm(4), { id: 'strip' })\n  // the pins stand above the plate, so say which way the cut goes: 0.6 mm\n  // down from the top face, nothing up\n  .cut(labels.on(top), { two: [mm(0), mm(0.6)] }, { id: 'labels' })\n\n// What you measured (see the notes): one row per coupon. Holes pair the\n// nominal size the coupon modelled with the size you measured.\nconst meas = data.measured\nconst cal = j.fit.calibrate([{\n  pin: mm(meas.pin),\n  holes: gaps.map((g, i) => [mm(pin.value + 2 * g), mm(meas.holes[i])]),\n  outcomes: meas.outcomes,\n  coupon: coupon.meta.sets.coupon,\n}])\n// Holes print as a·nominal + b, so a hole modelled (T − b) / a comes out at\n// T. The compensation is taken at the pin size: over 6 to 7 mm it changes by\n// less than 0.01 mm. Pins print pinOversize too large; xyComp takes half of it\n// off each side.\nconst T = pin.value\nconst profile = j.fit.profile('my-printer', {\n  classes: cal.classes,\n  holeComp: mm((T - cal.hole.b) / cal.hole.a - T),\n  xyComp: mm(-cal.pinOversize / 2),\n})\n\n// The proof block: three holes for the pin you measured, sized from your\n// profile. The pin should press into the left hole, slide through the middle\n// one and drop through the right one. It sits behind the coupon on the stage;\n// the job packs the plate anyway.\nconst at = [pitch * steps / 2, depth + 20]\nconst proof = j.part('proof')\n  .params({ pin: mm(meas.pin), w: mm(42), d: mm(14), h: mm(6) })\n  .expose(['pin'], {\n    labels: { pin: 'pin diameter as measured' },\n    ranges: { pin: [mm(5.8), mm(6.2)] },\n  })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { at }), 'h', { id: 'block' })\n  .hole([[at[0] - 13, at[1]]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'press')),\n    { id: 'press' })\n  .hole([at], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'slide')), { id: 'slide' })\n  .hole([[at[0] + 13, at[1]]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'free')),\n    { id: 'free' })\n\nreturn j.print('fit-coupon', {\n  printer: j.printer('generic-fdm'), material: j.material('PLA'),\n  fit: profile, parts: [labelled, proof],\n}).orient('+Z')\n"}},{"id":"C-02","title":"Overhang test","group":"Calibration","order":2,"difficulty":"easy","about":"six ledges leaning 20° to 70° from vertical: print without supports and find the angle where your printer starts to droop","view":"front","overlays":"shadows,grid,dims","tries":"Test 45° to 70° in 5° steps instead | Give my printer a 55° overhang limit and show what changes in the job | Explain why the 50° underside is reported and the 40° one is not","agent":{"role":"a print-process engineer who designs test pieces that isolate one printer limit","prompt":"Prefer changing the angles list and h over new geometry; keep every ledge the same height so only the angle varies; never add supports or chamfers to the test faces.","fragments":{"angles":"Keep angles in degrees from vertical, the convention of print.overhang and the printer's maxOverhang."},"defaultFragments":["angles"]},"files":{"notes.md":"# Overhang test\n\nSix ledges in a row, each rising 8 mm while it leans out over air, with undersides at 20°, 30°, 40°, 50°, 60° and 70° from vertical. Print it without supports and the ledge where the underside first turns rough or droops is your printer's overhang limit. The idea it teaches: an overhang is an angle, and one expression, `h * tan(θ)`, turns that angle into geometry.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front): the six undersides fan out from one point. [Select the 50° underside](lab:select/fin50/side[f50.edge.3]), the first one past the 45° rule. Then [make the ledges rise 12 mm](lab:param/h=12) and watch the 70° ledge reach 33 mm.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`angles` is a plain list at the top of the script. For each angle a sketch on its own XZ plane draws one closed polyline: a post `t` = 4 mm thick, an underside from (`t`, `base`) to (`t + h * tan(θ)`, `base + h`), and a flat top `cap` = 1.2 mm thick. Each profile is extruded `w` = 8 mm, and the fins stand `pitch` = 11 mm apart on a 2 mm shelf. With `h` = 8 mm the ledges reach 2.9, 4.6, 6.7, 9.5, 13.9 and 22.0 mm.\n\nThe XZ plane's normal is −Y, so each sketch sits at the fin's far side (`-(i * pitch + w)`) and the extrude comes back towards the shelf. The first and last angles, `20` and `70`, are engraved on the shelf; the others step by 10° between them.\n\n## Reading the result\n\nThe job reports `print.overhang` as info: 414.7 mm² past 45° with +Z up, which is the 50°, 60° and 70° undersides. That is the point of the test, so the orientation is fixed with `.orient('+Z')` and supports should be off in your slicer.\n\nMost printers with good part cooling print 50° cleanly, 60° with a rough underside and 70° with visible droop. If 40° is already rough, look at cooling before you blame the geometry. The 45° that `j.rules.fdm` and the orientation search use comes from the printer profile (`maxOverhang`); a printer that passes 55° can be given that.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright, about 6 g of PLA and 20 min (the estimate counts tree supports under the three steep ledges; print without them). Keep the fan at full speed from the second layer.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [C-03 Bridging test](lab:preset/C-03): the 90° case, a span held at both ends.\n- [C-05 Hole sizes](lab:preset/C-05): why horizontal holes become teardrops.\n","script.js":"// C-02 · Overhang test: six ledges whose undersides lean 20° to 70° from\n// vertical. Notice that each ledge is one polyline whose reach is h · tan(θ),\n// and that the job reports the ledges past 45° as overhang on purpose.\nconst angles = [20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70]   // degrees from vertical\n\n// One fin per angle, drawn in its own XZ plane (normal −Y, so the plane\n// sits at the fin's far side and the extrude runs back towards it):\n// a post t thick, then a ledge whose underside rises h while it reaches\n// h · tan(θ), capped by a flat top `cap` thick.\nconst fin = (a, i) => j.sketch(`f${a}`, {\n  on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XZ, `-(${i} * pitch + w)`),\n}).polyline('edge', [\n  [0, 0], ['t', 0], ['t', 'base'],\n  [`t + h * tan(${a}°)`, 'base + h'],\n  [`t + h * tan(${a}°)`, 'base + h + cap'],\n  [0, 'base + h + cap'],\n], { closed: true })\n\nlet test = j.part('overhang-test')\n  .params({\n    h: mm(8),          // rise of every ledge\n    t: mm(4),          // the post the ledges grow from\n    w: mm(8),          // width of a fin (along Y)\n    pitch: 'w + 3 mm', // fin to fin\n    cap: mm(1.2),      // top of each ledge: six layers\n    base: mm(2),       // the ledges start this far above the bed\n    shelf: mm(12),     // label shelf behind the posts\n  })\n  .expose(['h', 'w', 'cap'], {\n    labels: { h: 'rise of each ledge', w: 'fin width', cap: 'ledge thickness' },\n    ranges: { h: [mm(4), mm(15)], w: [mm(5), mm(15)], cap: [mm(0.6), mm(3)] },\n  })\n  // the shelf: a plate behind the posts that ties the fins together\n  .extrude(j.sketch('foot', { on: j.plane.XY })\n    .rect('plate', 'shelf', `${angles.length} * pitch - The printer has to lay each span across thin air between two pillars. Look underneath after printing: the longest span that is still flat is the bridge length you can design for. The idea it teaches: the openings are cut out of one solid bar by one sketch, so the test is two extrudes and a cut, whatever the number of spans.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front): every opening shows. A bridge is a 90° overhang held at both ends, and slicers print it differently from a ledge: straight lines across the gap, fan at full speed, often slower and cooler. The printer profile says bridges up to 10 mm need no thought; this test tells you what yours really does.\n\nTypical results in PLA: up to 20 mm flat, 30 mm with a little sag in the first layer, 40 mm drooping 0.5 to 1 mm. 0.60 mm in 0.05 mm steps. After printing, try to turn each one with a screwdriver: the smallest gap that turns freely is the clearance your printer needs for hinges, chains and anything else printed assembled. The idea it teaches: a gap that has to survive printing is a different number from a gap that has to fit.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan): the bottom row starts at `15` (0.15 mm), the top row at `40`. [Select the waist of the fourth rotor](lab:select/rotor4/side[r4.half.3]), the 0.30 mm one. Then [make the plate 8 mm thick](lab:param/T=8) and see every rotor grow with it.\n\n## How it is built\n\nEvery hole is the same hourglass: a Ø8 mm waist with a 45° cone 1.5 mm high at each face, so Ø11 mm at the top and at the bed. Two hole features drill all ten: `top` is a countersink from a sketch of points on the top face, `bottom` the same countersink from a sketch on `j.plane.XY.flip()`, the bed looking up. Flipping a plane keeps x and turns y round, so the points underneath are written at −y.\n\nEach rotor is its own `revolve` of one polyline: the hole's outline moved inwards by its gap `g`, measured square to every face. On the straight waist that is `g`; on the 45° cones it is `g · √2` sideways. A rotor is wider than the waist at both ends, so it cannot fall out, and a 6 mm knob with a 1.2 mm slot stands 3 mm proud to turn it by.\n\nThe rotor axes are placed with numbers (`pitch = 15`), not params: in this engine a revolve axis through points written as expressions comes out tilted.\n\n## Reading the result\n\nTurn each rotor a full turn. Write one word per rotor, smallest gap first: `fused` (does not move), `stiff` (breaks free and then turns with effort), `free` (turns with a finger). The pitch is a number, not a\n// param: a revolve axis through points given as expressions comes out wrong\n// in this engine, so the axes are placed with numbers.\nconst pitch = 15\nconst cell = i =>\n  [(i % cols + 0.5) * pitch, (Math.floor(i / cols) + 0.5) * pitch]\n\n// The hole is an hourglass: a 45° cone from Ø(d + 2c) at each face to the\n// Ø d waist. down through a 5 mm plate and the same seven drilled sideways through a 6 mm wall standing on it. Printed holes come out small, and sideways holes come out a different shape; this piece measures both on your printer. The idea it teaches: the kernel turns every hole whose axis lies flat into a teardrop, because it knows which way the part is printed.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the sideways 6 mm hole](lab:select/across6/bore[c]) and then [its left teardrop flank](lab:select/across6/tear.l[c]). [Open the trace](lab:show/trace) and find the seven `teardrop` decisions. Then [space the holes 18 mm apart](lab:param/pitch=18).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`plate` and `wall` are two extrusions from the bed: a 98 × 20 × 5 mm plate and an 18 mm wall behind it. Each size gets two hole features, `down<d>` and `across<d>`, each at one sketch point. The `down` sketches lie on the top of the plate, so their holes drill straight down. The `across` sketches lie on `j.plane.XZ` moved to the wall's front face; that plane faces −Y, and a hole drills against its sketch's normal, so they go +Y through the wall.\n\nThe holes are `j.hole.simple(mm(d))`: the size as modelled, with no fit class and no hole compensation. That is what a test needs. For real parts you pass `j.fit.hole(d, 'slide')` and the profile adds the clearance and the compensation.\n\n## Teardrops\n\nThe job prints with +Z up (`.orient('+Z')`), so every `across` hole has its axis at right angles to the build direction. The kernel records a decision for each, \"axis +Y ⟂ build direction +Z → 45° teardrop, tip toward +Z\", and adds two flat flanks that meet above the hole. A round hole lying on its side would have a flat ceiling at the top, which sags; the teardrop's roof is two 45° overhangs, which print. Orient the job with `+Y` up and the roles swap: the wall's holes come out round and the plate's become teardrops.\n\n## What to measure\n\nMeasure every hole with the shanks of drill bits (the largest that goes in) or with callipers. For the sideways holes measure the width, not the height: the height includes the tip. Most printers make Ø2 and Ø3 holes 0.2 to 0.3 mm small and large holes 0.1 to 0.2 mm small; sideways holes often come out 0.1 mm smaller again.\n\nThe vertical pairs make a row for `j.fit.calibrate` in [C-01](lab:preset/C-01), which then fits its hole line over 2 to 10 mm instead of 6 to 7 mm:\n\n```js\n{ holes: [[2, 1.75], [3, 2.78], [4, 3.8], [5, 4.82], [6, 5.85],\n  [8, 7.86], [10, 9.88]] }\n```\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright, about 14 g of PLA and 42 min, no supports.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [C-01 Fit coupon](lab:preset/C-01): from hole sizes to fit classes.\n- [C-02 Overhang test](lab:preset/C-02): why the teardrop's roof is 45°.\n","script.js":"// C-05 · Hole sizes: seven nominal holes, Ø2 to Ø10 mm, drilled down through\n// a plate and sideways through a wall. 0.45 mm lines. Slice it before you print it. The walls that are whole numbers of lines fill solid; the ones in between show what your slicer does with the remainder: a gap, a thin infill line, or a wall it quietly drops. The idea it teaches: a wall is a number of extrusion lines, and `lw` writes it that way.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the face of the 1.5-line fin](lab:select/fins/side[fins.t2.left]) and read its thickness in the inspector. [Open the params](lab:show/params): the fins are not params, they are `1.5 lw` and so on, resolved with the printer's line width. Then [make the fins 25 mm tall](lab:param/h=25) for a stiffer test of single-line walls.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`widths` is a list in line widths. One sketch, `fins`, holds a rectangle `${w} lw` × `len` for each, at x = k × `gap`, and one extrude stands all six on the 1.6 mm `base`. The unit `lw` is symbolic: it stays `1.5lw` until the job resolves it with the printer's `lw`, 0.45 mm for `generic-fdm`, 0.42 mm for `x1c`. Change the printer and every fin follows.\n\nThe first and last widths, `1` and `4`, are engraved on the strip in front, 0.6 mm deep, cut downwards because the fins stand above the sketch plane.\n\n## Reading the result\n\nIn the slicer's preview, look at each fin from above:\n\n- **1 line** (0.45 mm): one perimeter, the thinnest wall that prints at all. Many slicers need \"detect thin walls\" on.\n- **2, 3, 4 lines**: whole numbers of perimeters, solid.\n- **1.5 and 2.5 lines**: the slicer has half a line left over. Arachne-style slicers vary the line width and fill it; older ones leave a gap or drop it.\n\nAfter printing, squeeze each fin: anything under 2 lines (0.9 mm) bends easily, which is why `j.rules.fdm` reports shell walls thinner than 0.9 mm and the presets on this shelf use `lw(2)` or more.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright, about 5 g of PLA and 16 min, no supports.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [C-03 Bridging test](lab:preset/C-03): the other slicer limit, spans.\n- [A-01 A box with walls](lab:preset/A-01): a shell with a wall parameter.\n","script.js":"// C-06 · Wall thickness: six fins from one to four line widths thick.\n// Notice that the thicknesses are symbolic (`1.5 lw`): they follow the\n// printer's line width, so the same test fits a 0.4 or a 0.6 mm nozzle.\nconst widths = [1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4]   // in line widths\nconst n = widths.length\n\n// fin k: a wall widths[k] line widths thick, `len` long and `h` tall,\n// standing on the base at x = k · gap; all six in one sketch\nconst fins = widths.reduce((sk, w, k) => sk.rect(`t${k + 1}`, `${w} lw`,\n  'len', { at: [`${k} * gap`, 'front'] }),\nj.sketch('fins', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'base') }))\n\nconst test = j.part('wall-test')\n  .params({\n    len: mm(20),     // fin length (along Y)\n    h: mm(12),       // fin height above the base\n    gap: mm(7),      // fin to fin\n    base: mm(1.6),\n    front: mm(7),    // label strip in front of the fins\n  })\n  .expose(['len', 'h', 'gap'], {\n    labels: { len: 'fin length', h: 'fin height', gap: 'fin spacing' },\n    ranges: { len: [mm(10), mm(40)], h: [mm(5), mm(30)], gap: [mm(5), mm(15)] },\n  })\n  .extrude(j.sketch('foot', { on: j.plane.XY })\n    .rect('plate', `${n - The idea it teaches is printability by shape: the windows are pointed at both ends, so their tops print as 45° slopes instead of a bridge that needs support.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select one of the flats](lab:select/body/side[base.hex.2]), then [make the windows 20 mm wide](lab:param/win_w=20) and [look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front). The selector asks for the concave edges of `pocket` at the floor height, `ctx.p.floor`, so it still finds them when the floor changes. The scoop radius equals the corner radius (6 mm): with equal radii the corners become quarter spheres, and the largest radius that works is the corner radius.\n\n## Configurations\n\n`configs` holds three layouts: `pens` (4 × 1, 80 mm deep), `mixed` (3 × 2) and `parts` (4 × 3, 25 mm tall). The idea it teaches is sizing a pocket from a standard and a gap: the hexagon is 6.35 mm across the flats (ISO 1173 E6.3) plus 0.15 mm on each flat, and the spacing follows from that.\n\n> **Try it.** [Make the rows twelve wide](lab:param/per_row=12) and watch the block grow, then [loosen the pockets to 0.25 mm a flat](lab:param/gap=0.25). [Select the middle step](lab:select/body/side[side.p.5]) and [look at the steps from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`side` is the stepped side profile on the YZ plane: three treads 14 mm deep (`row`) rising by `rise`. `body` extrudes it symmetrically along X to `w`, which is the row of pockets plus 3 mm at each end.\n\nEach row is its own sketch on its own tread height: one hexagon `hex` at the left end of the row and a linear pattern of it, `per_row` long. A loop in the script cuts the three rows (`row1`, `row2`, `row3`) down by `depth`; the extent is negative because each sketch plane faces up and the cut goes down.\n\n## The pocket and the spacing\n\nThe hexagon is drawn by its apothem, `af / 2 + gap`, so the pocket measures 6.65 mm across the flats. A 0.15 mm gap sits between the snug (0.10 mm) and slide (0.20 mm) fit classes: bits stay put when the block is tipped and still pull out with two fingers. The corners point along the row, so the pitch is the width across the corners plus the web:\n\n```js\npitch: 'af / cos(30 deg) + 2 * gap + web',   // 7.33 + 0.30 + 2.25 = 9.88 mm\n```\n\n`web` is `lw(5)`, five line widths of plastic at the narrowest point between two pockets.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright, as it sits on the bench: 81.3 cm³ solid, about 47 g of PETG with 15 % infill and 124 min. The idea it teaches is a layout driven by a table: `cards.csv` has one row per card format, the script makes one column per row, and the block's width and depth are expressions the script writes from the table.\n\n> **Try it.** [Loosen the slots to 0.4 mm a side](lab:param/gap=0.4), then [hold less of each card](lab:param/hold=0.35) and watch the block get lower. [Select the front face](lab:select/body/side[base.r.bottom]) and [look from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan).\n\n## The table\n\n```text\ncard,label,w,t,h,count\nSD,SD,24,2.1,32,8\nmicroSD,µSD,11,1,15,12\n```\n\nSizes are the SD Association's: an SD card is 32 × 24 × 2.1 mm, a microSD card 15 × 11 × 1.0 mm. A card stands on its short edge `w`, so `h` is its height in the slot. Add a row for another format (miniSD is 21.5 × 20 × 1.4 mm) and the block gains a column.\n\n## Expressions written from the table\n\nThe script joins the rows into parameter expressions, so the sizes still show in Params and still follow `gap` and `wall`:\n\n```js\nW: `${sum(r => `${r.w} mm`)} + ${2 * n} * gap + ${n + 1} * wall`,\nD: `max(${lengthOf.join(', ')}) + label + wall`,\n```\n\nWith the two rows above, `W` is 24 mm + 11 mm + 4 × 0.25 mm + 3 × 4 mm = 48 mm, and `D` is the longer column (twelve microSD slots, 45.5 mm) plus the 9 mm label band and a 4 mm wall: 58.5 mm.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`body` is a rounded block, `W` × `D` × `H`, where `H` holds the tallest card to `hold` of its height plus a 1.6 mm floor. Notice that the table (cards.csv) decides the\n// columns, and that the block's size is an expression built from its rows.\n// Card sizes (SD Association): SD 32 × 24 × 2.1 mm,\n// microSD 15 × 11 × 1.0 mm.\nconst cards = data.cards.rows        // card, label, w, t, h, count (mm)\nconst n = cards.length\n\n// Expressions built from the table: the block grows with every new row.\nconst sum = f => cards.map(f).join(' + ')\nconst widthOf = cards.map(r => `${r.w} mm + 2 * gap`)\nconst lengthOf = cards.map(r =>\n  `${r.count} * (${r.t} mm + 2 * gap) + ${r.count - 1} * web`)\n\nlet holder = j.part('card-holder')\n  .params({\n    gap: mm(0.25),        // per side: a card drops in and stands upright\n    web: mm(2.5),         // plastic between two cards of one column\n    wall: mm(4),          // between columns and round the edge\n    hold: 0.5,            // how much of a card's height sits in its slot\n    floor: layer(8),      // 1.6 mm under the deepest slot\n    label: mm(9),         // band in front of the slots for the engraved name\n    text: mm(3.5),        // cap height of the names\n    W: `${sum(r => `${r.w} mm`)} + ${2 * n} * gap + ${n + 1} * wall`,\n    D: `max(${lengthOf.join(', ')}) + label + wall`,\n    H: `floor + hold * ${Math.max(...cards.map(r => r.h))} mm`,\n  })\n  .expose(['gap', 'web', 'hold'], {\n    labels: {\n      gap: 'gap on each side of a card', web: 'web between cards',\n      hold: 'share of the card held',\n    },\n    ranges: {\n      gap: [mm(0.1), mm(0.5)], web: [mm(1.5), mm(5)], hold: [0.3, 0.7],\n    },\n  })\n  .extrude(j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n    .rect('r', 'W', 'D', { at: [0, 0], r: mm(3) }).profiles(),\n  'H', { id: 'body' })\n\n// The top face; It is one side profile extruded 30 mm wide, tightened from below by an M6 bolt that threads through a captive nut. The idea it teaches is orientation for strength: the part prints lying on its side, so the layers follow the hook round its bend instead of stacking across it.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the profile from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front), then [fit it to a 38 mm worktop](lab:param/desk=38) and [widen the hook for a thick headband](lab:param/reach=55). [Select the floor of the hook](lab:select/body/side[side.p.3]).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`side` is a closed polyline on the XZ plane with the desk top at z = 0: a top jaw, a spine, a bottom jaw 12 mm deep and, below it, the hook. Every corner is an expression, and two params are derived so the hook follows the desk:\n\n```js\nthroat: 'desk + travel',              // 25 + 8 = 33 mm between the jaws\nsaddle: '-throat - bj - drop',        // the floor of the hook, 55 mm below the desk top\n```\n\n`body` extrudes the profile 30 mm (`b`). `hook` is a selector kept in a variable: the edges along Y, left of the spine and below the top of the lip. Its concave edges get a 6 mm fillet (`saddle`), where the headband rests, and its convex edges 3 mm (`tips`), less than half the 7 mm lip so both fillets fit.\n\n## The bolt\n\n`bolt` is `j.hole.nutTrap('M6', { from: 'top', depth: 'bj' })` drilled up from the underside of the bottom jaw: a hex pocket 10.4 mm across the flats (10 mm plus the slide gap on each side) and 5.6 mm deep, then a 6.4 mm bore. `depth: 'bj'` stops it at the throat; with the default `through` it would also pierce the top jaw. Press an M6 nut into the pocket and turn an M6 × 25 bolt up against the underside of the desk; 8 mm of `travel` takes up the play.\n\n## Printing it\n\n`.orient('+Y')` lays the profile on the bed. The 69 mm height becomes the part's length on the plate, the 30 mm width its print height, and the hook bends within the layers, where PETG is strongest. The kernel sees that the bolt is now horizontal and makes it a 45° teardrop pointing up. The one overhang the check reports, 33.6 mm², is the flat top of the nut pocket: a 6 mm bridge, which prints without support. [Fit a 240 mm drawer](lab:param/W=240). [Select the side of a slot](lab:select/long-slots/side[long-slots.s.left]).\n\n## The half-lap joint\n\nWhere two dividers cross, each gives up half its height. The long divider is slotted from the top and the short ones from the bottom, each slot `h / 2 + gap` deep and `t + 2 · gap` wide: 20.2 mm by 2.65 mm for 40 mm dividers 2.25 mm thick. Together the two slots are 0.4 mm deeper than the divider is tall, so the tops sit flush with a little play. `gap` is 0.2 mm a side, the slide fit class: the dividers go together by hand and do not rattle.\n\n## How it is built\n\nEvery divider lies in the XY plane, its length along X and its height along Y. `long` extrudes the long plate `t` thick, `long-round` rounds its two top corners by 4 mm, and `long-slots` cuts a rectangle and a linear pattern of it, `nx − 1` slots at a step of `W / nx`, so the compartments are equal. `short` and `short-slots` do the same for a short divider, which starts at `y0` on the plate. The fillets come before the slots so that the corner selector, edges along Z above mid-height, cannot catch the mouth of a slot.\n\n`lead-in` chamfers the mouth of every slot by 0.6 mm: the convex edges along Z of `long-slots` and `short-slots`. Then `longs` and `shorts` pattern the finished bodies, not the features: `j.feat('long').body()` copies the plate with its slots, rounds and lead-ins, `ny − 1` long and `nx − 1` short dividers in all.\n\n## Printing it\n\nFlat on the bed, one plate: three bodies, 43.9 cm³, about 48 g of PLA and 120 min. A divider 2.25 mm thick is five line widths, stiff enough at 40 mm tall. Measure the drawer inside and set `W` and `D` to it; `clear` already takes 1 mm off each end.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [D-03 Desk tray](lab:preset/D-03): compartments cut from a solid block instead.\n- [D-09 Gridfinity baseplate](lab:preset/D-09): a grid that bins drop into.\n","script.js":"// D-08 · Drawer divider set: egg-crate dividers that slot together, laid out\n// flat as one part with a body per divider, ready for one plate.\n// Notice the half-lap joint: long dividers are slotted from the top, short\n// ones from the bottom, each to half the height, so they cross flush.\n// Every divider lies in the XY plane: its length along X, its height along Y.\nconst longs = j.sketch('long', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('plate', 'W - 1', step: 'D / ny' }))\n\n// the two top corners of a divider whose foot is at y = base: edges along Z,\n// above mid-height\nconst topCorners = (id, base) => j.feat(id).edges('|Z').where((e, ctx) =>\n  e.center.y > (base ? ctx.p[base].value : 0) + ctx.p.h.value / 2)\n\nconst set = j.part('drawer-dividers')\n  .params({\n    W: mm(200),            // inside width of the drawer (X)\n    D: mm(150),            // inside depth of the drawer (Y)\n    h: mm(40),             // divider height: a little under the drawer's\n    nx: 3, ny: 2,          // compartments across and deep\n    t: lw(5),              // divider thickness: five 0.45 mm lines, 2.25 mm\n    gap: mm(0.2),          // per side of a slot: the slide fit class\n    clear: mm(1),          // each divider is this much short at each end\n    row: 'h + 6 mm',       // spacing of the dividers on the plate\n    y0: '(ny - 1) * row',  // where the short dividers start on the plate\n    r: mm(4),              // rounding of the top corners\n    lead: mm(0.6),         // 45° lead-in at the mouth of each slot\n  })\n  .expose(['W', 'D', 'h', 'nx', 'ny'], {\n    labels: {\n      W: 'drawer width inside', D: 'drawer depth inside',\n      h: 'divider height', nx: 'compartments across',\n      ny: 'compartments deep',\n    },\n    ranges: {\n      W: [mm(60), mm(240)], D: [mm(60), mm(240)], h: [mm(15), mm(80)],\n      nx: [2, 6], ny: [2, 5],\n    },\n  })\n  // the first long divider: a plate t thick, rounded, slotted from the top\n  .extrude(longs.profiles(), 't', { id: 'long' })\n  .fillet(topCorners('long', null), 'r', { id: 'long-round' })\n  .cut(longSlots.profiles(), 't', { id: 'long-slots' })\n  // the first short divider, slotted from the bottom\n  .extrude(shorts.profiles(), 't', { id: 'short' })\n  .fillet(topCorners('short', 'y0'), 'r', { id: 'short-round' })\n  .cut(shortSlots.profiles(), 't', { id: 'short-slots' })\n  // a lead-in at every slot mouth: the convex edges along Z of the slots\n  .chamfer(j.feat('long-slots').edges('|Z').where(e => e.convex)\n    .or(j.feat('short-slots').edges('|Z').where(e => e.convex)),\n  'lead', { id: 'lead-in' })\n  // copies: ny − 1 long dividers and nx − 1 short ones, as separate bodies\n  .pattern(j.feat('long').body(), j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+Y'),\n    { count: 'ny - 1', step: 'row' }), { id: 'longs' })\n  .pattern(j.feat('short').body(), j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+Y'),\n    { count: 'nx - 1', step: 'row' }), { id: 'shorts' })\n\n// Flat on the bed: each divider is a 2.25 mm plate, quick to print and flat.\nreturn j.print('drawer-dividers', {\n  printer: j.printer('generic-fdm'), material: j.material('PLA'), parts: [set],\n}).orient('+Z').use(j.rules.fdm)\n"}},{"id":"D-09","title":"Gridfinity baseplate","group":"Desk and storage","order":9,"difficulty":"advanced","about":"a light 3 × 2 Gridfinity baseplate: one loft through four rounded squares makes the 45° socket, a grid pattern repeats it","view":"axo","overlays":"shadows,grid,dims","tries":"Add a 6.5 × 2.4 mm magnet pocket at each corner of every cell, on a thicker plate | Fill a 300 × 200 mm drawer: compute nx and ny from the drawer and centre the grid","agent":{"role":"a Gridfinity designer who keeps every size to the published grid","prompt":"Prefer the standard numbers (42 mm pitch, 7 mm units, 2.15 / 1.8 / 0.7 mm socket steps) over invented ones; keep corner radii as offsets of each other so slopes stay at 45°; never change the pitch.","fragments":{"loft":"Build stepped profiles as one ruled loft through rounded squares, inset by the step and with the radius shrunk by the same inset.","bed":"Keep a baseplate within the bed: 5 × 4 cells on a 250 × 210 mm bed."},"defaultFragments":["loft"]},"files":{"notes.md":"# Gridfinity baseplate\n\nA 3 × 2 baseplate for the Gridfinity storage system: six 42 mm sockets that bins drop into and stay put, in a drawer or on a shelf. Shrinking the radius by the inset makes each corner a true 45° cone, the offset of the one above. `socket` lofts four sections with `ruled: true`, so the faces between them are flat:\n\n| section | z | inset | across |\n|---|--:|--:|--:|\n| `s-bottom` | −0.5 mm | 3.35 mm | 35.3 mm |\n| `s-low` | 0.7 mm | 2.15 mm | 37.7 mm |\n| `s-high` | 2.5 mm | 2.15 mm | 37.7 mm |\n| `s-top` | 5.15 mm | −0.5 mm | 43 mm |\n\nThe first and last sections carry the slopes 0.5 mm past the plate (`over`), so the cut leaves no skin on either face, and next to each other the tools overlap above the plate. The question is how thin and how narrow the arm can be before it is too weak, and what that saves in plastic.\n\n> **Try it.** [Run the search](lab:exp/search.js), click the lightest feasible row to load it, then [look at it from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side).\n\n## The proxy\n\nThere is no simulation in the print suite, so strength is a proxy: the bending capacity of the arm's root, $F = \\sigma Z / L$ with $Z = b t^2 / 6$. With σ = 50 MPa along the layers, a 6 mm arm 20 mm wide holds 150 N at 40 mm.\n\n## The search\n\n`search.js` compiles fifteen points, five thicknesses by three widths, and returns their mass and capacity. Points under 100 N are not feasible; the table ranks the rest by mass.\n\n> **Exercise.** Add `arm` to the grid and see whether a shorter arm buys a thinner one. Then round the inside corner where the arm meets the plate with a fillet, and check that the search still runs.\n","script.js":"// E-01 · A wall hook: a plate against the wall, an arm, an upturned tip, as\n// three joined extrudes.\nconst back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .rect('r', 't', 'plate', { at: [0, 0] })\nconst arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .rect('r', 'arm', 't', { at: [0, 0] })\nconst tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .rect('r', 't', 12, { at: ['arm - t', 0] })\n\nreturn j.part('hook')\n  .params({ b: mm(20), t: mm(6), arm: mm(40), plate: mm(50) })\n  .expose(['b', 't', 'arm'], {\n    labels: {\n      b: 'width of the hook', t: 'thickness of the arm',\n      arm: 'reach of the arm',\n    },\n  })\n  .extrude(back.profiles(), 'b', { id: 'back' })\n  .extrude(arm.profiles(), 'b', { id: 'arm' })\n  .extrude(tip.profiles(), 'b', { id: 'tip' })\n","search.js":"// Arm thickness and width against mass\n// A 5 × 3 grid over t and b, each point a fresh compile. capacity is the\n// beam proxy of example 42, σ · Z / L with σ = 50 MPa along the layers,\n// so it needs no simulation.\nconst rows = []\nfor (const t of [4, 5, 6, 7, 8]) {\n  for (const b of [15, 20, 25]) {\n    const P = await compile(files['script.js'],\n      { override: { t: mm(t), b: mm(b) } })\n    const arm = 40                                // mm, the lever arm\n    const capacity = 50 * (b * t * t / 6) / arm   // N = MPa · mm³ / mm\n    rows.push({\n      params: { t, b },\n      metrics: {\n        mass: P.metrics.mass,\n        capacity: { value: capacity, unit: 'N' },\n      },\n      feasible: capacity >= 100,\n    })\n  }\n}\nreturn {\n  name: 'Arm thickness and width against mass',\n  objective: { minimize: 'mass', require: 'capacity ≥ 100 N' },\n One script draws all three; a config picks the standard. The idea it teaches is configs as a table of standards, with units written as the standard writes them.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the back](lab:view/stage?view=plan). Then [deepen it to hide an uneven wall box](lab:param/depth=9), [round the corners more](lab:param/r=12), or [select a countersink](lab:select/screws/csink[1]).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`body` extrudes a rounded rectangle `pw` by `ph` to `depth`, modelled front-down as it prints: z = 0 is the front. `skirt` shells it with the back face open, leaving a 2.4 mm front (`layer(12)`) and a 1.8 mm skirt round the edge that stands the plate off the wall and hides the box. `bevel` chamfers the front edge, `opening` cuts the switch's window through everything, and `screws` drills two countersunk holes at (±`sx`, ±`sy`).\n\nThe countersink is `j.hole.countersink(j.expr('screw_d'), j.expr('head_d'), j.expr('sink'))`. Its sizes are params, so they are passed as expressions: a plain string as the first argument would be read as a thread name such as `'M4'`.\n\n## Configs\n\n| config | plate | opening | screws |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| `eu86` | 86 × 86 mm | 45 × 45 mm Euro module | M3.5, 60.3 mm apart across (BS 4662) |\n| `us_decora` | 2.75 × 4.5 in | 1.31 × 2.63 in | #6-32, 3.281 in apart |\n| `us_toggle` | 2.75 × 4.5 in | 0.406 × 0.94 in | #6-32, 2.375 in apart |\n\nThe US rows are written in inches, `'2.75 in'`, and the expressions convert them: the params view shows 69.85 mm. US flat-head screws have an 82° head, metric ones 90°, so `sink` changes with the row too.\n\n## Printing it\n\nFront-down, so the face that shows is the smooth first layer. The bevel is 0.6 mm across and 1.2 mm up, steeper than 45°. The `eu86` plate is 12,473 mm³, 15 g of PLA and about 41 min; the US plates take about 44 and 55 min. The 90° countersinks of `eu86` open from the bed at exactly 45°: the check reports about 16 mm² of their facets as an overhang, an info and not a problem, since a 45° cone prints cleanly. The 82° US countersinks are steeper and pass.\n\n> **Exercise.** Add a `us_duplex` row for a US outlet: two round openings and one screw between them. 160 × 60 mm plate that hangs on two screws through keyhole slots, so it lifts off the wall without tools. The idea it teaches is a feature pattern with params for its count and step: the plate's length and the keyholes' positions are written from them, so a rack for three keys or eight is one number.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the back](lab:view/stage?view=plan) to see the keyholes. Then [add a peg](lab:param/n=6) and watch the plate grow, [tilt the pegs up more](lab:param/tilt=25), or [select a keyhole's seat](lab:select/seats/side[slot.a2]#1).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`plate` is a rounded rectangle `L` by `H`, with `L = n * step + 10 mm`. `peg` is one peg, sketched from the side on a YZ plane at the first peg's x, `-(n - 1) * step / 2`: a parallelogram that leans `tilt` (15°), so a ring hung on it slides back to the plate. `pegs` repeats it with `j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+X'), { count: 'n', step: 'step' })`: the pattern replays the extrude `n − 1` more times.\n\n`keyholes` is a region, a circle unioned with a slot: the screw head enters at the 9 mm circle, the plate drops 8 mm, and the shank rides up the 4.6 mm slot. The keyholes sit at ±`kx` = `(n - 2) * step / 2`, between the outer pairs of pegs, and above them, so they never meet a peg. `seats` cuts the same keyhole grown by 2 mm up from the bed with a −40° taper, closing to the keyhole's own size: a countersunk head sits in it and the plate lies flat on the wall.\n\n## Printing it\n\nBack on the bed. The pegs grow straight up and lean 15°; the seats' walls lean 40°; the plate's top edge is filleted and its bed edge chamfered. Nothing needs support. It is 65,181 mm³, 41 g of PLA and about 1 h 55 min. The pegs' layers run across them, which is fine for keys; for a heavy bag, print it in PETG.\n\n## Fixing it\n\nDrive two 4 mm countersunk screws into the wall `2 * kx` apart (90 mm for five pegs), leaving 2–3 mm of each head proud. Hang the rack by the circles and let it drop.\n\n> **Exercise.** Give each peg a ball on its tip: add a 10 mm sphere at the end of the first peg with `.add(j.sphere(5), { at, id: 'ball' })`, then repeat it with a second pattern that uses the same `count` and `step`.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-08 Wall hook](lab:preset/S-08) holds one heavier coat on two screws. The arc's centre is on the axis at `zc`. For the circle to pass through both (0, `H - depth`) and (`rin`, `H`), its centre must satisfy `rin² + (H - zc)² = (H - depth - zc)²`, which gives\n\n`zc = H - (depth * depth - rin * rin) / (2 * depth)`,\n\nabout 1.4 mm below the rim for a 43 mm egg. `rin` is `egg / 2 - 1.5 mm`, so the egg rests on the rim just below its widest point, and `depth` is half the egg. a bundle of cables: twelve Cat6 network cables, or the leads behind a desk, held parallel at the same spacing so the bundle stays tidy. Each cable snaps in sideways through a slit narrower than itself, so the comb can be added to a bundle that is already plugged in. The idea it teaches is drawing one closed outline from lines and an arc, then mirroring and patterning it inside the sketch.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look down on it](lab:view/stage?view=plan). Then [switch to thin USB leads](lab:param/cable=4), [add holes](lab:param/n=8), or [select the round end of the first hole](lab:select/slots/side[slots.a]).\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe outline of one hole and its slit is a polyline `u` of three lines (up the slit's right side, across beyond the edge, down its left side) and an arc `a` about the hole's centre from the left side round the bottom to the right. They share end points, so the sketch finds one closed loop, and the slit and the hole are cut together. `y0`, where the slit meets the circle, is `row + sqrt(r * r - slit * slit / 4)`, so the lines end exactly on the arc.\n\n`.mirror(['u', 'a'], 'X')` adds the lower row's copy, with its slit facing the other edge. `.pattern(['u', 'a', 'a.m'], j.pattern.linear(…, { count: 'n', step: 'pitch' }))` repeats both across the comb; `u` stands for its segments and their mirror copies, the arc's copy is named on its own. One cut, `slots`, then makes all twelve.\n\nThe hole is `cable / 2 + 0.2 mm` in radius, a loose fit; the slit is `cable * 0.75`, 4.7 mm for a 6.2 mm cable, so the cable squeezes through and stays. `ease` chamfers the top edges of every hole and slit by 0.6 mm.\n\n## Configs\n\n`cat6` (6.2 mm, six per row) is the default; `usb` makes eight 4 mm holes per row, `power` four 8 mm ones. `pitch`, the plate's size and the hole positions are expressions of `cable` and `n`, so each row fits.\n\n## Printing it\n\nFlat on the bed. The holes are vertical and the bed edges stay square, because a chamfer there would overhang. It is 8,068 mm³, 7 g of PETG and about 19 min. PETG, because the teeth flex every time a cable goes in; PLA teeth snap after a few.\n\n> **Exercise.** Make a round comb for a 24-cable bundle: a disc with the holes on two rings, the slits running outward. Use `j.pattern.circular` in place of the linear pattern.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-01 Cable clip](lab:preset/S-01) holds one cable at a desk's edge. [H-05 Key rack](lab:preset/H-05) repeats a feature with a param count.\n","script.js":"// H-08 · Cable comb: two rows of cable holes, each with a slit to the edge\n// that the cable snaps through, for dressing a bundle neatly.\n// Notice the outline of one hole: an arc and three lines drawn as a single\n// loop, so the slit and the hole are one cut, then mirrored and patterned\n// in the mirror copy is its own entity, a.m\n  .pattern(['u', 'a', 'a.m'],\n    j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+X'), { count: 'n', step: 'pitch' }))\n\nconst comb = j.part('cable-comb')\n  .params({\n    cable: mm(6.2),     // cable diameter: Cat6 is 6.0–6.5 mm\n    r: 'cable / 2 + 0.2 mm',   // the hole: a loose fit round the cable\n    slit: 'cable * 0.75',      // the cable squeezes through the slit\n The bowl is a hemisphere under a short cylinder, and its radius is not drawn: it is solved from the capacity. The idea it teaches is designing to a number you can check: compile the inside of the bowl as a solid, and the volume metric reads 30,000 mm³, 30.0 ml.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look down on it](lab:view/stage?view=plan). Then [make the bowl deeper and narrower](lab:param/k=1) and watch `r` shrink in [the params](lab:show/params) while the capacity stays the same, or [select the bowl](lab:select/cavity/side[inside.bottom]).\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe capacity is `ML`, a constant at the top, because the label is written from it too. The inside is a hemisphere of radius `r` with a cylinder `c = k * r` on top, so its volume is π r² c + ⅔ π r³ = π r³ (k + ⅔), and\n\n`r = pow(ml * 1000 / (pi * (k + 2 / 3)), 1 / 3) * 1 mm`,\n\n20.15 mm for 30 ml. `inside` is the half-section of that space: a polyline up the side and across the rim, and an arc `bottom` about (0, `floor + r`). `bowl` is a cylinder `r + wall` across, `handle` a 16 mm slot along X on the bed, and `cavity` revolves `inside` with `op: 'cut'`. `label` engraves `${ML} ml` 0.6 mm into the handle's top face, `hang` drills a 6 mm hole at its end, and `foot` chamfers the bowl's bed edge.\n\n## Checking the volume\n\nSet `SHOW` to `'capacity'` and press [Run](lab:run). higher than the one in front so every lid can be read from above. One module holds two rows of two jars; a front and a back module make a 388 mm staircase of four rows, and pairs of modules side by side fill the drawer's width. The idea it teaches is a sheet part: an open zigzag thickened by an offset, printed on its side.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the profile](lab:view/stage?view=front). Then [select the first slope](lab:select/sheet/side[sheet.top.2]) and open [the params](lab:show/params): each module lists its own copy. To size both for tall jars, set `jar` to 110 mm in the code and press [Run](lab:run); the back module follows, because its `x0` and `lift` are written from `p` and `rise`.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`top` is an open polyline written by a loop: up the front wall to `h0`, then for each row a slope falling `d` back to its riser, up the riser by `rise`, and after the last row a stop `stop` tall. `p = jar * cos(a)` is a row's depth and `d = p * tan(a)` its fall, so a row is exactly one jar long at any tilt. `.offset('under', ['top'], '-t')` adds a parallel copy 1.8 mm underneath with end caps: the sketch's one profile is now a closed sheet, `t` thick. A second sketch adds a leg under the foot of each slope, so the sheet stands on the drawer's floor.\n\n`x0` and `lift` move a module back and up. The `back` config sets them to two rows' depth and two rises, so the back module continues the staircase behind the front one. The print job adds the same part twice, `insert` and `insert.config('back')`.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOn its side (`+Y` up), both modules on one plate: every layer is the whole zigzag, so the 15° slopes, the risers and the legs all print as walls, with no overhang and no support. The pair is 116,107 mm³, 144 g of PLA and about 6 h 32 min. Most of that is the 100 mm width; `W` of 50 mm holds one jar per row in half the time.\n\n> **Exercise.** Measure your drawer. Work out how many pairs fit its width, and whether a third module (`x0` of `4 * p`, `lift` of `4 * rise`) fits its depth and its height.\n\n## Where next\n\n[D-03 Desk tray](lab:preset/D-03) divides a drawer into compartments. Two modules, front\n// and back, make four rows; both print on one plate.\n// Notice the profile: one open zigzag, thickened by an offset into a\n// sheet, and one part turned into two modules by its configs.\nconst TIERS = 2                        // rows of jars in one module\nconst front = i => `x0 + ${i} * p`     // x where row i of the module begins\n\n// the top surface, front to back: up the front wall, then each row's\n// slope falling back to its riser, and a stop behind the last row\nconst pts = [['x0', 0], ['x0', 'h0']]\nfor (let i = 0; i < TIERS; i++) {\n  const top = `h0 + ${i} * rise`          // front edge of row i\n  pts.push([front(i + 1), `${top} - d`])   // down the slope\n  pts.push([front(i + 1),                  // up the riser, or the stop\n    i < TIERS - 1 ? `${top} + rise` : `${top} - d + stop`])\n}\nconst sheet = j.sketch('sheet', { on: j.plane.XZ })   // x back, z up\n  .polyline('top', pts)\n  .offset('under', ['top'], '-t')\n\n// a leg under the foot of each slope, so the sheet stands on the drawer\nlet legs = j.sketch('legs', { on: j.plane.XZ })\nfor (let i = 1; i <= TIERS; i++) {   // a sketch is immutable: reassign it\n  legs = legs.rect(`leg${i}`, 't', `h0 + ${i - 1} * rise - d`,\n    { at: [`${front(i)} - t`, 0] })\n}\n\nconst insert = j.part('spice-insert')\n  .params({\n    jar: mm(100),      // jar length, lid included: 50 g jars are 95–110\n    a: deg(15),        // jars lie tilted 15°, lids up\n    p: 'jar * cos(a)', // depth of one row\n    d: 'p * tan(a)',   // how far each slope falls\n    rise: mm(10),      // each row stands this much above the one in front\n    x0: mm(0),         // where the module starts, front to back\n    lift: mm(0),       // and how high its first row starts\n    h0: 'lift + d + 4 mm',   // the module's front edge\n    stop: mm(12),      // the back stop's height above the last slope\n    t: lw(4),          // 1.8 mm sheet: four lines, solid\n    W: mm(100),        // module width: two 45 mm jars side by side\n  })\n  // the back module sits behind the front one and two rows higher; The external thread is added on the jar's shoulder with `bore: 'bore'`, a hollow threaded tube 10 mm long; the internal one is added inside the lid with `internal: true`, which cuts instead of adding. The idea it teaches: a press fit is a fit class, not a number you guess. Every pocket is `j.fit.hole('D', 'press')`, so a calibrated printer profile resizes all four at once.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the hub pocket](lab:select/pockets/bore[hub]) and read its size: Ø22.2 mm for a Ø22 mm bearing. The outline is solids, not one sketch: a hub disc, one lobe disc and one arm bar, then `.pattern(j.feat('lobe'), j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 3 }))` and the same for the arm. Overlapping circles in one sketch would make the overlaps holes, because a sketch's profiles alternate in and out where curves cross.\n\n`waist` rounds the twelve inside corners where arms meet discs, selected as `j.sel.edges('|Z').where(e => e.concave)`. The arm's straight sides are only 4 mm long between the discs, so the largest rounding that fits is about 4.1 mm; ask for more and the compile says so (`fillet.tooLarge`) with the number.\n\nThe pockets are one hole feature at four sketch points, `hub`, `l1`, `l2` and `l3`, 120° apart on radius `R`. The default profile makes a press hole 22 mm + 2 × 0.05 mm + 0.1 mm compensation = 22.2 mm. A 0.6 mm chamfer on every top and bottom edge gives the bearings a lead-in and takes off the first layer's elephant's foot.\n\n## The caps\n\nEach cap is a Ø20 mm pad, a Ø10.5 mm spacer 0.8 mm high that bears on the bearing's inner race only, and a Ø7.9 mm stub that presses 3 mm into the 8 mm bore. Two caps from opposite sides leave 1 mm between their stubs. The stub size is written as a number, 8 mm − 2 × 0.05 mm: `j.fit.pin` gives a diameter, and a sketch circle takes a radius.\n\n## Printing it\n\nAll flat, no supports: about 10 g of PETG and 27 min for the body and both caps. finger cap for each side of the hub bearing: a pad, a spacer that bears\n// on the inner race only (so the pad clears the spinning body), and a stub\n// pressed into the 8 mm bore. Neither is as strong as steel, but both are exact enough to hold a jig together, replace a lost knob bolt or show how a thread works. The idea it teaches: a thread is a library feature with a fit class. The same `j.lib.thread('M10')` grows into the bolt and cuts into the nut, each side moved by its clearance.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the underside of the head](lab:select/head/end), the face the thread grows from. [Make the bolt 30 mm long](lab:param/L=30) and watch the compile time: a thread costs about a second for every few turns. [Open the trace](lab:show/trace) to see where the time goes.\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe sizes are ISO 4017 and ISO 4032 for M10 coarse: pitch 1.5 mm, 16 mm across flats, head 6.4 mm, nut 8.4 mm. A hexagon is a sketch `polygon` with 6 sides on a circle of `s / sqrt(3)`, the radius that puts the flats 16 mm apart.\n\nThe bolt is `head`, a 0.8 mm `crown` chamfer round the face that lies on the bed, then the thread placed with `at: j.frame.on(j.feat('head').endFace())`: a frame on the head's top face, so the thread starts there and runs `L` up. `tip` turns a small triangle round the axis and cuts a 45° lead-in at the end, so the first turn starts cleanly in the nut.\n\nThe nut is `body` with both faces chamfered, then the same thread with `internal: true`, placed on the top face with `{ flip: true }` so that it cuts down into the nut. The internal tool runs 0.5 mm past each face, so the thread opens cleanly at both ends.\n\n## The fit\n\n`j.lib.thread` takes `fit = 'slide'` by default. The bolt's crests are modelled at Ø9.6 mm (10 mm − 2 × 0.2 mm) and the nut's tool at Ø10.4 mm, so the flanks have 0.4 mm between them in all. That is loose by machine-shop standards and about right for a printed pair that has to turn by hand. With a calibrated profile ([C-01](lab:preset/C-01)) the gap follows your printer; until then the compile reports `fit.uncalibrated` as info.\n\n## Printing it\n\nBoth parts with +Z up, the bolt on its head, in PETG: about 5 g and 22 min. A standing thread prints its flanks as stacked rings, which is why the bolt stands; laid down, the thread would need supports and come out oval. The flanks overhang 60° from vertical, and the job reports 423.1 mm² on the bolt and 158.3 mm² in the nut as info: at a 1.5 mm pitch each overhang is under a millimetre wide and prints without support.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [M-06 Threaded jar](lab:preset/M-06): a two-start thread you can open in one turn.\n- [C-05 Hole sizes](lab:preset/C-05): how far a printed hole shrinks.\n","script.js":"// M-02 · M10 bolt and nut: a fully threaded M10 × 25 hex bolt and its nut.\n// Notice that both threads come from j.lib.thread('M10'): the same spec\n// grows into the bolt and cuts into the nut, each side by its fit gap.\n\n// ISO 4017 / ISO 4032, M10 coarse: pitch 1.5 mm, 16 mm across flats, head\n// 6.4 mm, nut 8.4 mm.\nconst hex = (id, at) => j.sketch(id, { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polygon('hex', 6, 's / sqrt(3)', { at })\n\nconst bolt = j.part('bolt')\n  .params({\n    s: mm(16),        // across flats\n    k: mm(6.4),       // head height\n    L: mm(25),        // under the head, all threaded (20 mm fails to fuse here)\n    tip: mm(1),       // 45° lead-in at the end of the thread\n  })\n  .expose(['L'], { labels: { L: 'length under the head' },\n    ranges: { L: [mm(10), mm(40)] } })\n  .extrude(hex('head', [0, 0]).profiles(), 'k', { id: 'head' })\n  // a 0.8 mm chamfer round the head's top face, which lies on the bed:\n  // no elephant's foot, and the crown a real bolt has\n  .chamfer(j.feat('head').startFace().edges(), mm(0.8), { id: 'crown' })\n  // the thread grows from the head's underside, which faces +Z here\n  .add(j.lib.thread('M10', { length: 'L' }),\n    { at: j.frame.on(j.feat('head').endFace()), id: 'thread' })\n  // the lead-in: a 45° triangle turned round the axis, from 1 mm inside the\n  // crests at the end out past them\n  .revolve(j.sketch('lead', { on: j.plane.XZ }).polyline('cut', [\n    ['5 mm - tip', 'k + L'], ['6 mm', 'k + L'], ['6 mm', 'k + L - 1 mm - tip'],\n  ], { closed: true }).profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { op: 'cut', id: 'tip' })\n\n// The nut stands 30 mm to the right of the bolt on the stage; the job packs\n// the plate anyway.\nconst nut = j.part('nut')\n  .params({ s: mm(16), m: mm(8.4), x: mm(30) })\n  .extrude(hex('body', ['x', 0]).profiles(), 'm', { id: 'body' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('body').startFace().edges(), mm(0.8), { id: 'under' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('body').endFace().edges(), mm(0.8), { id: 'over' })\n  // cut down from the top face's centre: the frame flipped to point into\n  // the nut\n  .add(j.lib.thread('M10', { length: 'm', internal: true }),\n    { at: j.frame.on(j.feat('body').endFace(), { flip: true }), id: 'thread' })\n\n// Bolt on its head, thread up: the flanks print as round as they can. Every module is the same part, so you print as many as you like and slide them together into a row: pens in one, clips in the next. The idea it teaches: tail and socket are one library feature. `j.lib.dovetail` adds the tail as modelled and cuts the socket wider by the fit gap, so the two always match.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan): the tail on the right, the socket on the left. The end walls are thick because the socket goes 5.2 mm into one of them.\n\nBoth dovetails are placed with `j.frame({ origin, x, z })`. Its z is the face's outward normal (+X for the tail, −X for the socket) and its x is chosen so that its y points up, the direction the dovetail runs in. That makes each dovetail a prism standing on the bed: no overhangs, and the modules slide together vertically.\n\n```js\n.add(dovetail, { at: tailAt, op: 'join', id: 'tail' })\n.add(dovetail, { at: socketAt, op: 'cut', id: 'socket' })\n```\n\nThe tail is 14 mm wide at the root, 5 mm deep and widens at 15°. Move the second one 0.05 mm away and the gap is 0.050 mm: the tail sits inside the socket without touching it. A 0.4 mm chamfer round every bed edge keeps elephant's foot from jamming the joint and gives the tail a lead-in.\n\n## Printing it\n\nThe job prints two modules, upright and without supports: about 76 g of PLA and 200 min. Two hooks hang from the lid, bend as it goes on, and spring out into windows in the side walls. The idea it teaches: a snap fit is a beam, and a beam has a strain limit. `j.lib.snap.cantilever` computes the strain as it compiles and stops the compile with `print.snapStrain` if the beam would crack.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the ramp of the right-hand hook](lab:select/hookR/ramp), the face that pushes the beam aside as the lid goes on, then [its flat retaining face](lab:select/hookR/retention). Bending the tip 1.0 mm strains the root by ε = 1.5 · t · y / L² = 1.5 × 1.4 × 1.0 / 144 = 1.46 %. PETG takes 2.5 %, so the hook works at 58 % of its limit and survives being opened again and again. Each window runs from below the beam's tip to 0.2 mm above the barb's retaining face, so the closed lid can lift 0.2 mm and no more. Two trays print side by side, joined along their back rims by a 0.4 mm strip of polypropylene that folds through 180°. A lip on the lid slips into the base and holds it shut. The idea it teaches: where a hinge sits decides how a part folds. A strip on the bed would fold the trays back to back; a strip at rim height folds them rim to rim.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the underside of the hinge](lab:select/hinge/start), the strip the printer bridges across the 1 mm gap. [Look at it from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side) and imagine the lid turning about the hinge's top edge. Then [loosen the lip to 0.4 mm](lab:param/slip=0.4) if your PP grips too hard.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`tray` is a small function that adds one rounded 50 × 35 × 12 mm block and cuts its pocket; the script calls it twice, `base` at y > 0 and `lid` at y < 0, 1 mm apart. The hinge is `j.lib.hinge.living({ length, thickness: mm(0.4), gap: mm(1), material: 'PP' })`, placed with `j.tx.move(0, 0, h - t)` so that its top is flush with both rims. It is 38 mm long, the straight part of the back walls, and overlaps each rim by 2 mm, the walls' thickness; with it the two trays become one body.\n\nThe lid's walls are thicker than the base's by the lip and its clearance, so the lip (`lw(3)` thick, 2 mm high) stands on the lid's rim and still fits inside the base with `slip` = 0.25 mm all round. The lip stops 6 mm short of the hinge side so that nothing near the fold has to swing past the base's back wall. A tab on the lid's front edge is for your thumb.\n\n## Why PP, and why bridged\n\nA living hinge bends a thin strip far past what most plastics take: the lib warns (`print.layerStrength`) if the material's allowable strain is under 5 %, which rules out PLA and PETG. PP takes 10 % and lasts thousands of folds. The strip prints as a bridge across the gap, so its lines run across the fold: the direction a hinge bends without splitting between lines. The job reports its 38.0 mm² underside as overhang info; that is the bridge.\n\nFold it once while it is still warm from the bed, fully, to set the hinge.\n\n## Printing it\n\nFlat, open sides up: about 12.5 g of PP and 48 min. PP does not stick to most beds: print on PP tape or a PP sheet, with a brim, and keep the fan low.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [M-08 Print-in-place hinged box](lab:preset/M-08): the same box with a pin hinge, in any material.\n- [M-04 Snap-fit battery cover](lab:preset/M-04): another joint that bends to work.\n","script.js":"// M-05 · Living-hinge box: a one-piece clamshell for small parts. Turn the pinion twice and the wheel turns once. The idea it teaches: the centre distance of a gear pair is not a dimension you choose. It follows from the module and the tooth counts, a = m (z₁ + z₂) / 2, and here `j.inspect.gearMesh` computes it and the base is built from its answer.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan) to see the teeth in mesh. [Turn the wheel back to 0°](lab:param/wheel=0): a wheel tooth now points straight at a pinion tooth, and the interference check fails. [Set it to 5°](lab:param/wheel=5), half a tooth, and the gap is back.\n\n## How it is built\n\nBoth gears are `j.lib.gear.spur({ module: mm(2), teeth, width: mm(8) })`: exact involute flanks generated by the ISO 53 rack, a trochoidal root fillet and 1.25 m of dedendum. Each gets a bore for its peg with `j.fit.hole(peg, 'free')`, Ø5.7 mm on a Ø5 mm peg.\n\n`j.inspect.gearMesh(pinion, wheel)` checks that the modules and pressure angles match and reports the centre distance, 54 mm, the contact ratio, 1.61 (more than one tooth pair always in contact), the ratio, 2:1, and the backlash, 0.4 mm. If the pair did not mesh the script stops with the reasons. The base is a slot-shaped bar from axle to axle with a 0.6 mm spacer and a 14 mm peg at each end, all placed at `a`.\n\n## The assembly\n\nThe script returns an assembly. The base is grounded; each gear's `axis` port sits on a port of the base by a revolute joint, so the assembly has 2 degrees of freedom (a gear mesh is not a joint here, so the two turn independently in the model). The wheel's joint starts at 180°/36 = 5°: 36 is even, so without that half-tooth turn a wheel tooth would point straight at a pinion tooth.\n\n`.interference()` measures the smallest gap between every two parts: 0.200 mm between pinion and wheel at this pose, half the backlash on each side, and 0.200 mm between each gear and the base, because the axle ports sit 0.2 mm above the spacers.\n\n## Printing it\n\nAll three parts flat, gears on a face, so every tooth is a vertical prism: about 28 g of PLA and 75 min. The print job is in the script; set `printJob` to `true` to compile it. It is not the default because the job's slicing pass takes about a minute on involute flanks in this engine, against a few seconds for the assembly.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [M-09 Rack and pinion](lab:preset/M-09): a gear of infinite radius.\n- [M-10 Planetary gearbox](lab:preset/M-10): an internal gear and the tooth-count rules that make three planets fit.\n","script.js":"// M-07 · Spur gear pair: an 18-tooth pinion driving a 36-tooth wheel on a\n// base whose two pegs stand exactly one centre distance apart. Notice that\n// the distance is not typed in: j.inspect.gearMesh computes it from the two\n// gears, and the base is built from that number.\nconst modul = mm(2), width = mm(8)\nconst peg = mm(5)                        // printed axles\nconst bore = j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole(peg, 'free'))\nconst pinion = j.lib.gear.spur({ module: modul, teeth: 18, width })\n  .hole([[0, 0]], bore, { id: 'bore' })\nconst wheel = j.lib.gear.spur({ module: modul, teeth: 36, width })\n  .hole([[0, 0]], bore, { id: 'bore' })\n\n// The check: same module and pressure angle, contact ratio over 1.1, no\n// undercut (18 teeth is above the 17 where 20° teeth start to undercut).\nconst mesh = j.inspect.gearMesh(pinion, wheel)\nif (!mesh.ok) throw new Error(`gears do not mesh: ${mesh.messages.join('; ')}`)\nconst a = mesh.centreDistance.value     // m (z1 + z2) / 2 = 54 mm\n\n// The base: a bar with round ends from axle to axle, a 0.6 mm spacer round\n// each peg so the gears clear the bar, the pegs, and a port on each axle\n// 0.2 mm above its spacer, where a gear's `axis` port goes.\nconst axles = (id, r) => j.sketch(id, { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 4) })\n  .circle('p1', r).circle('p2', r, { at: [a, 0] })\nconst axle = x => j.frame({ origin: j.pt(x, 0, 4.8), x: j.dir('+X'),\n  z: j.dir('+Z') })\nconst base = j.part('base')\n  .params({ bar: mm(16), plate: mm(4), pegH: mm(14) })\n  .extrude(j.region.slot([0, 0], [a, 0], 16), 'plate', { id: 'bar' })\n  .extrude(axles('spacers', mm(5)).profiles(), mm(0.6), { id: 'spacers' })\n  .extrude(axles('pegs', peg.value / 2).profiles(), 'pegH', { id: 'pegs' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('pegs').endFace().edges(), mm(0.5), { id: 'tips' })\n  .port('axle1', axle(0)).port('axle2', axle(a))\n\n// The pair, assembled: each gear on a revolute joint. The idea it teaches: a print-in-place mechanism is an assembly checked in the pose it prints in. The script builds the two halves as parts, joins them with a revolute joint and asks `.printInPlace()` for every gap between them.\n\n> **Try it.** [Swing the lid to 120°](lab:param/hinge=120), then [almost close it](lab:param/hinge=170). [Open the trace](lab:show/trace) and find the `gap` decision: 0.400 mm between base and lid as printed. [Select a knuckle of the base](lab:select/hinge/knuckle1).\n\n## The hinge\n\n`j.lib.hinge.pin({ knuckles: 5, length: mm(50), d: mm(3), clearance: 'printInPlace', wall: mm(1.6) })` makes the knuckles along a frame on the hinge axis. The base takes it with `leaf: 'a'`: knuckles 1, 3 and 5 and a Ø3 mm pin through all five. The lid takes `leaf: 'b'`: knuckles 2 and 4, bored to 3 + 2 × 0.4 = 3.8 mm. The knuckles are 7 mm across and 9.68 mm long, with 0.4 mm between them. The clearance is the `printInPlace` fit class, so a profile calibrated with [C-04](lab:preset/C-04) changes it everywhere.\n\nThe axis sits at rim height, between the trays. Folding 180° about it brings rim onto rim; an axis on the bed would fold the halves back to back.\n\n## The webs\n\nA horizontal knuckle's underside is an overhang. Under every knuckle a web stands on the bed and rises to 2.5 mm below the axis, where the knuckle's surface turns steeper than 45° (R · cos 45° = 2.47 mm), so nothing overhangs and the webs stay clear of the pin and the bores. Each web runs from its own half's back wall to R past the axis. The back walls stand R + c = 3.9 mm from the axis, clear of the other half's knuckles by the same 0.4 mm.\n\n## The assembly\n\nThe base is grounded and the lid hangs on a revolute joint between two ports on the hinge axis, limited to 0°–180°. The port's z points along −X so that a positive angle swings the lid up and over. `.printInPlace(['base', 'lid'], { clearance: mm(c), pose: { hinge: deg(0) } })` measures the smallest gap in the print pose: 0.400 mm. The engine has no swept-volume check yet, so the swing was checked by compiling the joint at 60°, 120° and 170°: 0.400 mm each time. The check runs in the pose on the stage, so at 180° it reports an error: closed, the rims touch, as they should.\n\n## Printing it\n\nA print job takes an assembly's parts and packs them apart, which would separate the halves, so the job builds both halves as one part with two bodies, using `leaf: 'both'`. It is the mechanism of a steering box, a drill press feed or a sliding gate, small enough to print in an afternoon. The idea it teaches: a rack is a gear of infinite radius, so the rules of a gear pair still hold, and the assembly's joints make the coupling visible.\n\n> **Try it.** [Turn the pinion 5°](lab:param/turn=5), a quarter of a tooth: the interference check fails, tooth on tooth. Now [move the rack 1.5708 mm](lab:param/travel=1.5708), r × θ = 18 mm × 0.0873, and the gap is back. [Select the crank](lab:select/crank/side[circle]).\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe pinion is `j.lib.gear.spur({ module: mm(2), teeth: 18, width: mm(8) })` with a Ø16 mm hub and a Ø6 mm crank peg 12 mm off the axis, bored `j.fit.hole(peg, 'free')` for its Ø5 mm axle. The rack is `j.lib.gear.rack({ module: mm(2), teeth: 16, width: mm(8) })`: straight-sided teeth at 20°, a pitch of π × 2 = 6.283 mm, 100.5 mm long, its pitch line on y = 0.\n\n`j.inspect.gearMesh(pinion, rack)` reports the pitch-line distance, 18 mm, the pinion's pitch radius, and the travel per turn, π × m × z = 113.1 mm. The base puts the pinion's peg 18 mm from the rack's pitch line over the middle of the rack, at 7.5 pitches: a pinion with 18 teeth has a gap pointing straight down, so a rack tooth must sit right under its axis.\n\n## The assembly\n\nThe rack is on a slider joint along X and the pinion on a revolute joint at its axle, so the assembly has 2 degrees of freedom; a gear mesh is not a joint here, so the two are coupled only by you. `.interference()` measures every gap: 0.200 mm between rack and pinion, half the 0.4 mm backlash on each flank, and 0.200 mm between each moving part and the base, because the ports sit 0.2 mm above the plate. Turn the pinion by θ without moving the rack by 18 mm × θ and the teeth collide; that is the coupling, and the check finds it.\n\n## Rounded roots\n\nA sharp inside corner where the peg meets the plate gathers the stress, and that corner is where a peg snaps when the pinion leans on it. `.fillet(j.feat('peg').edges().where(e => e.concave), mm(1), { id: 'peg-root' })` rounds only that edge: the peg's edges include the one where it joins the plate, and `concave` keeps the inside corner while dropping the chamfered tip. The new face is named `peg-root/face[peg/side[circle] ∩ plate/end]` after the two faces it blends, so it survives edits to either. [Find it in the graph](lab:node/peg-root).\n\nA fillet adds material, and this one climbs 1 mm up the peg, right where the pinion's bore sits. So the bore gets a 1.2 mm lead-in chamfer on its lower edge (`lead-in`). Delete that chamfer and `.interference()` reports base and pinion overlapping. The hub is rounded into the gear the same way, at 0.8 mm. At 1 mm the fillet's rim would just touch the root of the crank, and the engine refuses the fillet and names the crank's faces.\n\n## Printing it\n\nAll three flat: about 30 g of PLA and 81 min. The rack rides on the plate against the 6 mm rail; the pinion keeps it there. The print job is in the script; set `printJob` to `true` to compile it (slower, because slicing involute flanks is slow in this engine).\n\n## Where next\n\n- [M-07 Spur gear pair](lab:preset/M-07): two gears of finite radius.\n- [M-10 Planetary gearbox](lab:preset/M-10): an internal gear, the inside-out case.\n","script.js":"// M-09 · Rack and pinion: an 18-tooth pinion with a crank drives a 100 mm\n// rack along a rail. Notice that the rack is a gear of infinite radius: the\n// same module and pressure angle, and j.inspect.gearMesh gives the distance\n// from the pinion's axis to the rack's pitch line and the travel per turn.\nconst modul = mm(2), width = mm(8)\nconst peg = mm(5)\nconst pinion = j.lib.gear.spur({ module: modul, teeth: 18, width })\n  // a hub and a crank peg on top, to turn it by\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(8).on(j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 8)), mm(5),\n    { id: 'hub' })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(3, { at: [12, 0] })\n    .on(j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 8)), mm(14), { id: 'crank' })\n  .hole([[0, 0]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole(peg, 'free')), { id: 'bore' })\n  // round the hub into the gear; a lead-in on the bore's lower edge clears\n  // the fillet at the root of the base's peg\n  .fillet(j.feat('hub').edges().where(e => e.concave), mm(0.8),\n    { id: 'hub-root' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('bore').edges().where(e => e.center.z < 0.1), mm(1.2),\n    { id: 'lead-in' })\nconst rack = j.lib.gear.rack({ module: modul, teeth: 16, width })\n  .port('slide', j.frame({ origin: j.pt(0, 0, 0), x: j.dir('+Y'),\n    z: j.dir('+X') }))\n\nconst mesh = j.inspect.gearMesh(pinion, rack)\nif (!mesh.ok) throw new Error(`no mesh: ${mesh.messages.join('; ')}`)\nconst yc = mesh.pitchLineDistance.value     // m · z / 2 = 18 mm\nconst p = Math.PI * 2                       // circular pitch, π · m\nconst L = 16 * p                            // rack length, 100.5 mm\n\n// The base: a plate, a rail behind the rack, and a peg for the pinion over\n// the middle of the rack.
