Four turns of the knob turn the carrier once. The idea it teaches: tooth counts in a planetary set are not free. The ring must have z_sun + 2 z_planet teeth, and the planets fit evenly only when (z_sun + z_ring) / n is a whole number. The script checks both before it builds anything.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the knob's 45° underside](lab:select/knob/side[knob.half.2]), shaped so the sun prints standing on its teeth. [Turn the first planet back to 0°](lab:param/planet1=0) and [look from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan): its teeth now sit on the sun's. [Open the graph](lab:show/graph) to see seven parts and five joints.\n\n## The numbers\n\nWith z_sun = 18 and z_planet = 18 the ring needs 18 + 2 × 18 = 54 teeth, and (18 + 54) / 3 = 24 is whole, so three planets fit 120° apart. With the ring held, sun in and carrier out, the ratio is 1 + z_ring / z_sun = 4. Give the sun 16 teeth and the script stops: (16 + 52) / 3 is not whole. With 15 teeth three planets fit again, (15 + 51) / 3 = 22, but `j.inspect.gearMesh` adds a message: \"15 teeth (shift 0) are undercut; shift: 0.12 avoids it\".\n\nBoth meshes are checked with `j.inspect.gearMesh`: sun to planet, m (z_s + z_p) / 2 = 27 mm, and planet to ring, m (z_r − z_p) / 2 = 27 mm. The script compares the two and builds the carrier with its pins on a 27 mm circle.\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe ring is `j.lib.gear.internal({ module: mm(1.5), teeth: 54, width: mm(8.4), rim: mm(4) })`, printed as the library makes it: any boolean on a 54-tooth ring costs several seconds in this engine, so instead of joining a floor to it, the base has a 4 mm recess the ring presses into, `j.fit.hole(mm(D), 'snug')` for its Ø92.75 mm outside. The base also carries the sun's Ø5 mm axle.\n\nThe sun has a Ø10 mm hub up through the carrier and a knob turned from a polyline whose underside is a 45° cone. The carrier is a hub and three arms (one `slot` patterned three times) with Ø5 mm pins for the planets, which are bored `j.fit.hole(pin, 'free')`. A notch in one arm shows the output turning.\n\n## The assembly\n\nThe base is grounded and the ring placed on it; sun and carrier turn on revolute joints at the base's ports, and each planet turns on a joint at a port of the carrier: 5 degrees of freedom, because gear meshes are not joints here. The sun has a tooth pointing at every planet, so each planet's joint starts at 10°, half a tooth, and the ring is turned 180° / 54 to show each planet a tooth.\n\n`.interference()` confirms the meshes: 0.200 mm between sun and each planet, 0.180 mm between each planet and the ring, 0.350 mm round the pins, and 0 mm between ring and base, where the ring sits in its recess. It is not in the script because it takes about two minutes on 21 pairs of parts.\n\n## Printing it\n\nSeven parts, all flat: about 54 g of PETG and 2 h 20 min. The carrier prints arms down with `orient: '-Z'`, and the sun stands on its teeth under its 45° knob. The print job is in the script: set `printJob` to `true` to compile it; it takes about a minute, because slicing involute flanks is slow in this engine. Press the ring into the base, drop the planets on the carrier's pins, set the sun on its axle and lower the carrier over it.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [M-07 Spur gear pair](lab:preset/M-07): one external mesh and its centre distance.\n- [M-09 Rack and pinion](lab:preset/M-09): the straight case.\n","script.js":"// M-10 · Planetary gearbox: a sun, three planets and a ring, 4:1 from the\n// sun's knob to the carrier. Notice the tooth counts: a ring must have\n// z_sun + 2 z_planet teeth, and three planets fit evenly only when\n// (z_sun + z_ring) / 3 is a whole number. The script checks both.\nconst zs = 18, zp = 18, n = 3\nconst zr = zs + 2 * zp                       // 54\nif ((zs + zr) % n) throw new Error(`${n} planets do not fit: ` +\n  `(${zs} + ${zr}) / ${n} is not a whole number`)\n// Ring fixed, sun in, carrier out: 1 + zr / zs = 4 turns of the sun for one\n// of the carrier.\n\nconst modul = mm(1.5), w = 8\nconst pin = mm(5)\nconst bore = j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole(pin, 'free'))\nconst sunGear = j.lib.gear.spur({ module: modul, teeth: zs, width: mm(w) })\nconst planet = j.lib.gear.spur({ module: modul, teeth: zp, width: mm(w) })\n  .hole([[0, 0]], bore, { id: 'bore' })\n// The ring is printed as it comes from the library and pressed into the\n// base: any boolean on a 54-tooth ring costs seconds in this engine.\nconst ring = j.lib.gear.internal({ module: modul, teeth: zr,\n  width: mm(w + 0.4), rim: mm(4) })\n\n// Sun to planet and planet to ring must give the same centre distance:\n// m (zs + zp) / 2 = m (zr − zp) / 2 = 27 mm.\nconst inner = j.inspect.gearMesh(sunGear, planet)\nconst outer = j.inspect.gearMesh(planet, ring)\nif (!inner.ok || !outer.ok) throw new Error([...inner.messages,\n  ...outer.messages].join('; '))\nconst a = inner.centreDistance.value\nif (Math.abs(outer.centreDistance.value - a) > 1e-6)\n  throw new Error('sun–planet and planet–ring centre distances differ')\n\n// Heights: the ring stands on z = 0, the gears from 0.2 to 8.2, the\n// carrier plate from 8.6 to 11.6, the sun's knob from 12.\nconst zc = w + 0.6, D = 2 * ring.meta.sets.gear.outerRadius   // Ø92.75\nconst up = j.dir('+Z'), east = j.dir('+X')\n\n// The base: a 7 mm disc with a 4 mm recess the ring presses into (a snug\n// fit), and an axle for the sun.\nconst base = j.part('base')\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(D / 2 + 3).on(j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, -3)),\n    mm(7), { id: 'disc' })\n  .hole([[0, 0]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole(mm(D), 'snug'), mm(4)),\n    { id: 'recess' })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(pin.value / 2), mm(w - 0.4), { id: 'axle' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('disc').startFace().edges(), mm(0.6), { id: 'foot' })\n  .port('sun', j.frame({ origin: j.pt(0, 0, 0.2), x: east, z: up }))\n  .port('carrier', j.frame({ origin: j.pt(0, 0, zc), x: east, z: up }))\n\n// The sun: a hub up through the carrier, a knob whose underside is a 45°\n// cone (it prints standing on the gear), and a blind bore for the axle.\nconst zk = zc + 3.4\nconst sun = sunGear\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(5).on(j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, w)),\n    mm(zk - w), { id: 'hub' })\n  .revolve(j.sketch('knob', { on: j.plane.XZ }).polyline('half', [\n    [0, zk], [5, zk], [9, zk + 4], [9, zk + 8], [0, zk + 8],\n  ], { closed: true }).profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'knob' })\n  .hole([j.pt(0, 0, 0)], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole(pin, 'free'), mm(w - 0.4)),\n    { id: 'bore' })\n\n// The carrier: a hub and three arms carrying the planet pins (you can see\n// the planets between the arms), a hole for the sun's hub and a notch in\n// one arm to watch the output turn. It is one sketch and one extrude: the side view is drawn as a closed outline of eighteen points, and the extrude gives it its width. The idea it teaches is that a profile written in parameters is a whole family of parts.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front) to see the outline as drawn. Then [fit it to a 25 mm desk](lab:param/desk=25), [widen the channel to 12 mm](lab:param/cable=12), and [select the face that sits against the desk edge](lab:select/body/side[side.outline.14]).\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe sketch `side` lies on the XZ plane, so x runs into the desk and z points up. The desk fills x ≥ 0 between z = 0 and z = `desk`; the spine sits behind it at x = −`t`. Every corner of `outline` is an expression of the params, for example `['cable + t', '-2 * t - cable']` for the outside corner of the hook, so changing `desk` moves the top jaw and nothing else. With no constraints and no dimensions the sketch is explicit: the coordinates are fixed, the DOF is 0.\n\n`body` extrudes the outline by `w`, 12 mm. Each face is named after the segment it came from: `body/side[side.outline.13]` is the top of the bottom jaw, `body/side[side.outline.15]` the underside of the top jaw.\n\n## The grip\n\nA clip that fits the desk exactly falls off; one that is too tight cracks. The jaw tips carry two small bumps that pinch the board by `grip`, 0.6 mm each side, so the jaws flex a little and hold. The sloped faces in front of the bumps are lead-ins: they spread the jaws as you push the clip on. PETG is the material because it bends further than PLA before it breaks.\n\n## Printing it\n\nThe print job lays the clip on its side (`-Y` up, chosen automatically): the outline is the first layer, so there are no overhangs and the layers run along the jaws, which is the strong direction for a part that flexes. It is 2,885 mm³, 3.7 g of PETG and about 12 min.\n\n> **Exercise.** Make a double clip: change `cable` to 14 mm and `w` to 20 mm, then check in the header that the print time stays under 30 min.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-02 Door wedge](lab:preset/S-02) is another single extrude, with a toothed underside. [H-08 Cable comb](lab:preset/H-08) keeps a whole bundle in order.\n","script.js":"// S-01 · Cable clip for a desk edge: one sketch, one extrude.\n// Notice how every corner of the outline is an expression of five params,\n// so one change to `desk` moves the jaws.\n// The side view is drawn on XZ: x runs into the desk, z up;\n// the spine sits at x = −t, the desk fills x ≥ 0, 0 ≤ z ≤ desk.\nconst side = j.sketch('side', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .polyline('outline', [\n    ['jaw', 'desk + t'],                 // top jaw, outer tip\n    ['-t', 'desk + t'],                  // over the spine\n    ['-t', '-2 * t - cable'],            // down the back\n                                         // to the bottom of the hook\n    ['cable + t', '-2 * t - cable'],     // under the cable channel\n    ['cable + t', '-t - cable / 2'],     // up the lip\n    ['cable', '-t - cable / 2'],\n    ['cable', '-t - cable'],             // inside the channel\n    [0, '-t - cable'],\n    [0, '-t'],                           // under the bottom jaw\n    ['jaw', '-t'],\n    ['jaw', '-1 mm'],                    // bottom jaw tip:\n                                         // a lead-in up to the grip bump\n    ['jaw - 2 mm', 'grip'],\n    ['jaw - 4 mm', 0],\n    [0, 0],                              // the desk's underside\n    [0, 'desk'],                         // the desk's edge\n    ['jaw - 4 mm', 'desk'],\n    ['jaw - 2 mm', 'desk - grip'],       // top jaw grip bump\n    ['jaw', 'desk + 1 mm'],\n  ], { closed: true })\n\nconst clip = j.part('cable-clip')\n  .params({\n    desk: mm(18),     // board thickness: 18 mm is the common chipboard\n                      // and MDF desktop; The underside carries fifteen V-grooves; the ribs between them catch on carpet, tiles and mats, where a flat wedge skates. Like S-01 it is one sketch and one extrude, but the outline is written by a loop: the script is JavaScript, and a `for` loop is the shortest way to say \"fifteen grooves\".\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front) to see the grooves. Then [make it taller for a wide gap](lab:param/H=40), [deepen the grooves](lab:param/rib=2.5) and [select the slope the door rides up](lab:select/body/side[side.outline.2]).\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe outline starts at the tip, climbs the slope to `H` at the back, comes down and runs back along the floor. On the way back the loop adds three points per groove, `[x + rib, 0]`, `[x, rib]`, `[x − rib, 0]`: a 90° V `rib` deep. Each `x` is an expression, `margin + i * (L − 2 * margin) / 14`, so changing `L` spreads the grooves out instead of leaving a gap. The count, 15, is a constant in the script because it shapes the outline itself; change it in the code and press [Run](lab:run).\n\nThe circle `hang` sits inside the outline, so it becomes a hole in the profile: one extrude makes the wedge and its 8 mm hanging hole together.\n\n## Printing it\n\nThe job lays the wedge on its side (`-Y` up): the outline is the first layer, so the grooves and the hole print without support and the slope is smooth. PLA is enough; a wedge printed in TPU grips even better and needs a slower print.\n\n> **Exercise.** Make a low wedge for a 6 mm gap: set `H` to 15 mm and `tip` to 1.2 mm, then look at the front view and decide whether `margin` should shrink too.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-01 Cable clip](lab:preset/S-01) is the same build with a profile that flexes. The pockets catch the drip that runs down a mug, and the solid rim keeps it off the table. The idea it teaches is a pattern in a sketch: one hexagon, copied onto a hexagonal lattice and filtered to the cells that fit inside the rim.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look down on it](lab:view/stage?view=plan). Then [make the cells smaller](lab:param/cell=6) and watch the count grow in the trace, [widen the webs](lab:param/web=2), or [make it a 110 mm pot stand](lab:param/d=110).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`body` is a disc `d` across and `t`, 4 mm, thick. The sketch `cells` sits on its top face, `j.feat('body').endFace()`, and holds one closed polyline: a hexagon whose corners are `cell` from its centre. `.pattern(['cell'], …)` copies it with `j.pattern.hex`, pitch `cell * sqrt(3) + web`: two flat sides apart plus one web. The lattice frame is turned 30° so that neighbours sit across flat sides, which is what makes every web the same width.\n\nThe filter keeps a copy only when its centre's distance plus `cell` stays inside `d / 2 − rim`, so the rim is never thinner than `rim`. `pockets` then cuts all the cells 1.6 mm deep in one feature; the compiler records that it cut into the material because everything lies below the sketch plane. Each pocket floor is a face of its own, `pockets/start#a` and so on; [select one](lab:select/pockets/start#a).\n\n## Printing it\n\nFlat on the bed, top up: the pockets are open at the top, so nothing overhangs. It is 20,302 mm³, about 18 g of PLA at 15 % infill and about 46 min.\n\n> **Exercise.** Change the polyline to a circle of radius `cell` and compare: circles leave triangular lands between them that hexagons do not.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-02 Door wedge](lab:preset/S-02) builds a repeat with a loop instead. sleeve along it. The film leaves through the slot on top, and the rod cannot follow because the slot is narrower than the rod. It is two parts printed together on one plate, and the idea it teaches is a fit: the bore is sized from a clearance class, not drawn.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the end of it](lab:view/stage?view=side) to see the rod in the bore. Then [select the bore](lab:select/bore/bore[1]) and [leave more room for a thick bag](lab:param/sleeve.bag=0.5). For a cereal bag, set `L` in `P` to 160 mm and press [Run](lab:run): both parts read it.\n\n## How it is built\n\nBoth parts read the same params, `P`, so a change to `rod` or `L` changes both. `rod` is a hexagon on the YZ plane, `rod` (10 mm) across its corners, extruded `L + grip`: 8 mm stands out of the sleeve as a handle. The sleeve is a square section with 45° corners, extruded `L`, then bored and slotted.\n\nThe bore is `j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('rod + 2 * bag', 'slide'))`: the nominal size is the rod plus two layers of film, and the `slide` class adds the clearance on each side, so the modelled bore is about 11.1 mm. The slot, `rod * 0.6` wide (6 mm), runs along the top. The rod is 8.7 mm across its flats, so it stays in the sleeve however it turns.\n\nBoth parts are modelled on the bore's axis, as they sit in use; the print job lays them side by side on the plate.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOrientation is automatic: the rod lies on a flat, the sleeve on its bottom. The bore runs across the build direction, so the kernel makes it a 45° teardrop (a `teardrop` decision in the [trace](lab:show/trace)), and the slot cuts the teardrop's tip away. The outside corners are 45° chamfers for the same reason: a round corner on the bed would overhang. Both parts together are 21,383 mm³, about 20 g of PETG and 54 min. PETG flexes where PLA cracks, and it does not mind the freezer.\n\n> **Exercise.** A bag of frozen peas is thicker film: set `bag` to 0.4 mm and check that the bore is still smaller than `H − 2 · 2 mm`, so the sleeve wall stays at least 2 mm.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-04 Plant label](lab:preset/S-04) joins three extrudes into one part. [S-07 Furniture leg pads](lab:preset/S-07) prints four copies of one part on a plate.\n","script.js":"// S-05 · Bag clip: a hexagonal rod and a slotted sleeve, two parts, one plate.\n// Notice the bore: a hole with a fit class, so its size comes from the slide\n// clearance, and it prints as a teardrop because it lies across the build.\nconst P = {\n  L: mm(110),     // sleeve length: a crisp packet is 130–180 mm wide and is\n                  // folded once before clipping\n  rod: mm(10),    // hexagonal rod, measured across its corners\n  bag: mm(0.3),   // room for the folded film: two layers of a 0.05–0.1 mm\n                  // bag, plus slack\n  wall: mm(3),    // sleeve wall round the bore\n  grip: mm(8),    // the rod stands out of the sleeve by this much: a handle\n  slot: 'rod * 0.6',  // the film leaves through this slot; It is one sketch, one extrude and one emboss, and the idea it teaches is text: the name is real outlines from a font, raised from a face of the part, and the tag's length is written from the name.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look down on it](lab:view/stage?view=plan). Then [make the letters bigger](lab:param/size=10) and watch the tag grow with them, [select the ring hole](lab:select/plate/side[outline.ring]), or change `NAME` at the top of the script and press [Run](lab:run).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`outline` is a rounded rectangle `w` by `h` with a circle inside it, so `plate` comes out of one extrude with its 5 mm ring hole already in it. `letters` embosses `j.region.text(NAME, …)` onto `j.feat('plate').endFace()` by `relief`, `layer(4)`: four 0.2 mm layers. `size` is the cap height, 8 mm, and the text is left-aligned just past the ring end.\n\nThe width is an expression the script writes from the name: `` `${NAME.length} * size + h + 4 mm` ``, which for MAREN is `5 * size + h + 4 mm`, 62 mm. A capital in this font is at most about as wide as it is tall, so the name always fits, and because `size` and `h` stay symbolic, a change to `size` still lengthens the tag. The name is a JavaScript constant rather than a param: params hold numbers and sizes, and the text is something you type once.\n\n## Printing it\n\nFlat on the bed with the letters up. It is 3,281 mm³, 3 g of PLA and about 9 min. The plate is 15 layers, enough not to snap on a key ring; the letters are four layers, enough to feel.\n\nFor a two-colour tag on a single-nozzle printer, add a filament change (a pause, `M600`) at the first layer above 3.0 mm in the slicer. The plate prints in the first colour and the name in the second.\n\n> **Exercise.** Add a 1 mm raised border round the edge at the same height as the letters: emboss a second region, `j.region.rect('w', 'h', { r: 'h / 2 - 1 mm' })` minus a smaller one, so the letters are protected in a pocket.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-04 Plant label](lab:preset/S-04) is the tag's cousin for the garden, written by hand. Each one is a tapered disc with a blind pocket for the leg, and the print job makes four at once. The idea it teaches is configs: S, M and L are named rows of sizes, and switching between them changes several params together.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front) to see the taper. Then [fit a 32 mm leg](lab:param/leg=32), [straighten the sides](lab:param/taper=0), and [select the floor of the pocket](lab:select/pocket/floor[1]). Open [the params](lab:show/params) to see which values come from the config.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`body` extrudes a circle `base_d` across by `h` with a taper of −12°: a positive taper flares outward as it rises, so the sign is negative and the cup narrows to `leg + 2 * wall` at the rim. `base_d` is written from the taper, `leg + 2 * wall + 2 * h * tan(taper)`, so the rim keeps its 2.25 mm wall whatever the angle.\n\n`pocket` is a blind hole from the top, `j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('leg', 'snug'), 'depth')`. The snug class makes it a little larger than the leg, enough to push on by hand and stay on when the chair is lifted. `rim` rounds both edges of the top, so the pocket's mouth is a lead-in; `foot` chamfers the bed edge.\n\n## Configs\n\n`.configs({ S, M, L })` holds three rows: S is a 20 mm stool leg 6 mm deep, M a 28 mm dining-chair leg, L a 40 mm table leg with a 10 mm pocket and a sixth line in the wall. `.config('M')` picks the active row; a param that a row does not mention keeps its default. A value you set by hand overrides the config, and the [params view](lab:show/params) shows where each value came from.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOpen side up, four to a plate: 4,344 mm³ each, 18 g of TPU 95A for the set and about 49 min. TPU is soft, so it grips the floor and does not mark it; print it slowly (20–30 mm/s) with retraction low. In PETG the pads are harder and slide, which suits a chair you move a lot.\n\n> **Exercise.** Measure your own chair's feet and add a fourth config, `mine`, with `leg` and `depth`. It stands 38 mm off the wall and has a 22 mm lip so the loop cannot slide off. The idea it teaches is printing for strength: the hook lies on its side, so the profile is in every layer, and its screw holes become teardrops with countersunk mouths.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the profile](lab:view/stage?view=front). Then [select the plate's front face](lab:select/body/side[side.p.6]), [make it reach further for a thick coat](lab:param/reach=50) and [raise the lip](lab:param/lip=30).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`side` is one closed polyline on the XZ plane, eight corners written in params: x runs out of the wall, z up. `body` extrudes it `w` (22 mm) symmetrically about the plane. The corners of the profile are now the body's edges along Y, so two fillets pick them by shape: `edges('|Y').where(e => e.concave)` are the two inside corners, rounded to `arm / 2` (4.5 mm) because that is where the load concentrates; `where(e => e.convex)` are the outside corners, rounded to 2 mm.\n\n`screws` drills two M4 clearance holes (4.4 mm with the slide class) from the plate's front face. `countersink` then chamfers their mouths by `sink`, 2.4 mm at 45°: a 90° cone, the shape of a countersunk head, opened to 9.2 mm. The chamfer picks its edges as the ones the front face, `sideFace('p.6')`, shares with the holes.\n\n## Printing it\n\nThe job fixes `+Y` up, so the hook lies on its side. A hook loaded at the lip bends at the root of the arm; on its side, every layer runs round that corner and the arm cannot split between layers, which it would if printed upright. The holes now run across the build direction, so the kernel makes them 45° teardrops pointing up (two `teardrop` decisions in the [trace](lab:show/trace)), and the chamfer follows the teardrop, so the countersink needs no support either. It is 17,345 mm³, 11 g of PETG and about 34 min.\n\n## Changing it\n\n`H`, `reach`, `lip` and `w` are exposed. Hold `t` at 6 mm or more: the countersink takes 2.4 mm of it. For a heavy winter coat, widen it to 30 mm and add a third screw.\n\n> **Exercise.** Add a third screw at mid-height: add a third point to `screws` and check that `countersink` sinks it too, with no other change, because it selects edges by what they belong to.\n\n## Where next\n\n[S-01 Cable clip](lab:preset/S-01) is another side profile printed on its side. It is a revolve: a six-point half-section turned about Z. The idea it teaches is choosing the print direction from the shape: upside down, every surface of this knob leans inward or stands upright, so it needs no support at all.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front) and turn it upside down in your head. Then [make it a small drawer knob](lab:param/d=25), [give it 16 grooves](lab:param/flutes=16), or [select the insert hole](lab:select/insert/bore[1]).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`section` is the knob's profile on the XZ plane, x the radius and z the height: the base on the door, the neck narrowing to `neck_d`, the underside of the head rising at 45° to `d`, the rim and the flat top. `head` is written so the underside stays at 45°: `neck + (d - neck_d) / 2`. `body` revolves it a full turn about `j.axis.Z`; a revolve has no end caps, so the top and the base are picked by the profile segment that made them, `sideFace('p.5')` and `sideFace('p.1')`.\n\n`grip` is twelve 90° V-grooves round the rim. One triangle is patterned in the sketch with `j.pattern.circular`, so a single cut makes all twelve. The sketch sits below the head and the cut rises to 2.5 mm under the top, so each groove runs out through the 45° underside and stops short of the crown.\n\n`insert` is `j.hole.insert('M4')`: a Ø5.6 × 9.1 mm hole for an M4 × 8.1 brass heat-set insert, drilled up from the centre of the base. Press the insert in with a soldering iron at about 220 °C; the cabinet's bolt then threads into brass, not plastic.\n\n## Printing it\n\nThe job fixes `-Z` up: the flat top is the first layer and the base is the last. The crown chamfer is 1.5 mm across and 1 mm down, steeper than 45° on purpose, because it is now the bed edge; the grooves' ends face up; the insert hole opens upward, so it has no ceiling to bridge. It is 12,603 mm³, 6.5 g of PLA and about 25 min.\n\n> **Exercise.** Switch to `.config('L')` for a 40 mm wardrobe knob and check in the header that it still prints with no support.\n\n## Where next\n\n[H-06 Egg cup](lab:preset/H-06) is another turned profile, hollowed with a shell. It is a side profile of ten corners extruded to 70 mm, a cut for the cable and three rounding features. The idea it teaches is trigonometry in params: the leaning back is written with `sin(angle)` and `cos(angle)`, so one number tilts the whole profile.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the profile](lab:view/stage?view=front). From there the back's front face runs `S` along the angle, to `top_x = x0 - S * cos(angle)` and `top_z = b + S * sin(angle)`; its top is `t` across, perpendicular to it; and `foot_x` is where its back face comes down to the base. Each is an expression of the params, so the profile cannot come apart when `angle` changes.\n\n`cable` cuts a 13 mm notch through the seat, the lip and the base, centred on the width, so a USB-C plug goes straight down into the phone and the cable leaves under the front. `root` is a 10 mm fillet behind the back, where it meets the base: that corner carries the phone's weight. `round` softens the other outside corners, and `foot` chamfers the bed edges.\n\n## Configs\n\n`phone` is the default; `tablet` makes the back 120 mm, the base 120 by 140 mm and the seat 15 mm, enough for a 10 or 11 inch tablet in a case. The phone leans its centre of mass well inside the base in both, so the stand does not tip back.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright on its base. [Select the top of the upper peg](lab:select/body/side[side.p.3]).\n\n## The pegboard\n\nPegboard has 1/4 in (6.35 mm) holes on 1 in (25.4 mm) centres, in hardboard 1/4 in or 1/8 in thick. The profile is drawn with the face of the board at x = 0, the upper hole at z = 0 and the lower one at z = −`pitch`. The upper peg reaches 0.5 mm past the back of the board (`back`) and turns up by `rise` behind it; the lower peg is a stub 0.5 mm shorter than the board. To hang it, put the upper peg in at an angle and swing the hook down until the lower peg drops into the hole below.\n\n## The peg's section\n\nThe hook is `peg` = 5.2 mm wide, and the pegs are 5.2 mm tall in the profile, so their section is a 5.2 mm square, whose diagonal of 7.35 mm will not go into a 6.35 mm hole. `ease` chamfers every long edge by 1.2 mm, which cuts the corners off. The param `diag` measures what is left:\n\n```js\ndiag: '2 * hypot(peg / 2, peg / 2 - ease)',   // 5.9 mm, under the 6.35 mm hole\n```\n\nRaise `peg` and `diag` follows; keep it at least 0.3 mm under the hole.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOn its side, `.orient('+Y')`: the profile lies on the bed, so the arm, the tip and the turned-up peg bend within the layers instead of across them, and the 45° chamfers face the bed. 3.6 cm³, about 5 g of PETG and 13 min. bores for 3, 4, 5, 6, 8 and 10 mm drills, each with its size engraved in front of it, and a V-groove underneath that centres the guide on a dowel or a pipe. The idea it teaches is sizing holes by a fit class from a table: each row of `drills.csv` becomes one hole feature, and the class decides how much bigger than the drill the bore is.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the 6 mm bore](lab:select/bore-6/bore[1]), then [make the block 40 mm tall](lab:param/H=40) for a longer guide. [Turn the V-groove off](lab:param/vee=0) for flat work, and [look from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side) to see it.\n\n## The table and the bores\n\n`drills.csv` has a label and a diameter per drill. The block's length is an expression written from the table, every drill plus a 7 mm web between and outside them: 85 mm for the six drills here. `xOf(k)` writes the expression for the centre of bore k in the same way.\n\nEach row becomes `bore-<label>`, a countersunk through hole at a 3D point on the top face:\n\n```js\nj.hole.countersink(j.fit.hole(mm(r.d), 'snug'), mm(r.d + 2), deg(90), 'through')\n```\n\n`j.fit.hole(d, 'snug')` is the drill plus 0.1 mm a side plus 0.1 mm of hole compensation, so the 6 mm bore is modelled at 6.3 mm: printed holes come out a little small, and a drill should turn freely without wobble. The 1 mm lead-in at the top guides the drill's point in. The point is a 3D `j.pt(x, 0, 'H')` rather than a 2D `[x, 0]`, because only 3D points take expressions.\n\n## The groove and the sizes\n\n`groove` is a 90° V 8 mm deep, cut along the whole block: round stock up to 22 mm across sits in it centred under the bores. `size-<label>` engraves each size 0.6 mm deep, one region per drill; a text region given `at` lands where it says on the face.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright: every bore is vertical, so it prints round and on size, and the V's sides face the bed at 45°. `foot` chamfers the bed edges but leaves the groove's edges alone, since a chamfer there would be flatter than 45°. 70.8 cm³ solid, about 32 g of PETG and 89 min; three or four perimeters give each bore a solid wall.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [D-06 Memory-card holder](lab:preset/D-06): a block laid out from a table.\n- [W-08 Spacer and standoff set](lab:preset/W-08): a table that makes many parts.\n","script.js":"// W-03 · Drill guide: a block that holds a hand drill square to the work,\n// one bore per drill size in drills.csv, with a V-groove underneath for\n// centring on round stock. Notice that each bore is a hole feature sized\n// by a fit class, so a 6 mm drill gets a 6.3 mm bore that prints round.\nconst drills = data.drills.rows          // label, d (mm)\nconst n = drills.length\n\n// centre of bore k along X: the bores sit `web` apart, edge to edge\nconst xOf = k => `-L / 2 + ${k + 1} * web + ` +\n  drills.slice(0, k).map(r => `${r.d} [guide],\n}).orient('+Z').use(j.rules.fdm)\n"}},{"id":"W-04","title":"Knob for a D-shaft","group":"Workshop and repair","order":4,"difficulty":"medium","about":"a 30 mm fluted knob for a 6 mm D-shaft D-shaft\n\nA 30 mm fluted knob for a 6 mm D-shaft, the shaft with one flat found on potentiometers, rotary encoders and small gear motors. It pushes on with a snug fit and an M3 grub screw locks it on the flat. The idea it teaches is printing for accuracy: upside down, the D-bore is vertical and comes out round and on size, while the grub-screw hole lies horizontal and the kernel turns it into a teardrop.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan) to see the pointer end on a ridge, then [give it 24 flutes](lab:param/flutes=24). [Select the flat of the bore](lab:select/bore/side[d.flat]) and [fit a shaft with a 4 mm flat](lab:param/flat=4).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`grip` is a sketch of the rim and one flute circle, patterned 18 times round Z. The arrangement has many regions; `grip.profile([0, 0])` keeps the one that contains the centre, so the outer halves of the flutes are left out and `body` extrudes a scalloped disc. The first flute sits half a step round from +X so that the pointer, a groove along +X, ends on a ridge rather than in a flute.\n\n`d` is the bore's outline: an arc of radius `shaft / 2 + gap` drawn the long way round from one end of the flat to the other, closed by the line `flat`. Two derived params place the flat:\n\n```js\nflat_at: 'flat - shaft / 2 + gap',                          // 1.6 mm from the axis\nchord: 'sqrt(pow(shaft / 2 + gap, 2) - pow(flat_at, 2))',   // half the flat: 2.66 mm\n```\n\n`bore` cuts it 12 mm deep. `gap` is 0.1 mm a side, the snug fit class: the bore is Ø6.2 mm and 4.7 mm across the flat, a push fit on a 6 mm shaft with a 4.5 mm flat.\n\n## The grub screw\n\n`grub` drills from a point on a plane 1 mm outside the rim, in along −Y onto the flat: `j.hole.tapped('M3', { depth: 'R + 1 mm' })` is a 2.9 mm self-tapping hole, so an M3 grub screw cuts its own thread. The print job orients the knob with `-Z` up; the hole is then horizontal, and the kernel records a teardrop decision and makes it a 45° teardrop pointing up the print.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpside down, the flat top on the bed: 10.0 cm³, about 6 g of PLA and 20 min. it."},"defaultFragments":["hoses"]},"files":{"notes.md":"# Hose adapter\n\nA reducer that joins a 1/2\" hose to a 3/8\" one: three barbs on each end and a collar between them to push against. It is a single revolve of a half-section, and the half-section is a polyline whose points a loop in the script writes. The idea it teaches is shaping for the print: every face that looks down is at least 50° from the bed, so the adapter prints upright with no support.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the section from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front), then [fit a 5/8\" hose at the bottom](lab:param/d_a=15.9) and [a 1/4\" one at the top](lab:param/d_b=6.35). [Select the collar](lab:select/body/side[section.p.8]).\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe half-section lies on the XZ plane, x the radius and z the axis, and `body` revolves it a full turn about Z. The script writes its points with two loops, one per end, so `BARBS` sets how many barbs each end gets. Every point is an expression of the params: the barbs' tips are 0.6 mm under the hose's inside radius so a hose starts on easily, their crests 0.9 mm over it so the hose is stretched and grips.\n\n## Shaped for the print\n\nThe adapter stands on the 1/2\" end. Down there the barbs point down: each is a ramp that widens going up and then a flat back, and a flat back that faces up needs nothing under it. The upper barbs point up, so their backs face down; they are drawn as steep slopes rather than flat steps, using `steep`:\n\n```js\nsteep: 1.2,   // every face that looks down rises 1.2 for 1 across: 50° from the bed\n```\n\nThe underside of the collar and the step inside the bore where it narrows from 8.3 mm to 5.1 mm use the same slope. A steep back holds a hose a little less well than a square one; a hose clip over the barbs makes up for it.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright: 4.5 cm³, about 5 g of PETG and 28 min. The foot is a ring of only 50 mm², under the 100 mm² that the fdm rules ask of a first layer, so the job asks for a 5 mm brim with `.brim({ width: mm(5) })` and replaces the rule with `j.rule.firstLayer({ minArea: 40 })`. The brim is recorded for the slicer; the rule set says why the check is satisfied. PETG is the better choice for water: it is tougher than PLA and does not soften in the sun.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [W-04 Knob for a D-shaft](lab:preset/W-04): printing for accuracy.\n- [A-06 Extrude and revolve](lab:preset/A-06): the volume of a revolve.\n","script.js":"// W-05 · Hose adapter: joins a 1/2\" hose to a 3/8\" one, with three barbs on\n// each end and a collar between them, revolved from one half-section.\n// Notice the barbs: the lower ones have flat backs that face up, the upper\n// ones backs at 50°, so the whole adapter prints upright without support.\nconst BARBS = 3          // barbs on each end (the profile's shape, not a size)\n\n// The half-section on XZ (x is the radius, z the axis), drawn with a loop:\n// up the outside from the lower tip, then down the bore.\nconst pts = [['bore_a', 0], ['tip_a', 0]]\nfor (let k = 0; k < BARBS; k++) {\n  // lower barb: a ramp out to the crest, then a flat back (facing up)\n  pts.push(['crest_a', `${k + 1} * pitch`], ['tip_a', `${k + 1} * pitch`])\n}\npts.pop()                                            // the last back ...\npts.push(['collar', 'z_c0'], ['collar', 'z_c1'])     // ... becomes the collar\nfor (let k = 0; k < BARBS; k++) {\n  // upper barb: a steep back out to the crest, then a ramp in to the next tip\n  pts.push(['tip_b', `z_c1 + ${k} * pitch`],\n    ['crest_b', `z_c1 + ${k} * pitch + steep * (crest_b - tip_b)`])\n}\npts.push(['tip_b', 'z_top'], ['bore_b', 'z_top'],\n  ['bore_b', 'z_c1'], ['bore_a', 'z_c1 - steep * (bore_a - bore_b)'])\n\nconst adapter = j.part('hose-adapter')\n  .params({\n    d_a: mm(12.7),         // lower hose, inside diameter: 1/2 in\n    d_b: mm(9.5),          // upper hose, inside diameter: 3/8 in\n    pitch: mm(6),          // length of one barb\n    wall: mm(1.6),         // under the tips of the barbs\n    tip_a: 'd_a / 2 - 0.6 mm',   // the tips go into the hose freely ...\n    crest_a: 'd_a / 2 + 0.9 mm', // ... the crests stretch it\n    tip_b: 'd_b / 2 - 0.6 mm',\n    crest_b: 'd_b / 2 + 0.9 mm',\n    bore_a: 'tip_a - wall',\n    bore_b: 'tip_b - wall',\n    collar: 'max(crest_a, crest_b) + 3 mm',\n    collar_h: mm(5),\n    // every face that looks down rises 1.2 for 1 across: 50° from the bed,\n    // a little steeper than the 45° limit\n    steep: 1.2,\n    // the collar starts at the last crest, with a steep underside\n    z_c0: `${BARBS} * pitch + steep * (collar - crest_a)`,\n    z_c1: 'z_c0 + collar_h',\n    z_top: `z_c1 + ${BARBS} * pitch`,\n  })\n  .expose(['d_a', 'd_b', 'pitch', 'wall'], {\n    labels: {\n      d_a: 'lower hose inside diameter', d_b: 'upper hose inside diameter',\n      pitch: 'barb length', wall: 'wall under the barbs',\n    },\n    ranges: {\n      d_a: [mm(4), mm(25)], d_b: [mm(4), mm(25)], pitch: [mm(3), mm(10)],\n      wall: [mm(1.2), mm(3)],\n    },\n  })\n  .revolve(j.sketch('section', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .polyline('p', pts, { closed: true }).profiles(),\n  j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'body' })\n\n// Upright, the 1/2\" end on the bed: every face is vertical, faces up, or\n// slopes at 50°, so it needs no support. Its foot is a 50 mm² ring, under\n// the fdm rules' 100 mm² for a first layer: the job asks for a 5 mm brim\n// and lowers that rule's limit to 40 mm², which a brim makes safe.\nconst rules = j.rules('hose-adapter',\n  [j.rules.fdm, j.rule.firstLayer({ minArea: 40 })])\nreturn j.print('hose-adapter', {\n  printer: j.printer('generic-fdm'), material: j.material('PETG'),\n  parts: [adapter],\n}).orient('+Z').brim({ width: mm(5) }).use(rules)\n"}},{"id":"W-06","title":"Shelf bracket","group":"Workshop and repair","order":6,"difficulty":"medium","about":"an L-bracket with a gusset and four M4 countersunk holes, printed on its side so the bending load runs along the layers","view":"front","overlays":"shadows,grid,dims","tries":"Add a lip at the end of the arm that stops the shelf sliding off | Make the gusset hollow: a triangular frame 5 mm wide","agent":{"role":"a hardware designer who orients load-bearing printed parts for their bending load","prompt":"Prefer printing brackets on their side with gussets flush with the bed face; size screw holes with j.hole.countersink and a fit class; never put a hole where a screwdriver cannot reach it.","fragments":{"beam":"Check the arm as a beam: σ = M / Z with Z = b·t²/6, at the section where the gusset ends.","screws":"Use M4 countersunk wood screws into the wall and the shelf; M5 for loads over 15 kg."},"defaultFragments":["beam"]},"files":{"notes.md":"# Shelf bracket\n\nAn L-bracket for a light shelf: a 100 mm leg screwed to the wall, a 120 mm arm screwed up into the shelf, a gusset between them and four M4 countersunk holes. The idea it teaches is orientation for strength. A shelf load bends the bracket round its inside corner; printed on its side, that bending runs along the layers, where printed plastic is about twice as strong as across them.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front), then [make the arm 150 mm](lab:param/arm=150) and watch the gusset and the shelf holes follow. [Select the gusset's slope](lab:select/gusset/side[gusset.g.2]) and [thicken the bracket to 8 mm](lab:param/t=8).\n\n## How it is built\n\n`side` is the L on the XZ plane, the wall at x = 0 and the shelf's underside at z = 0; `body` extrudes it 25 mm. `corner` rounds the inside corner by 6 mm, where the bending stress is highest. `gusset` is a triangle reaching 60 % of the way along the leg and the arm, extruded 6 mm from the same plane, so it is flush with the face at y = 0 rather than centred.\n\n`wall` and `shelf` are `j.hole.countersink('M4', { fit: 'free' })` at 3D points on the inside of the leg and the underside of the arm, placed beyond the gusset's ends so a screwdriver reaches them. The spec takes the ISO 10642 head (8.96 mm) and the free gap: a 4.6 mm bore under a 9.56 mm, 90° countersink, so the heads of wood screws sit flush.\n\n## Printed on its side\n\n`.orient('-Y')` lays the face at y = 0 on the bed. Both extrudes run along −Y, the XZ plane's normal, so the gusset is on the bed too; a gusset in the middle of the width would float over nothing. The four holes are now horizontal and become teardrops, which the kernel records as decisions. 43.2 cm³, about 27 g of PETG and 74 min.\n\nA rough check of the arm, as a beam: 100 N (10 kg of shelf) at 100 mm from the wall bends the arm where the gusset ends, 74.4 mm out, by 100 N × 25.6 mm = 2560 N·mm. The arm's section there is 25 × 6 mm, with a section modulus of 25 × 6² / 6 = 150 mm³, so the stress is about 17 MPa: well under the 45 MPa or so of PETG along its layers, and too close to its strength across them to print the bracket standing up.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [W-07 Corner bracket for 2020 extrusion](lab:preset/W-07): a bracket with keys and counterbores.\n- [W-01 Pegboard hook](lab:preset/W-01): a smaller part printed on its side.\n","script.js":"// W-06 · Shelf bracket: an L-bracket with a gusset and countersunk screw\n// holes, printed on its side so the bending load runs along the layers.\n// Notice the gusset: it is flush with one side face, the one on the bed,\n// because a gusset in the middle would float over nothing when printed.\n// Side view on XZ: the wall is x = 0, the shelf's underside is z = 0; The profile comes from a table, so the same script makes brackets for 3030 and 4040. The idea it teaches is a key sized from the slot by a fit class, and drawn so that it prints on its side without support.\n\n> **Try it.** [Open the params](lab:show/params) and switch the configuration to `4040`: the bracket, its keys and its screws all scale. [Select the sloped side of a key](lab:select/key-a/side[key-a.k.2]), then [loosen the keys to 0.3 mm a side](lab:param/gap=0.3).\n\n## The table\n\n`profiles.csv` gives each extrusion its size, slot width and screw: 2020 has 6.2 mm slots and takes M5, 3030 and 4040 (B-type) have 8.2 mm slots and take M6 and M8. `j.table` turns the rows into configurations and `.config('2020')` picks one. The bracket's own sizes are ratios of `size`: legs of `1.4 * size` (28 mm), a thickness of `0.25 * size` (5 mm), a web of `0.2 * size`.\n\n## The keys\n\nA key is `slot − 2 · gap` wide, 5.8 mm for a 6.2 mm slot with the slide class's 0.2 mm a side, and 1.5 mm tall, so the bracket sits square on the extrusion and cannot turn about its screw. `key-a` and `key-b` are drawn as cross-sections, on YZ and on XY, and extruded along their legs, stopping 1 mm short of the corner so they clear each other and the corner of the extrusion. The side of each key that faces the bed in print slopes at 45°, which makes the key 4.3 mm wide at its tip: it still registers on its straight side.\n\n## Printing it\n\n`.orient('-Y')`: the face at y = 0 lies on the bed, with the web. The L, the web and the keys all grow straight up from it, and the screw holes, `j.fit.hole('screw_d', 'free')` at 5.7 mm (5 mm, 0.3 mm a side and 0.1 mm of hole compensation), become teardrops. 6.2 cm³, about 5 g of PETG and 20 min. Printed this way the corner bends within the layers; a bracket printed standing on a leg breaks along a layer line at the corner.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [W-06 Shelf bracket](lab:preset/W-06): the same orientation for a bigger load.\n- [W-08 Spacer and standoff set](lab:preset/W-08): more parts from one table.\n","profiles.csv":"profile,size,slot,screw_d\n2020,20 mm,6.2 mm,5 mm\n3030,30 mm,8.2 mm,6 mm\n4040,40 mm,8.2 mm,8 mm\n","script.js":"// W-07 · Corner bracket for aluminium extrusion: an inside corner bracket\n// with keys that register in the T-slots, for 2020, 3030 or 4040 profile\n// picked from a table. Notice the keys: each is a fit-class gap narrower\n// than the slot, so the bracket drops in square and cannot twist.\n// Slots: 2020 has 6.2 mm slots and M5 screws, 3030 and 4040 8.2 mm slots\n// with M6 and M8 screws (B-type profile).\nconst profiles = j.table([data.profiles.columns, ...data.profiles.rows\n  .map(r => data.profiles.columns.map(c => String(r[c])))])\n\n// Side view on XZ: extrusion A runs along X under z = 0, extrusion B stands\n// at x < 0; the bracket fills the corner. cupboard door whose concealed-hinge screws have torn out of the chipboard. The hinge's 35 mm cup passes through the plate into the door's old bore, the hinge's own screws bite into the plate, and four countersunk screws hold the plate in sound wood further out. The idea it teaches is placing holes by a standard: the hinge-screw spacing and offset come from a table of the common boring patterns.\n\n> **Try it.** [Open the params](lab:show/params) and switch the configuration to `45/9.5` for a Blum hinge, then back to `48/6`. [Set a 3 mm boring distance](lab:param/C=3) and [select the cup hole](lab:select/cup/bore[1]); it moves towards the door's edge.\n\n## The boring patterns\n\nA concealed hinge sits in a 35 mm cup bored into the door, with two screws on a line beside it. Makers use a few patterns, written as spacing / offset: the screws are `a` apart and `b` further from the door's edge than the cup's centre.\n\n| pattern | a | b | used by |\n|---|--:|--:|---|\n| 45/9.5 | 45 mm | 9.5 mm | Blum |\n| 48/6 | 48 mm | 6 mm | Hettich, Salice |\n| 52/5.5 | 52 mm | 5.5 mm | others |\n\n`hinges.csv` holds the three rows and `j.table` makes them configurations; with none chosen the middle one, 48/6, is active. The cup's centre is `e = cup / 2 + C` from the door's edge, where `C` is the boring distance, the gap between the edge and the cup, usually 3 to 6 mm.\n\n## How it is built\n\nThe plan is drawn with the door's edge along X at y = 0. `body` is a 96 × 51.5 mm plate, 4 mm thick, whose depth `D` follows from `e` and `b`. `cup` is `j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('cup', 'free'))`: 35 mm plus 0.3 mm a side plus 0.1 mm of hole compensation, 35.7 mm, so the cup goes through without binding. `hinge-screws` are 2.5 mm pilots for the hinge's own 3.5 mm chipboard screws, and `fixing` puts four M4 countersunk holes near the corners, two of them along the edge beyond the cup. `ease` chamfers the top edge of the outline by 1 mm; the selector subtracts the holes' edges so only the outline is chamfered.\n\n## Fitting and printing\n\nFlat, the door side on the bed: every hole is vertical and round and the countersinks open upwards. 15.0 cm³, about 12 g of PETG and 30 min. The plate raises the hinge by its 4 mm thickness; turn the hinge's depth screw to bring the door back into line, and use hinge screws 4 mm longer than the old ones so they reach through the plate into the wood.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [W-06 Shelf bracket](lab:preset/W-06): countersunk screws in a load-bearing part.\n- [W-03 Drill guide](lab:preset/W-03): drill the new fixing holes square.\n","script.js":"// W-10 · Hinge repair plate: a plate that bridges a cupboard door whose\n// concealed-hinge screws have torn out of the chipboard. The base screws down with two countersunk M3 screws and carries two captive M3 nuts; the cap closes over the cable with two more M3 screws. The idea it teaches is designing a clamp that clamps: the groove is smaller than the cable and the halves stop short of each other.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look along the cable](lab:view/stage?view=side), then [fit a 4 mm USB cable](lab:param/cable=4) and [squeeze it harder](lab:param/squeeze=1). Both halves follow: they are two bodies of one part. [Select a side of a nut pocket](lab:select/nuts/nut.1[1]).\n\n## Squeeze and gap\n\nEach half is drawn groove up, as it prints, with the cable's axis at `zc = h + gap / 2` above it, so when the cap is turned over onto the base the two axes meet. Each half has a groove of radius `rc = (cable − squeeze) / 2`, 2.7 mm for a 6 mm cable, and the halves stop `gap` = 1 mm apart. So the closed groove is 0.6 mm under the cable across, and tightening the screws squeezes the cable's jacket; if the groove matched the cable, the halves would meet first and the cable would slide.\n\n## How it is built\n\n`half(part, name, x0, width)` adds either half to the part: a rounded block `h` thick centred at `x0`, a groove cut along X with radius `rc − rib`, and three `relief` cuts of the full radius `rc` patterned along it. The base is at x = 0 and the cap at `cap_x`, 8 mm past the base's end. What the relief cuts leave between them are two ribs that stand 0.4 mm into the groove and bite into the jacket.\n\nThe base is wider. `nuts` is `j.hole.nutTrap('M3', { from: 'top' })` drilled up from the base's underside: a hex pocket 5.9 mm across the flats (5.5 mm plus the slide gap a side) for a nut pushed in before the base is screwed down. `mount` puts two M3 countersunk holes in the base's wings. The cap has two plain `j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'free' })` holes over the nuts.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOne plate, both halves groove up: the grooves are open channels that need no support. The check reports 41.1 mm² of overhang: the ceilings of the two nut pockets, which bridge 6 mm. 8.7 cm³, about 7 g of PETG and 18 min.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [X-02 LED strip channel](lab:preset/X-02): a long profile with a part that slides in.\n- [W-02 Vise soft jaws](lab:preset/W-02): one part printed twice, face down.\n","script.js":"// X-01 · Cable strain relief: a two-part clamp that grips a cable where it\n// leaves a box or a bench, so a tug pulls on the clamp and not on the\n// solder joints. A fixed hook takes the rail's top edge; a springy arm with a catch snaps over the bottom edge, and two M3 heat-set inserts in the front face carry whatever you mount. The idea it teaches is designing a snap fit by its strain, kept as a param you can watch.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at the section from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side), then [thin the arm to 1.2 mm](lab:param/ta=1.2) or [make the body shallower](lab:param/tp=10) and [open the params](lab:show/params) to see `strain` rise. [Select the catch's lead-in](lab:select/body/side[section.p.11]).\n\n## The rail\n\nEN 60715 TS35 × 7.5 rail is a top hat: its two flanges, 1 mm thick, stand 7.5 mm off the panel, and its outer edges are 35 mm apart. The section is drawn with the flanges' front face at y = 0 and the rail's centre at z = 0, so the edges are at z = ±`edge`. Each hook reaches `k` = 1.5 mm behind a flange with `g` = 0.3 mm of clearance.\n\n## The springy arm\n\nTo clip on, hook the top edge and swing the bottom in: the flange's edge rides down the catch's 45° lead-in and bends the arm until the catch springs up behind it. The arm is a cantilever, `ta` thick and `L_arm` long from its root to the catch, which has to bend by `k + g`. Its peak strain is the beam formula, written as a param:\n\n```js\nstrain: '1.5 * ta * (k + g) / pow(L_arm, 2)',   // 1.5 × 1.6 × 1.8 / 14.8² = 0.020\n```\n\nPETG takes about 0.04 before it yields, so 0.020 leaves a factor of two. The slit `s` over the arm runs back to within `root` = 2 mm of the front, which is what makes the arm long; a shallower body shortens it and the strain climbs with the square. To take the clip off, lever the catch down with a small screwdriver from below.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOn its side, `.orient('+X')`: the section lies on the bed, so the arm bends within its layers; printed standing, it would bend across them and snap at the root on the first use. The two insert holes, `j.hole.insert('M3x5.7')` at 4.0 mm, are horizontal in this orientation and become teardrops; press the inserts in from the front. 16.0 cm³, about 10 g of PETG and 35 min.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [X-05 Project box](lab:preset/X-05): a box to hang on it.\n- [W-01 Pegboard hook](lab:preset/W-01): another part printed on its side.\n","script.js":"// X-03 · DIN-rail clip: hooks a board, a relay module or a small box onto\n// a 35 mm top-hat rail. k'], ['-(f + g)', 'edge - k'],   // the fixed hook\n    ['-(f + g)', 'edge + g'], [0, 'edge + g'],\n    [0, '-edge - g + s'],                         // down the rail's face\n    ['tp - root', '-edge - hh'],                  // lead-in on its back\n    ['-back', '-edge - g - ta'],\n    ['tp', '-edge - g - ta'],                     // under the arm\n  ], { closed: true })\n\nconst clip = j.part('din-clip')\n  .params({\n    edge: mm(17.5),        // half the rail's height\n    f: mm(1),              // flange thickness\n    g: mm(0.3),            // clearance round the flanges\n    k: mm(1.5),            // how far each hook reaches behind a flange\n    hh: mm(1.5),           // hook thickness\n    back: 'f + g + hh',    // how far the hooks reach behind the face\n    tp: mm(14),            // depth of the body in front of the rail\n    ta: mm(1.6),           // arm thickness\n    s: mm(1.2),            // the slit over the arm\n    root: mm(2),           // where the arm joins the body\n    top: 'edge + g + hh',\n    w: mm(30),             // width along the rail\n    // the arm bends by k + g to let the catch past the flange\n    L_arm: 'tp - The cell comes from a table, so the same script holds 14500 or 21700 cells. The idea it teaches is a pocket that holds by its shape: each bay is a cylinder cut whose axis is just below the rim, so the cell clicks in, and still nothing in the trough leans more than 17° from vertical.\n\n> **Try it.** [Hold four cells](lab:param/n=4), then [open the params](lab:show/params) and switch the configuration to `21700`. [Select the trough of a bay](lab:select/bay/side[bay.c]) and [look from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan) to see the contact slots and tab holes.\n\n## The table\n\n`cells.csv` lists three common lithium-ion sizes by their largest diameter and length: 14500 Ø14.5 × 50.5 mm, 18650 Ø18.6 × 65.2 mm and 21700 Ø21.7 × 70.6 mm. These are unprotected cells; a protected 18650 is up to 69 mm long, so add 4 mm to `l` for those. With no configuration chosen, the middle row, 18650, is active.\n\n## The bay\n\n`bay` is a circle on the XZ plane, `d / 2 + gap` in radius (the free class's 0.3 mm), cut along Y for the bay's length `Lb = l + spring`: 70.2 mm, with 5 mm for the spring contact compressed. The cells' axis is at `zc` and the rim at `zc + 0.15 · d`, so the opening at the rim is 2 × √(9.6² − 2.79²) = 18.37 mm, 0.23 mm under the cell: a cell presses in past the rim and stays when the holder is turned over. At the rim the wall leans 17° from vertical, well inside the 45° limit.\n\n`slots` cuts a 0.8 mm × 12 mm slot down the inside of each end wall for a 0.3 to 0.5 mm contact plate, and `tabs` opens a 4 × 1.5 mm hole through the floor below it for the solder tab. `bays` patterns the three cuts together, `n` times at the cell pitch. Wire the tabs under the holder: end to end for cells in series, side by side for cells in parallel.\n\n## Printing it\n\nUpright: 22.0 cm³, about 21 g of PETG and 55 min. The slots, the tab holes and the two M3 holes in the end walls are vertical, so they print to size. The check's 2.2 mm² is the corners of the 0.5 mm bed chamfer, which sit exactly at 45°. PETG keeps its shape near a warm cell better than PLA.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [D-04 Battery holder](lab:preset/D-04): cells standing up, from a table and a count.\n- [X-05 Project box](lab:preset/X-05): a box to put the pack in.\n","script.js":"// X-04 · 18650 holder: a tray for lithium-ion cells lying side by side,\n// with slots at the ends of each bay for the sheet-metal contacts and a\n// hole in the floor for each contact's solder tab. Cell sizes come from a\n// table; 18650 is Ø18.6 × 65.2 mm (unprotected). Notice the bay: a\n// cylinder cut whose axis is below the top, so the rim holds the cell, yet\n// no part of the trough leans more than 17° from vertical.\nconst cells = j.table([data.cells.columns, ...data.cells.rows\n  .map(r => data.cells.columns.map(c => String(r[c])))])\n\n// one bay, cut along Y and patterned along X\nconst bay = j.sketch('bay', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .circle('c', 'd / 2 + gap', { at: ['x0', 'zc'] })\n// the contact slots and tab holes of the first bay, both ends\nconst slots = j.sketch('slots', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'H') })\n  .rect('a', 'contact_w', 'slot_t',\n    { center: true, at: ['x0', 'Lb / 2 + slot_t / 2'] })\n  .rect('b', 'contact_w', 'slot_t',\n    { center: true, at: ['x0', '-Lb / 2 - The box and the lid are two bodies of one part, laid out side by side as they print. The idea it teaches is keeping two pieces consistent by building them from the same params and the same list of corners, so the screws in the lid always land on the inserts in the box.\n\n> **Try it.** [Make it 120 mm wide](lab:param/W=120) and watch the bosses and the screws move together, then [make it 50 mm tall](lab:param/H=50). [Select the gland hole](lab:select/gland/bore[1]) and [set it for a PG9 gland](lab:param/gland=15.2).\n\n## The box\n\n`body` is a rounded block and `shell` hollows it with the top open; the walls and the floor are `lw(5)`, five line widths or 2.25 mm. In each inside corner, `boss1` to `boss4` add `j.lib.insert('M3x5.7').boss({ wall: 'boss_wall', height: 'H - wall' })`: a column from the floor to the rim, 6.7 mm across, with the insert's 4.0 × 6.7 mm hole at its top. Each boss is placed at `j.pt(± bx, ± by, 'wall')`, where `bx` and `by` put it against both walls so it joins them and stiffens the corner.\n\n`gland` is `j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('gland', 'free'))`, 12.5 mm for a PG7 thread plus the free gap, drilled through the right-hand end only (`depth: 'wall + 1 mm'`). It is horizontal, so the print job makes it a teardrop.\n\n## The lid\n\n`lid` is a second body, extruded from the same outline `lid_y` = `D + 8 mm` along Y, so it lies beside the box at any size. Its four `j.hole.countersink('M3', { fit: 'free' })` holes use the same `corners`, shifted by `lid_y`, so they sit over the four inserts. `edge` chamfers the lid's top outline by 1 mm; the selector subtracts the screws' edges so only the outline is touched. Because box and lid are one part, a change in Params reaches both; two parts in a print job would each get their own copy of every param.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOne plate, both bodies as they sit: the box on its floor and the lid on its underside, so the countersinks open upwards. 52.7 cm³ for the two, about 64 g of PETG and 167 min. The board's numbers live in `pico.json`. The idea it teaches is building a case outwards from the board: every size of the tray is the board plus a clearance plus a wall, and the lid's lip is the inside of the tray less a fit-class gap. Tray and lid are two bodies of one part, so every param reaches both.\n\n> **Try it.** [Raise the board to 6 mm](lab:param/standoff=6) for wires underneath, then [loosen the lid](lab:param/gap=0.2). [Select the floor of the USB notch](lab:select/usb/start) and [look from the side](lab:view/stage?view=side).\n\n## The board\n\n`pico.json` holds the datasheet's numbers: a 51 × 21 × 1 mm board, four 2.1 mm holes 47 mm apart along it and 11.4 mm across, and a micro-USB socket 8 mm wide and 2.6 mm tall at one end. The script reads them into `P`, so `hx` and `hy`, half the hole spacings, place the standoffs.\n\n## The tray\n\nThe inside is the board plus `clear` (1 mm) all round, the outside that plus `wall` (`lw(4)`, 1.8 mm). `posts` is a sketch with one circle and a mirror pattern of it across both axes; it is extruded from the floor by `standoff` and, drawn again at the posts' tops, gives the hole feature its four centres: `j.hole.tapped('M2', { depth: 'standoff' })` is 1.9 mm, so M2 × 6 mm screws cut their own thread. The USB notch is cut down from the rim to 0.5 mm below the board's top face, the wire notch to the board's top face.\n\n## The lid\n\nThe lid is drawn as it prints: upside down, `lid_y` along Y from the tray, its top on the bed. Its lip is a rectangle with a rectangular hole, the inside of the tray less `gap` (0.1 mm, the snug class) on each side and 1.2 mm thick, standing 3 mm up from the plate. In use it goes 3 mm down into the tray, and the tray's height leaves 3.9 mm above the USB socket, so the lip clears it.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOne plate, as drawn: 8.4 cm³ for both, about 11 g of PETG and 30 min. two-part case for a Raspberry Pi 4 Model B: a base whose four standoffs carry M2.5 heat-set inserts, and a lid with openings for every socket, vents over the processor and four tubes that press the board down. Four M2.5 screws go through the tubes and the board's holes into the inserts, so one set of screws holds all three together. Base and lid are two bodies of one part on one plate: the base as it sits, the lid upside down beside it, as it prints. The idea it teaches is splitting a case where the openings print well: at the underside of the board, every socket opening becomes a notch from an edge, so neither part needs a bridge.\n\n> **Try it.** [Select the end of the Ethernet notch](lab:select/ethernet/side[ethernet.o.bottom]), then [give the USB end more room](lab:param/over=5) and [raise the board](lab:param/standoff=6): the openings follow the board. [Look from above](lab:view/stage?view=plan) to see the tubes over the board's holes and the vents between them.\n\n## The board and its sockets\n\n`pi4.json` holds the board (85 × 56 × 1.4 mm), its holes 3.5 mm in from the edges on a 58 × 49 mm rectangle, and a row per socket: its edge (`front` for the USB-C edge, `right` for the USB and Ethernet end, `left` for the SD card), its centre along that edge, its width and its height above the board. The positions are the Raspberry Pi 4 drawing's: USB-C at 11.2 mm, the micro-HDMI sockets at 26.0 and 39.5 mm, audio at 53.5 mm, and the USB stacks and Ethernet at 9.0, 27.0 and 45.75 mm up the end.\n\n## The openings\n\n`opening(part, p)` turns one row into one cut: a rectangle on a plane just outside the socket's wall, `gap` (1 mm) wider than the socket each side, from the split to the socket's top plus `gap`, cut inwards through the wall. The SD card, which sits under the board, gets a notch down from the top of the base instead. The script folds the whole table into the part with `reduce`, so a new row is a new opening.\n\nThe lid is drawn turned over about X and moved `lid_y` along Y, so a point (x, y, z) of the closed case is (x, `lid_y − y`, `top − z`) on the lid. `ly(y)` writes the lid's y for a board y, and `opening` measures a lid opening's heights down from `top`; that is the only difference between a cut in the base and one in the lid.\n\n## The two parts\n\nThe base is a tray as tall as the board's underside, with `posts` on the holes and `j.hole.insert('M2.5x4')`, 3.6 × 5 mm, in each. The lid is a shell whose open side faces up on the plate; `tubes` rise from its roof to where the board's top face will be, and `screws` are M2.5 free-fit clearance holes through them, for M2.5 × 25 mm screws. `vent` is a 2.5 mm slot through the roof, patterned seven times 5 mm apart.\n\n## Printing it\n\nOne plate, as drawn: the base upright and the lid on its roof. Every opening in the lid is a notch that opens upwards and the vent slots go through its first layers. 31.4 cm³ for both, about 40 g of PETG and 104 min. In the\n// closed case it runs from the split (z0) to the socket's top (z1); the SD\n// card, under the board, gets a notch down from the top of the base.\nconst opening = (part, p) => {\n  const inBase = p.z0 < 0\n  const z0 = inBase ? `board_z + ${p.z0} mm - gap` : 'board_z - 1 mm'\n  const z1 = inBase ? This chapter shows how jig represents a quantity as a number with a dimension, why arithmetic on dimensions catches a class of mistakes at the line that makes them, and how units that belong to a printer (line widths and layer heights) stay symbolic until a printer is chosen. By the end you can read `lw(3).add(mm(0.1))` as a value, say what it becomes on a given machine, and explain why the compiler may cache it before it knows.\n\n## 1.1 A quantity carries its dimension\n\nA **quantity** is a number and a **dimension**: a vector of integer exponents over the base dimensions length L, mass M, time T, temperature Θ, angle and money. `mm(3)` is 3 with exponents L¹; an area has L², a density M¹L⁻³. jig stores every quantity in one canonical unit per dimension (millimetres, grams, seconds, radians, US dollars) and converts at the edges.\n\nThe exponents make two rules mechanical. **Multiplication adds exponents**: `mm(2).mul(mm(3))` is 6 mm², and a density times a volume is a mass, because M¹L⁻³ plus L³ is M¹. **Addition requires equal exponents**: a length plus an angle has no meaning, and\n\n```js\nmm(2).add(deg(3))\n// DimensionError: cannot add mm (L¹) to ° (angle¹)\n```\n\nthrows at the line that wrote it. Mathematically the quantities of one dimension form a vector space, and the dimensions themselves form a free abelian group under multiplication; this is what the SI Brochure's dimensional analysis amounts to, and it is the whole of what jig needs from it.\n\nThe test block below is 40 × 40 × 20 mm. Its volume is 32,000 mm³, and PLA's density is 1.24 g/cm³, so\n\n$$\nm = \\rho V = 1.24\\ \\mathrm{g/cm^3} \\times 32\\ \\mathrm{cm^3} = 39.68\\ \\mathrm{g} .\n$$\n\nThe compiler gives the same 39.68 g; the mass pill in the header rounds it to 40 g. Changing the material to PETG, 1.27 g/cm³, changes the mass to 40.64 g and leaves the geometry, and its cache, untouched.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nreturn j.part('block')\n  .material(j.material('PLA'))\n  .params({ w: mm(40), h: mm(20) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'w'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n**Figure 1.1.** A 40 × 40 × 20 mm block, compiled. Its volume, 32,000 mm³, is a quantity with exponents L³; times PLA's density it gives 39.68 g.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A test block and its mass\n// Change the material to 'PETG' or 'PA-CF' and read the mass pill.\nreturn j.part('block')\n  .material(j.material('PLA'))\n  .params({ w: mm(40), h: mm(20) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'w'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n```\n\nMoney works the same way. The default cost of PLA is 22 usd/kg, a quantity with exponents money¹M⁻¹, so the block's material costs 39.68 g × 22 usd/kg = 0.87 usd. Euros are money too, converted to dollars at `j.units.rates.eur`, 1.08 usd per eur by default.\n\n## 1.2 Bare numbers and typed slots\n\nWriting `mm(…)` around every number would make scripts unreadable, so jig lets a **slot** decide. A slot is an argument position with a declared kind: the radius of a circle is a length, the angle of a pattern is an angle, the count of a pattern is a plain number. A bare number in a length slot is millimetres; in an angle slot it is degrees, the unit people type; anywhere else it is dimensionless. So `.circle('hole', 2.2)` is a 2.2 mm radius and `j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 3, angle: 180 })` turns 180°.\n\nStrings are expressions, evaluated against the part's params with units written after numbers. The socket below holds a 1/4 in hex bit. Its across-flats size is written as it would be said, a quarter inch plus 0.3 mm of clearance:\n\n```js\n.params({ af: j.q('1/4 in + 0.3 mm'), h: mm(14), d: mm(16) })\n```\n\n`j.q` parses the text, converts each term to millimetres and adds: 6.35 + 0.3 = 6.65 mm, which the Params view shows as 0.2618 in because a parsed quantity keeps the first unit it was written in for display. The value inside is 6.65 mm whatever the display.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst socket = j.sketch('top', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .polygon('hex', 6, 'af / 2', { inscribed: false })\nreturn j.part('socket')\n  .params({ af: j.q('1/4 in + 0.3 mm'), h: mm(14), d: mm(16) })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle('d / 2'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(socket.profiles(), mm(10), { id: 'pocket' })\n```\n\n**Figure 1.2.** A socket for a 1/4 in hex bit: a 16 mm disc 14 mm tall with a hexagonal pocket 6.65 mm across flats and 10 mm deep. Inches and millimetres meet in one expression; the cut goes into the material because everything lies behind the sketch plane, a decision the Program records.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A hex-bit socket in mixed units\n// Try af: j.q('5/16 in + 0.3 mm') for a larger bit, or a bare 7.\nconst socket = j.sketch('top', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .polygon('hex', 6, 'af / 2', { inscribed: false })\nreturn j.part('socket')\n  .params({ af: j.q('1/4 in + 0.3 mm'), h: mm(14), d: mm(16) })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle('d / 2'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(socket.profiles(), mm(10), { id: 'pocket' })\n```\n\nA dimension error inside an expression is caught the same way as in code, with the column. The param `h: 'w - 3'`, where `w` is 60 mm, gives `units.dimension: 'w - 3': cannot subtract 1 (dimensionless) from mm (L¹)`. The fix is `'w - 3 mm'`.\n\n> **Careful.** `polygon(…, { inscribed: false })` takes the **apothem**, the distance from the centre to a flat, which is why the socket passes `af / 2`. With the default, `inscribed: true`, the size is the circumradius, the distance to a corner, and a hexagon is then $2/\\sqrt{3} \\approx 1.155$ times wider across corners than across flats.\n\n## 1.3 Printer units resolve late\n\nSome sizes in a printed part are not lengths in their own right but multiples of the machine. A wall should be a whole number of extrusion lines, or the slicer fills the remainder with a thin, weak line or a gap. A floor should be a whole number of layers. jig has units for these: `lw(n)` is n line widths, `layer(n)` n layer heights, `nozzle(n)` n nozzle diameters. They are **symbolic**: `lw(3)` stays \"three line widths\" until a printer says how wide a line is.\n\nThe tray in Figure 1.3 has walls of `lw(3)` and a floor of `layer(5)`. Compiled for three printers:\n\n| printer | line width | layer | wall | floor | volume | mass (PLA) |\n|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|\n| mk4 | 0.45 mm | 0.2 mm | 1.35 mm | 1.00 mm | 5,942.5 mm³ | 7.37 g |\n| x1c | 0.42 mm | 0.2 mm | 1.26 mm | 1.00 mm | 5,713.6 mm³ | 7.08 g |\n| generic-msla | 0.05 mm | 0.05 mm | 0.15 mm | 0.25 mm | 1,027.4 mm³ | 1.27 g |\n\nThe script does not change between rows: only `j.printer(…)` does. On the X1C the wall is three of its own 0.42 mm lines; on a resin printer, three lines are 0.15 mm, which is too thin to handle and shows why a wall in line widths suits FDM and not resin. A resin part would say `mm(1.2)`. To see the difference on your own machine, [C-06](lab:preset/C-06) prints six fins from one to four line widths thick and shows which ones the slicer fills, leaves gaps in or drops.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst pocket = j.sketch('pocket', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('r', 'w - 2 * wall', 'd - 2 * wall', { center: true, r: 3 })\nconst tray = j.part('tray')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(15), wall: lw(3), floor: layer(5) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { r: 'wall + 3 mm' }), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(pocket.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })\nreturn j.print('tray', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [tray] })\n```\n\n**Figure 1.3.** A 60 × 40 × 15 mm tray whose wall is `lw(3)` and floor `layer(5)`, in a print job for an mk4: 1.35 mm walls, a 1.00 mm floor. The outer corner radius is `wall + 3 mm`, so the inner corners stay 3 mm whatever the wall.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A tray in printer units\n// Change 'mk4' to 'x1c' or 'generic-msla' and read wall and floor in Params.\nconst pocket = j.sketch('pocket', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('r', 'w - 2 * wall', 'd - 2 * wall', { center: true, r: 3 })\nconst tray = j.part('tray')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(15), wall: lw(3), floor: layer(5) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { r: 'wall + 3 mm' }), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(pocket.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })\nreturn j.print('tray', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [tray] })\n```\n\nYour own printer is an object: `j.printer({ bed: [180, 180, 180], nozzle: mm(0.6), lw: mm(0.68) })` makes `lw(3)` 2.04 mm. A part compiled without a print job resolves against `generic-fdm`, whose line is 0.45 mm and layer 0.2 mm.\n\n## 1.4 Resolution is linear, so it can wait\n\nA symbolic quantity is a sum of terms, each a coefficient times a unit: `lw(3).add(mm(0.1))` prints as `3lw + 0.1mm`. **Resolving** it substitutes a printer context, and the result is linear in the context's values:\n\n$$\n\\operatorname{resolve}(a \\cdot \\mathrm{lw} + b) = a \\cdot \\mathrm{lw}_0 + b .\n$$\n\nWith the default 0.45 mm line that is 3 × 0.45 + 0.1 = 1.45 mm; with a 0.68 mm line, 3 × 0.68 + 0.1 = 2.14 mm. Both numbers are what `.resolve({ lw })` returns.\n\nLinearity is what makes waiting safe. Sums, differences and multiples of symbolic values stay sums of the same form, so an expression such as `'w - 2 * wall'` can be carried symbolically and resolved once, with the same result as resolving each term first. Products of two symbolic values, such as a line width times a layer height (the cross-section of one extruded line), and `min` or `max` of them, are not linear; jig keeps those **lazy**, `(2lw)·(1layer)`, and evaluates them when the context arrives. In both cases the compiler can hash a part, and cache everything that does not depend on the printer, before any printer is chosen. That is the property the quantity laws of the spec (`quantity.*`, §8) exist to justify.\n\nThe pen cup of [D-01](lab:preset/D-01) is a real use. Its wall is `lw(4)`, 1.8 mm on the generic printer, chosen so the slicer lays exactly four perimeters with no gap fill between them.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst cup = j.part('cup')\n  .params({ d: mm(60), h: mm(40), wall: lw(4) })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle('d / 2'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { id: 'shell' })\nreturn j.print('cup', { printer: j.printer('generic-fdm'), parts: [cup] })\n```\n\n**Figure 1.4.** A 60 mm cup shelled to `lw(4)`: four extrusion lines, 1.8 mm on the generic printer. The shell offsets the floor by the same amount; the tray of Figure 1.3 sizes its wall and floor separately.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A cup four lines thick\n// Make wall lw(4).add(mm(0.1)) and compare the volume pill.\nconst cup = j.part('cup')\n  .params({ d: mm(60), h: mm(40), wall: lw(4) })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle('d / 2'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { id: 'shell' })\nreturn j.print('cup', { printer: j.printer('generic-fdm'), parts: [cup] })\n```\n\n> **Try it.** [Load D-01](lab:preset/D-01), [open Params](lab:show/params) and find `wall`: its value is 1.8 mm and its raw value `4lw`.\n\n## 1.5 Key points\n\n- A quantity is a number with a vector of dimension exponents; multiplication adds the exponents and addition requires them to be equal.\n- A dimension mistake, in code or in an expression string, is an error at the line or column that made it.\n- In a typed slot a bare number is millimetres or degrees; elsewhere it is dimensionless.\n- `lw`, `layer` and `nozzle` are symbolic units that a printer resolves: `lw(3)` is 1.35 mm on an mk4 and 1.26 mm on an X1C.\n- Resolution is linear in the printer's values, so symbolic sizes can be combined, hashed and cached before a printer is known.\n- Mass, cost and print time are quantities too, derived from volume by a material's density and price.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** In the block of Figure 1.1, write the height as `'w / 2'` and check that the volume is unchanged. Then write it as `'w / 2 + 3'` and read the diagnostic.\n\n> **Exercise.** Make the tray's floor `layer(5)` on a printer with 0.12 mm layers, `j.printer({ layer: mm(0.12) })`. What floor thickness do you get, and how many layers is it?\n\n> **Exercise.** A wall of `lw(2.5)` is legal but prints badly. Explain why in terms of perimeters, and write a rule with `j.rule.custom` that warns when a wall param's raw value is not a whole number of line widths.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- [BIPM, The International System of Units (SI Brochure)](https://www.bipm.org/en/publications/si-brochure): the definitions of the base dimensions and of dimensional analysis that jig's quantities follow.\n- [The jig reference](lab:reference): every unit constructor and quantity method, with status.\n"},{"file":"02-parameters.md","text":"---\npart: I · Foundations\npresets: D-03, A-01\ndocs: getting-started\n---\n# Parameters as a graph\n\nA useful part has a handful of sizes a person would change and many more that follow from them: the width of a compartment follows from the width of the tray, the number of compartments and the wall between them. This chapter shows how jig stores those relations as a graph of named expressions, evaluates it in dependency order, records which layer of the program supplied each value, and uses the graph to recompute only what a change can reach. By the end you can read a parameter's provenance, choose a configuration, and predict which features a change will rebuild.\n\n## 2.1 Parameters are named expressions\n\n`.params({…})` declares **parameters**: names bound to quantities, numbers or expression strings. An expression names other parameters, so the set of parameters is a directed graph, with an edge from each name an expression mentions to the parameter that mentions it. The desk tray of [D-03](lab:preset/D-03), reduced to its essentials, has eight inputs and two derived sizes:\n\n```js\n.params({\n  w: mm(160), d: mm(100), h: mm(30), wall: lw(4), floor: layer(8),\n  nx: 3, ny: 2, r_in: mm(6),\n  cw: '(w - (nx + 1) * wall) / nx',\n  cd: '(d - (ny + 1) * wall) / ny',\n})\n```\n\n`cw` depends on `w`, `nx` and `wall`; `cd` on `d`, `ny` and `wall`. The compiler sorts the graph topologically and evaluates it in **levels**: level 0 holds the parameters with no dependencies, level k those whose dependencies are all below k. Here `cw` and `cd` are at level 1. Each value is evaluated once, after everything it reads.\n\nThe Program explains every derived value in the same terms. `why('cw')` answers\n\n```text\ncw = (w − (nx + 1)·wall) / nx = (160 mm − (3 + 1)·1.8 mm) / 3 = 50.9333 mm;\nnx from configs.mixed\n```\n\nwhich is the expression, the expression with values substituted, the result, and where the inputs that did not come from `.params` came from.
