The 1.8 mm is `lw(4)` resolved on the generic printer ([Chapter 1](lab:book?ch=1)).\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// height: 280\nconst pockets = j.sketch('pockets', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('c', 'cw', 'cd', {\n    center: true, r: 'r_in',\n    at: ['-w / 2 + wall + cw / 2', '-d / 2 + wall + cd / 2'],\n  })\n  .pattern(['c'], j.pattern.grid(j.dir('+X'), j.dir('+Y'), {\n    count: ['nx', 'ny'], step: ['cw + wall', 'cd + wall'],\n  }))\nreturn j.part('tray')\n  .params({\n    w: mm(160), d: mm(100), h: mm(30), wall: lw(4), floor: layer(8),\n    nx: 3, ny: 2, r_in: mm(6),\n    cw: '(w - (nx + 1) * wall) / nx',\n    cd: '(d - (ny + 1) * wall) / ny',\n  })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { r: 'r_in + wall' }), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(pockets.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })\n```\n\n**Figure 2.1.** The tray at its defaults: 160 × 100 × 30 mm, three by two compartments of 50.93 × 47.30 mm, walls of 1.8 mm and a 1.6 mm floor. Nothing in the sketch is a number: every corner is an expression of the params, so the compartments follow any change to the outside size.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A tray whose compartments follow its size\n// Set nx to 4 or w to 200 mm: cw follows, and so does every pocket.\nconst pockets = j.sketch('pockets', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('c', 'cw', 'cd', {\n    center: true, r: 'r_in',\n    at: ['-w / 2 + wall + cw / 2', '-d / 2 + wall + cd / 2'],\n  })\n  .pattern(['c'], j.pattern.grid(j.dir('+X'), j.dir('+Y'), {\n    count: ['nx', 'ny'], step: ['cw + wall', 'cd + wall'],\n  }))\nreturn j.part('tray')\n  .params({\n    w: mm(160), d: mm(100), h: mm(30), wall: lw(4), floor: layer(8),\n    nx: 3, ny: 2, r_in: mm(6),\n    cw: '(w - (nx + 1) * wall) / nx',\n    cd: '(d - (ny + 1) * wall) / ny',\n  })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { r: 'r_in + wall' }), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(pockets.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })\n```\n\nA graph that is not acyclic has no evaluation order. `.params({ wall: 'floor / 2', floor: 'wall * 2' })` asks for each of `wall` and `floor` before the other, and the compiler reports the cycle with its path and a fix instead of looping:\n\n```text\nparam.cycle: wall → floor → wall\nfix: give one of them a value: wall has no base value\n```\n\nA name the graph does not contain is `param.unknown`, with the column in the expression where it appears.\n\n## 2.2 Layers, configurations and provenance\n\nA parameter can be set in several places, and the program must say which one wins. jig stacks them in **layers**, from lowest to highest precedence: a library's default, `.params`, a row of `.configs`, `.override`, and the overrides from the Params view. A higher layer **shadows** a lower one; the lower value is kept, so the Program can show what was replaced.\n\nA **configuration** is a named set of overrides, one row of a small table. The tray declares three:\n\n```js\n.configs({\n  pens: { nx: 4, ny: 1, d: mm(80) },\n  mixed: { nx: 3, ny: 2 },\n  parts: { nx: 4, ny: 3, h: mm(25) },\n})\n```\n\nWith no `.config(…)` chosen, the middle row is active, here `mixed`: a default that lies inside the family, not at one end of it. Every value records its source:\n\n| config | nx × ny | d | h | cw | cd | volume |\n|---|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|\n| pens | 4 × 1 | 80 mm (shadows 100 mm) | 30 mm | 37.75 mm | 76.40 mm | 58,310.0 mm³ |\n| mixed | 3 × 2 | 100 mm | 30 mm | 50.93 mm | 47.30 mm | 73,180.5 mm³ |\n| parts | 4 × 3 | 100 mm | 25 mm (shadows 30 mm) | 37.75 mm | 30.93 mm | 79,472.3 mm³ |\n\nThe `parts` tray is 5 mm lower and yet uses more material. Its twelve pockets cover less floor than the six of `mixed`, 12 × 37.75 × 30.93 mm against 6 × 50.93 × 47.30 mm before the corner rounding, because two more dividers take up the difference. `why('nx')` under `mixed` reads `nx = 3; from configs.mixed`, and lists the shadowed `params` value of 3.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// height: 260\nconst pockets = j.sketch('pockets', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('c', 'cw', 'cd', {\n    center: true, r: 'r_in',\n    at: ['-w / 2 + wall + cw / 2', '-d / 2 + wall + cd / 2'],\n  })\n  .pattern(['c'], j.pattern.grid(j.dir('+X'), j.dir('+Y'), {\n    count: ['nx', 'ny'], step: ['cw + wall', 'cd + wall'],\n  }))\nreturn j.part('tray')\n  .params({\n    w: mm(160), d: mm(100), h: mm(30), wall: lw(4), floor: layer(8),\n    nx: 3, ny: 2, r_in: mm(6),\n    cw: '(w - (nx + 1) * wall) / nx',\n    cd: '(d - (ny + 1) * wall) / ny',\n  })\n  .configs({\n    pens: { nx: 4, ny: 1, d: mm(80) },\n    mixed: { nx: 3, ny: 2 },\n    parts: { nx: 4, ny: 3, h: mm(25) },\n  })\n  .config('pens')\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { r: 'r_in + wall' }), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(pockets.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })\n```\n\n**Figure 2.2.** The same script with `.config('pens')`: four compartments 37.75 mm wide in an 80 mm deep tray. The configuration changed three inputs; the expressions did the rest.\n\n```sketch\n// title: The tray's three configurations\n// Change .config('pens') to 'parts' or 'mixed', or delete the line.\nconst pockets = j.sketch('pockets', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('c', 'cw', 'cd', {\n    center: true, r: 'r_in',\n    at: ['-w / 2 + wall + cw / 2', '-d / 2 + wall + cd / 2'],\n  })\n  .pattern(['c'], j.pattern.grid(j.dir('+X'), j.dir('+Y'), {\n    count: ['nx', 'ny'], step: ['cw + wall', 'cd + wall'],\n  }))\nreturn j.part('tray')\n  .params({\n    w: mm(160), d: mm(100), h: mm(30), wall: lw(4), floor: layer(8),\n    nx: 3, ny: 2, r_in: mm(6),\n    cw: '(w - (nx + 1) * wall) / nx',\n    cd: '(d - (ny + 1) * wall) / ny',\n  })\n  .configs({\n    pens: { nx: 4, ny: 1, d: mm(80) },\n    mixed: { nx: 3, ny: 2 },\n    parts: { nx: 4, ny: 3, h: mm(25) },\n  })\n  .config('pens')\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd', { r: 'r_in + wall' }), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(pockets.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })\n```\n\nConfigurations are a finite set, which matters later: a compiler can build every row ahead of time, and a search can treat the configuration as one more discrete axis ([Chapter 10](lab:book?ch=10)). A table from a data file works the same way: [D-04](lab:preset/D-04) reads its cell sizes from `cells.csv` with `j.table(…)` and passes the result to `.configs`.\n\n> **Try it.** [Load D-03](lab:preset/D-03), [open Params](lab:show/params) and read the source column: `nx` and `ny` come from `configs.mixed`. Then [set w to 200 mm](lab:param/w=200) and watch `cw` follow.\n\n## 2.3 A change recomputes its downstream cone\n\nBecause the parameters form a graph and the features form a sequence, the compiler knows what each feature depends on. Each feature has a **cache key**, a hash of its type, the resolved values of the parameters it reads, the content hashes of the bodies it takes as input and the persistent names of the faces it refers to. Display names, colour and material are left out of the key: a material changes mass, not geometry. When a parameter changes, every feature whose key does not change is taken from the cache. What is recomputed is the parameter's **downstream cone**: everything reachable from it in the graph.\n\nThe box of [A-01](lab:preset/A-01) has two features: `body`, an extrude of height `h`, and `shell`, which reads `body`'s result and `wall`. Four compiles in a row, measured in Node on the build machine:\n\n| compile | volume | body | shell | total |\n|---|--:|---|---|--:|\n| defaults | 15,344.27 mm³ | 62.8 ms | 282.9 ms | 512 ms |\n| wall 2.5 mm | 18,836.63 mm³ | cached | 130.8 ms | 334 ms |\n| wall 2.5 mm, h 40 mm | 23,511.52 mm³ | 76.6 ms | 213.9 ms | 493 ms |\n| wall 2.5 mm again | 18,836.63 mm³ | cached | cached | 38 ms |\n\nChanging `wall` cannot reach `body`, so the extrude is reused. Changing `h` reaches both. Returning to an earlier value finds both features in the cache and costs only the mesh and the bookkeeping. The trace in the workbench shows these rows for every compile.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nreturn j.part('box')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(30), wall: mm(2), r: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(\n    j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n      .rect('outline', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 'r' }).profiles(),\n    'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { direction: 'in', id: 'shell' })\n```\n\n**Figure 2.3.** The box of A-01: `body` extrudes a 60 × 40 mm rounded rectangle by 30 mm, and `shell` hollows it with 2 mm walls. A change of `wall` rebuilds only `shell`; a change of `h` rebuilds both.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Which features does a change rebuild?\n// Change wall, Run, and open the Trace: body is marked cached.\nreturn j.part('box')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(30), wall: mm(2), r: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(\n    j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n      .rect('outline', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 'r' }).profiles(),\n    'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { direction: 'in', id: 'shell' })\n```\n\nThe cone is also why the timeline can be edited cheaply. `.suppress(id)` replaces a feature by the identity, `.rollback(id)` stops before it, and `.insertAfter(id, b => …)` adds a feature in the middle; in each case the features before the edit keep their keys and come from the cache. In the spec's terms the timeline is a monoid under concatenation and a suppressed feature is its identity element, and those two facts are exactly what prefix caching needs.\n\n> **Try it.** [Load A-01](lab:preset/A-01), [set wall to 2.5 mm](lab:param/wall=2.5) and [open the trace](lab:show/trace): `feature:body` is marked cached.\n\n## 2.4 Key points\n\n- Parameters form a directed acyclic graph of named expressions, evaluated level by level in topological order.\n- A cycle is reported as an error with its path; an unknown name or a dimension error with the column where it occurs.\n- Values come from layers (default, params, a configuration row, override, the Params view), and every value records the layer it came from and what it shadows.\n- Configurations are a finite set of named overrides; with none chosen, the middle row is active.\n- Each feature's cache key hashes what it reads, so a change recomputes only its downstream cone.\n- Suppressing, rolling back or inserting a feature leaves the features before it in the cache.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** Add a parameter `pitch: 'cw + wall'` to the tray and use it as the pattern's step. Which level is `pitch` at? Check with `why('pitch')`.\n\n> **Exercise.** Give the tray a fourth configuration, `wide: { w: mm(220), nx: 5 }`. With four rows, which one is active when none is chosen? Check the source column in Params.\n\n> **Exercise.** In A-01, change `r`, the corner radius, and predict which features the trace marks cached before you look.\n"},{"file":"03-sketches.md","text":"---\npart: I · Foundations\npresets: A-06, S-01\ndocs: solver\n---\n# Sketches as equations\n\nEvery solid in this book starts as a sketch: curves on a plane whose closed regions become profiles. A sketch can be a drawing, with every coordinate given, or a set of equations, with only the sizes that matter given and the rest solved. This chapter shows how jig decides which, how it counts the freedom a constrained sketch has left, how it tells a constraint that repeats another from one that contradicts it, and how a drag moves a sketch without breaking it. By the end you can predict a sketch's DOF from its entities and constraints, and read a conflict diagnostic as a list of things to check.\n\n## 3.1 An explicit sketch is a drawing\n\nThe bracket outline below is six lines and a circle, written as coordinates. With no constraint and no dimension, the sketch is **explicit**: every number is where the entity is, nothing can move, and there is nothing to solve. The compiler records the choice as a decision, `sketch.mode → explicit (no constraints: coordinates are fixed)`, and reports 0 degrees of freedom.\n\nWhat the sketch gives the solids that follow is its **profiles**: the bounded regions of the arrangement of its curves, nested even–odd. A region inside an odd number of loops is material and one inside an even number is a hole, so the circle inside the outline is a hole without being declared as one. The profile's area is\n\n$$\nA = 60 \\times 8 + 8 \\times 32 - \\pi \\cdot 2.2^2 = 736 - 15.21 = 720.79\\ \\mathrm{mm^2},\n$$\n\nand the compiler's profile reports 720.79 mm².\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\n// overlays: dims,dof\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .circle('hole', 2.2, { at: [40, 4] })\n```\n\n**Figure 3.1.** An explicit sketch: an L-shaped outline 60 × 40 mm with 8 mm legs and a 4.4 mm hole. Its one profile has an area of 720.79 mm²; the hole is a second loop, so it is subtracted without being declared.\n\n```sketch\n// title: An L-bracket outline as a drawing\n// Move the hole to [4, 20] and check the profile: it stays one region.\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .circle('hole', 2.2, { at: [40, 4] })\n```\n\nExplicit sketches are how most presets are written. In [S-01](lab:preset/S-01) every corner of the cable clip is an expression of five params, so a change to `desk` moves the jaws, with no solver involved. That is enough whenever you know how every point follows from the parameters.\n\n## 3.2 A constrained sketch is a system of equations\n\nSometimes you know relations, not coordinates: these lines are horizontal, this leg is 8 mm, that corner is fixed. Add one constraint or dimension and the sketch becomes **constrained**: the coordinates are now only starting positions, and the solver moves the entities until every relation holds.\n\nThe solver works with **parameters**: a point has 2 (x, y), a line 4 (its two ends), a circle 3, an arc 5. The six lines of the outline have $n = 24$. Each relation is one or more equations: the six corners where the polyline's lines meet are 12 coincidence equations; fixing the first corner is 2; each horizontal or vertical is 1; each length is 1. If $J$ is the Jacobian of all the equations, the number of **degrees of freedom** is\n\n$$\n\\mathrm{DOF} = n - \\operatorname{rank} J .\n$$\n\nWith the corner fixed, the six lines alternately horizontal and vertical, and the bottom 60 mm long, the rank is 21 and the DOF is 3. A count of equations would give the same here, but only because none repeats another; the next section shows why the rank is the quantity to count. Geometrically the three are the outline's height and the thickness of each leg: every other coordinate is now tied to one of them.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\n// overlays: dims,dof\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('p.1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('p.1'), j.c.vertical('p.2'), j.c.horizontal('p.3'),\n    j.c.vertical('p.4'), j.c.horizontal('p.5'), j.c.vertical('p.6'))\n  .dim('p.1', 60)\n```\n\n**Figure 3.2.** The outline constrained: $n = 24$, rank 21, 3 DOF. The DOF overlay marks the points that can still move; the warning `sketch.underConstrained: 3 DOF remain` lists them as p.2 (end.y), p.3, p.4, p.5 and p.6 (start.y).\n\n```sketch\n// title: An outline with 3 DOF left\n// Add .dim('p.6', 40) and watch the DOF pill drop to 2.\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('p.1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('p.1'), j.c.vertical('p.2'), j.c.horizontal('p.3'),\n    j.c.vertical('p.4'), j.c.horizontal('p.5'), j.c.vertical('p.6'))\n  .dim('p.1', 60)\n```\n\nThree more dimensions, `p.6` 40 mm, `p.2` 8 mm and `p.5` 8 mm, bring the rank to 24 and the status to `fully`. An under-constrained sketch is a warning, not an error: it solves, and the free parameters stay where the starting positions put them. `.strict()` turns the warning into an error for a sketch that must not drift.\n\n## 3.3 Redundant and conflicting constraints\n\nA constraint whose equations add no rank is one of two things, and the difference matters.\n\nA **redundant** constraint agrees with the others. In the fully constrained outline, `parallel(p.1, p.3)` follows from `horizontal(p.1)` and `horizontal(p.3)`: its row of $J$ lies in the span of theirs. Counting equations would say the sketch now has −1 DOF; the rank says 0, which is right. The compiler finds such rows incrementally, drops them, and says why:\n\n```text\nsketch.redundant: parallel(p.1, p.3) is implied by horizontal×2; dropped\nfix: remove it, or keep it as a driven (reference) constraint\n```\n\nA **conflicting** constraint disagrees: no point satisfies all the equations. Give the middle line `p.3` a length of 50 mm while the others make it 60 − 8 = 52 mm, and the system has no solution. \"Over-constrained\" alone would leave the person searching, so the compiler finds a **minimal conflicting set**: constraints that cannot all hold, such that removing any one of them leaves the rest solvable. It does this with a deletion filter, dropping each constraint in turn and keeping it out when the rest still conflict:\n\n```text\nsketch.overConstrained: no solution; minimal conflicting set:\nvertical(p.2) · vertical(p.4) · vertical(p.6) · length(p.1) = 60 mm\n· length(p.5) = 8 mm · length(p.3) = 50 mm\nfix: drop one of the 6; e.g. make dim(p.3) driven (it measures 52 mm)\n```\n\nThe six are exactly the constraints involved in the arithmetic 60 − 8 = 52: the two horizontal lengths, the thickness of the upright, and the three verticals that make those lengths add along x. A **driven** dimension, `.dim('p.3', null, { driven: true })`, measures instead of constraining; it reads 52 mm and leaves the rank alone.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\n// overlays: dims,dof\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('p.1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('p.1'), j.c.vertical('p.2'), j.c.horizontal('p.3'),\n    j.c.vertical('p.4'), j.c.horizontal('p.5'), j.c.vertical('p.6'))\n  .dim('p.1', 60).dim('p.6', 40).dim('p.2', 8).dim('p.5', 8)\n  .dim('p.3', null, { driven: true })\n```\n\n**Figure 3.3.** Fully constrained: rank 24 of 24, 0 DOF, and a driven dimension on `p.3` that measures 52 mm. Replacing the driven dimension by `.dim('p.3', 50)` makes the six-constraint conflict of the text.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A conflict to find and fix\n// expect: sketch.overConstrained\n// This sketch does not solve. Read the diagnostic, then make dim p.3 driven.\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('p.1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('p.1'), j.c.vertical('p.2'), j.c.horizontal('p.3'),\n    j.c.vertical('p.4'), j.c.horizontal('p.5'), j.c.vertical('p.6'))\n  .dim('p.1', 60).dim('p.6', 40).dim('p.2', 8).dim('p.5', 8)\n  .dim('p.3', 50)\n```\n\n## 3.4 Clusters, solving and dragging\n\nBefore solving, the compiler splits the system into **clusters**: the connected components of the graph whose nodes are parameters and whose edges are equations. Two groups of entities that no constraint links are solved separately. Add the hole back to the constrained outline, fix its centre and give it a 4.4 mm diameter, and the sketch has $n = 27$, rank 27, and two clusters: the six lines, and the circle. The decision list records one solver run per cluster, `outline/cluster0 solver → dogleg` and `outline/cluster1 solver → dogleg`.\n\nEach cluster is solved by Powell's DogLeg method: a trust-region step between Gauss–Newton (the minimum-norm solution of the linearised equations, through a rank-revealing QR factorisation) and steepest descent, with Levenberg–Marquardt as the fallback. This follows the decomposition-plan approach of Hoffmann, Lomonosov and Sitharam (2001) in a simple form: split first, then solve small systems. The point of splitting is locality. A **drag** moves one point to where the pointer is, keeps every constraint, and changes as little else as possible; it re-solves only the cluster that contains the point, starting from the current solution.\n\nIn the 3-DOF outline of Figure 3.2, dragging the top of the upright, `p.4.end`, to (14, 46) gives:\n\n| line | before | after |\n|---|---|---|\n| p.1 | (0, 0) → (60, 0) | (0, 0) → (60, 0) |\n| p.3 | (60, 8) → (8, 8) | (60, 8) → (14, 8) |\n| p.4 | (8, 8) → (8, 40) | (14, 8) → (14, 46) |\n| p.5 | (8, 40) → (0, 40) | (14, 46) → (0, 46) |\n\nThe fixed, dimensioned bottom stays; the upright and the top follow the pointer; the foot keeps its 8 mm height because nothing pulled it. The sketch still has 3 DOF: a drag moves geometry, it does not add constraints.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\n// overlays: dims,dof\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .circle('hole', 2.2, { at: [40, 4] })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('p.1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('p.1'), j.c.vertical('p.2'), j.c.horizontal('p.3'),\n    j.c.vertical('p.4'), j.c.horizontal('p.5'), j.c.vertical('p.6'),\n    j.c.fix('hole.center'))\n  .dim('p.1', 60).dim('p.6', 40).dim('p.2', 8).dim('p.5', 8)\n  .dim('hole', 4.4)\n```\n\n**Figure 3.4.** Two clusters: the outline (24 parameters) and the hole (3), solved separately. Nothing links them, so a drag on one cannot move the other; to keep the hole in the foot when the foot moves, constrain its centre to the foot instead of fixing it.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Drag the upright\n// Open it, then drag the top of the upright on the sheet: the bottom stays.\nreturn j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n  .constrain(\n    j.c.fix('p.1.start'),\n    j.c.horizontal('p.1'), j.c.vertical('p.2'), j.c.horizontal('p.3'),\n    j.c.vertical('p.4'), j.c.horizontal('p.5'), j.c.vertical('p.6'))\n  .dim('p.1', 60)\n```\n\n> **Try it.** Open the last sketch in the workbench. Its free points are drawn in blue on the sheet; drag the top corner of the upright, and then add `.dim('p.6', 40)` and Run: that corner can now move only sideways.\n\n## 3.5 Key points\n\n- A sketch with no constraints and no dimensions is explicit: its coordinates are fixed and its DOF is 0.\n- Any constraint or dimension makes the sketch constrained, and its coordinates become starting positions.\n- Profiles are the bounded regions of the sketch's curves, nested even–odd, so a loop inside a loop is a hole.\n- DOF is the number of parameters minus the rank of the constraint Jacobian, not minus the number of equations.\n- A redundant constraint is dropped with a warning; a conflict is reported with a minimal conflicting set and a driven dimension to try.\n- The solver splits a sketch into independent clusters and solves each with DogLeg, falling back to Levenberg–Marquardt.\n- A drag re-solves one cluster from the current solution and adds no constraints.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** A circle has 3 parameters. How many DOF does a sketch of two circles have after `j.c.concentric('c1', 'c2')` and `.dim('c1', 10)`? Write it, and check the count against the DOF pill.\n\n> **Exercise.** In the conflicting sketch, remove `j.c.vertical('p.4')` instead of making `p.3` driven. Does it solve? What shape does the upright take, and why is the DOF still 0?\n\n> **Exercise.** Write the outline of [A-06](lab:preset/A-06)'s plate (80 × 50 mm, 4 mm corners) as a constrained sketch: a rectangle helper, its centre fixed at the origin, its width and height as dimensions. Check that it reports `fully`.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- Hoffmann, Lomonosov and Sitharam, *Decomposition plans for geometric constraint systems* (2001): splitting a constraint system into small solvable pieces, the idea behind clusters.\n- [How sketches are solved](lab:doc?id=solver): the same material as a reference page.\n"},{"file":"04-solids-from-profiles.md","text":"---\npart: II · Solids\npartAbout: From profiles to solids, the algebra that combines them, the names their faces carry, and the symmetries that copy them.\npresets: A-06, S-04\ndocs: reference\n---\n# Solids from profiles\n\nMost printed parts are a profile moved through space: pushed straight up, turned about an axis, or carried along a curve. This chapter derives the volume of each kind of sweep from the profile alone, with two theorems older than calculus, and checks the kernel against them to many digits. The formulas matter for more than checking: a closed form for volume is a closed form for mass, which lets the compiler answer \"how heavy at this height?\" without building the solid ([Chapter 10](lab:book?ch=10)). By the end you can predict the volume of an extrude, a tapered extrude, a revolve and a helical sweep before compiling it.\n\n## 4.1 Extrude: Cavalieri's principle\n\nAn **extrude** moves a planar profile along the normal of its plane by a distance $h$. Every section of the result parallel to the sketch plane is a copy of the profile, so by **Cavalieri's principle**, two solids whose sections at every height have equal areas have equal volumes, the volume is the profile's area times the height:\n\n$$\nV = A\\,h .\n$$\n\nThe shape of the profile does not enter, only its area, and an inner loop subtracts its area. The plate below is 80 × 50 mm with 4 mm corner radii and four holes of radius 1.7 mm, extruded 4 mm. Its profile's area is\n\n$$\nA = 80 \\times 50 - 4(1 - \\tfrac{\\pi}{4})\\,4^2 - 4\\pi \\cdot 1.7^2 = 4000 - 13.73 - 36.32 = 3949.95\\ \\mathrm{mm^2},\n$$\n\nthe sketch reports 3,949.9487 mm², and the kernel's volume is 15,799.794686 mm³, which is 4 × 3,949.948671 to within $10^{-12}$ relative. The kernel computes its volume by integrating over the faces of the solid it built; the sketch computes its area from the curves. Two independent routes agree.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst outline = j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 80, 50, { center: true, r: 4 })\n  .circle('h1', 1.7, { at: [32, 17] })\n  .pattern(['h1'],\n    j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X).times(j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.Y)))\nreturn j.part('plate')\n  .material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .extrude(outline.profiles(), mm(4), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n**Figure 4.1.** An 80 × 50 × 4 mm plate with four M3 clearance holes, from one sketch. The holes are inner loops of the one profile, so the extrude subtracts them: $V = 3949.95 \\times 4 = 15{,}799.79$ mm³, 20.07 g in PETG.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Cavalieri: volume is area times height\n// Change the radius of h1 or the 4 mm corners; the volume follows A·h.\nconst outline = j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 80, 50, { center: true, r: 4 })\n  .circle('h1', 1.7, { at: [32, 17] })\n  .pattern(['h1'],\n    j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X).times(j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.Y)))\nreturn j.part('plate')\n  .material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .extrude(outline.profiles(), mm(4), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\nThe linearity in $h$ is the property the compiler uses: two compiles at different heights determine the mass at every height, $m(h) = \\rho A h$, with no third compile ([Chapter 10](lab:book?ch=10)).\n\n## 4.2 Tapered extrude: the frustum\n\nAn extrude with a **taper** offsets the profile inwards (negative angle) or outwards as it rises, so the faces lean by the taper angle. For a square of side $s_0$ and a taper of $-\\alpha$ the side at height $z$ is $s(z) = s_0 - 2z\\tan\\alpha$, and the section areas grow quadratically. Integrating $A(z) = s(z)^2$ from 0 to $h$ gives the **frustum** formula, valid for any pair of similar sections:\n\n$$\nV = \\frac{h}{3}(A_0 + A_1 + \\sqrt{A_0 A_1}).\n$$\n\nA 30 mm square extruded 20 mm with a taper of −10° has a top side of $30 - 40\\tan 10° = 22.947$ mm, so $A_1 = 526.56$ mm² and\n\n$$\nV = \\frac{20}{3}\\,(900 + 526.56 + 688.41) = 14{,}099.8\\ \\mathrm{mm^3}.\n$$\n\nThe kernel gives 14,099.79 mm³. A bare number in the taper slot is degrees, so `taper: -10` and `taper: deg(-10)` compile to the same solid.\n\n```figure\n// view: front\nconst sq = j.sketch('sq', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 30, 30, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('frustum')\n  .extrude(sq.profiles(), mm(20), { taper: deg(-10), id: 'f' })\n```\n\n**Figure 4.2.** A 30 mm square extruded 20 mm with a −10° taper, front elevation: the sides lean in by 10°, the top is 22.95 mm across, and the volume is the frustum's, 14,099.8 mm³.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A tapered extrude and the frustum law\n// Try taper: deg(10) (it widens), or a circle instead of the square.\nconst sq = j.sketch('sq', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 30, 30, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('frustum')\n  .extrude(sq.profiles(), mm(20), { taper: deg(-10), id: 'f' })\n```\n\nTaper is how a printed part gets a draft for easy removal from a mould, and how a peg gets a lead-in. Fit is the subject of [Chapter 9](lab:book?ch=9); here the point is that the volume is still a closed form in the profile.\n\n## 4.3 Revolve: Pappus's theorem\n\nA **revolve** turns a planar profile about an axis in its plane. **Pappus's centroid theorem** (Pappus–Guldinus) gives its volume: the profile's area times the distance its centroid travels,\n\n$$\nV = \\theta\\,r̄\\,A ,\n$$\n\nwhere $r̄$ is the distance from the axis to the profile's centroid and $\\theta$ the angle turned. The profile must stay on one side of the axis; a profile that crosses it would sweep some of its area twice.\n\nA 4 × 10 mm rectangle centred 25 mm from the Z axis, turned a full turn, makes a ring of volume $2\\pi \\cdot 25 \\cdot 40 = 6283.185307$ mm³; the kernel gives 6,283.185307 mm³, equal to the printed digits. A quarter turn gives a quarter, 1,570.80 mm³. In [A-06](lab:preset/A-06) a 3 × 8 mm section with its centroid 18 mm from Z is revolved onto the plate of Figure 4.1. The ring's volume is\n\n$$\nV = 2\\pi \\cdot 18 \\cdot 24 = 2714.34\\ \\mathrm{mm^3} ,\n$$\n\nand the whole part is 15,799.79 + 2,714.34 = 18,514.13 mm³, which is the compiled volume. The ring sits on the plate's top face, touches it, and so joins it: the `op` decision reads `ring → join (touches body1)`.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst outline = j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 4 })\n  .circle('h1', 1.7, { at: [32, 17] })\n  .pattern(['h1'],\n    j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X).times(j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.Y)))\nconst section = j.sketch('sec', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .rect('s', 'ring_t', 'ring_h', { at: ['ring_r - ring_t / 2', 't'] })\nreturn j.part('plate')\n  .params({ w: mm(80), d: mm(50), t: mm(4),\n    ring_r: mm(18), ring_t: mm(3), ring_h: mm(8) })\n  .material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .extrude(outline.profiles(), 't', { id: 'body' })\n  .revolve(section.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'ring' })\n```\n\n**Figure 4.3.** The part of A-06: the plate of Figure 4.1 and a ring revolved from a 3 × 8 mm section whose centroid is 18 mm from the axis. Pappus gives the ring 2,714.34 mm³; the total, 18,514.13 mm³, is the sum because the ring only touches the plate.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Pappus: volume is area times the centroid's path\n// Change ring_r: the ring's volume grows in proportion. Try turn(0.5).\nconst outline = j.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 4 })\n  .circle('h1', 1.7, { at: [32, 17] })\n  .pattern(['h1'],\n    j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X).times(j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.Y)))\nconst section = j.sketch('sec', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .rect('s', 'ring_t', 'ring_h', { at: ['ring_r - ring_t / 2', 't'] })\nreturn j.part('plate')\n  .params({ w: mm(80), d: mm(50), t: mm(4),\n    ring_r: mm(18), ring_t: mm(3), ring_h: mm(8) })\n  .material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .extrude(outline.profiles(), 't', { id: 'body' })\n  .revolve(section.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'ring' })\n```\n\n> **Careful.** `j.plane.XZ` follows replicad's convention: its normal is −Y and its sketch y axis is world +Z. A section drawn on XZ at sketch coordinates (x, y) lies at world (x, 0, y), which is why `['ring_r - ring_t / 2', 't']` puts the ring's foot on the plate's top face.\n\n## 4.4 Sweep: frames along a curve\n\nA **sweep** carries a profile along a path. For a straight path it is an extrude; for a circle about an axis, a revolve. Along a general curve the profile needs a **frame** at every point of the path, an orientation to be carried in, and the choice is not obvious. The Frenet frame, built from the curve's tangent and curvature, is undefined where the curve is straight and flips where the curvature changes sign, which twists the swept solid. jig's default is a **rotation-minimising frame**, computed by the double-reflection method of Wang, Jüttler, Zheng and Liu (2008): each step reflects the previous frame twice so that it turns no more about the tangent than the curve forces it to.\n\nFor a volume, the frame decides only how the profile meets the path. A circle of radius 1 mm drawn on the XZ plane at (10, 0) and swept along a helix of radius 10 mm, pitch 4 mm and 3 turns makes a coil. The helix has length $L = 3\\sqrt{(2\\pi \\cdot 10)^2 + 4^2} = 188.88$ mm, and if the profile were perpendicular to the path the volume would be $\\pi \\cdot 1^2 \\cdot L = 593.38$ mm³. It is not perpendicular: the helix climbs at $\\alpha = \\arctan(4 / 20\\pi) = 3.64°$, so the section normal to the path is the profile's area times $\\cos\\alpha$, and\n\n$$\nV = A\\,L\\cos\\alpha = 593.38 \\times 0.99798 = 592.18\\ \\mathrm{mm^3} .\n$$\n\nThe kernel gives 592.18 mm³. The 0.2 % between the naive and the true value is the tilt of the profile, not an approximation error.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst wire = j.sketch('c', { on: j.plane.XZ }).circle('w', 1, { at: [10, 0] })\nconst path = j.sketch3d('path')\n  .helix('h', { pitch: mm(4), turns: 3, r: mm(10) })\nreturn j.part('coil').sweep(wire.profiles(), path, { id: 'wire' })\n```\n\n**Figure 4.4.** A 2 mm wire swept along a helix of radius 10 mm, pitch 4 mm, 3 turns, with a rotation-minimising frame. Its volume is $A\\,L\\cos\\alpha$ = 592.18 mm³, because the profile on the XZ plane meets the helix at its climb angle of 3.64°.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A helical sweep\n// Raise the pitch to 12 mm: the climb angle grows and cos α shrinks.\nconst wire = j.sketch('c', { on: j.plane.XZ }).circle('w', 1, { at: [10, 0] })\nconst path = j.sketch3d('path')\n  .helix('h', { pitch: mm(4), turns: 3, r: mm(10) })\nreturn j.part('coil').sweep(wire.profiles(), path, { id: 'wire' })\n```\n\nPrinted threads are sweeps of this kind: `j.lib.thread('M8x1.25', …)` sweeps the ISO tooth profile along a helix with the same frame.\n\n## 4.5 The closed forms as tests\n\nThe two theorems are not only explanations; the compiler runs them as **laws**, property tests over random inputs. `extrude.volume` draws random profiles and heights and checks $V = A h$; `revolve.volume` checks $V = \\theta r̄ A$. Each law says what it pays for: the first justifies the closed form for mass that search uses, the second is a test of the kernel's revolve. Run in this build with `j.laws.check(j.laws.suite, { n: 8, seed: 1 })`, both pass on all eight samples, together with `brep.eulerPoincare`, `compile.deterministic` and `shape.boolean` from the next chapter. A kernel change that broke either theorem would fail here before it reached a part.\n\n## 4.6 Key points\n\n- An extrude's volume is its profile's area times its height (Cavalieri); inner loops subtract their area.\n- A tapered extrude of similar sections has the frustum's volume, $\\tfrac{h}{3}(A_0 + A_1 + \\sqrt{A_0 A_1})$.\n- A revolve's volume is the profile's area times the length of its centroid's path, $\\thetar̄ A$ (Pappus–Guldinus).\n- A sweep needs a frame along its path; jig's default is the rotation-minimising frame of Wang et al., which neither flips nor twists.\n- A swept profile that meets its path at an angle $\\alpha$ contributes $A\\cos\\alpha$ per unit length.\n- The kernel's volumes match these closed forms to within $10^{-12}$ relative for the extrude and to the printed digits for the revolve, and the compiler checks them as laws.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** Predict the volume of a 20 × 20 mm square revolved a half turn about an axis 30 mm from its nearest side. Compile it with a revolve and compare.\n\n> **Exercise.** Make the coil's profile perpendicular to the path by drawing it on a plane through (10, 0, 0) normal to the helix's starting tangent (`j.plane.angle(j.plane.XZ, j.axis.X, …)`). What volume do you expect, and what does the kernel give?\n\n> **Exercise.** [S-04](lab:preset/S-04) is three extrudes that join because they overlap by 1 mm. Compute the volume of each extrude from its sketch, add them, and explain the difference from the compiled volume.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- Wang, Jüttler, Zheng and Liu, *Computation of rotation minimizing frames*, ACM Transactions on Graphics 27(1), 2008: the double-reflection method used for sweeps and threads.\n- [The reference](lab:reference): the extrude extents (`symmetric`, `two`, `through`, `to`) and sweep frames.\n"},{"file":"05-booleans.md","text":"---\npart: II · Solids\npresets: S-04, A-06\ndocs: reference\n---\n# Booleans and valid solids\n\nUnion, intersection and difference are how solids are combined: a boss is joined to a plate, a pocket is cut from a block, a die is a cube intersected with a ball. This chapter explains why the ordinary set operations are the wrong ones for solids and what jig uses instead, gives the volume identity that every boolean must satisfy, and introduces the counting formula that tells a valid solid from a broken one. By the end you can predict the volume of a boolean, compute a solid's genus from its face, edge and vertex counts, and read the diagnostics that report an operation the kernel cannot complete.\n\n## 5.1 Regularised booleans\n\nA solid is a set of points, so the set operations apply. They apply badly. Two 20 × 20 × 10 mm boxes that share a face have, as point sets, an intersection that is that face: a set with area but no volume, which no printer can make. Their difference keeps a face's worth of boundary points on one side and not the other. Ordinary set operations produce such **dangling** pieces whenever solids touch, and touching is the usual case in modelling.\n\nRequicha (1980) defined the fix. The **regularisation** of a set is the closure of its interior: take the interior (which drops every face, edge or point with no volume around it) and add back its boundary. The **regularised** operations are the ordinary ones followed by regularisation:\n\n$$\nA ∪* B = cl(int(A \\cup B)), \\quad A ∩* B = cl(int(A \\cap B)), \\quad A −* B = cl(int(A − B)) ,\n$$\n\nwhere int is the interior and cl the closure.\n\nUnder them, the two touching boxes intersect in the empty set, and their union is one 40 × 20 × 10 mm box with 6 faces, not two boxes with a shared face inside. The kernel confirms it: the union has 8 vertices, 12 edges and 6 faces, and a volume of 8,000 mm³. Regularised sets form a Boolean algebra, so unions reorder and regroup freely, which lets the compiler turn a chain of unions into one n-ary union with fewer slivers.\n\nThe die below is the classic example: a cube of side 20 mm intersected with a ball of radius 13 mm, minus three rods of radius 5 mm along the axes.\n\n```js\nconst cube = j.box(mm(20), mm(20), mm(20), { center: true })\nconst ball = j.sphere(mm(13))\nconst rod = j.cyl(mm(5), mm(40), { center: true })\nconst rods = rod\n  .union(rod.moved(j.tx.rotate(j.axis.X, deg(90))))\n  .union(rod.moved(j.tx.rotate(j.axis.Y, deg(90))))\n```\n\nThe cube is 8,000 mm³ and the ball 9,202.77 mm³; their intersection is 7,167.02 mm³, and removing the rods leaves 3,868.84 mm³.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst cube = j.box(mm(20), mm(20), mm(20), { center: true })\nconst ball = j.sphere(mm(13))\nconst rod = j.cyl(mm(5), mm(40), { center: true })\nconst rods = rod\n  .union(rod.moved(j.tx.rotate(j.axis.X, deg(90))))\n  .union(rod.moved(j.tx.rotate(j.axis.Y, deg(90))))\nreturn j.part('die')\n  .add(cube.intersect(ball).subtract(rods), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n**Figure 5.1.** Cube ∩ ball − rods: a 20 mm cube with its corners rounded by a 13 mm ball and three 10 mm bores along the axes. Volume 3,868.84 mm³; a single shell of genus 5.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A die from three primitives\n// Grow the ball to mm(14.14): the corners almost return. Shrink the rods.\nconst cube = j.box(mm(20), mm(20), mm(20), { center: true })\nconst ball = j.sphere(mm(13))\nconst rod = j.cyl(mm(5), mm(40), { center: true })\nconst rods = rod\n  .union(rod.moved(j.tx.rotate(j.axis.X, deg(90))))\n  .union(rod.moved(j.tx.rotate(j.axis.Y, deg(90))))\nreturn j.part('die')\n  .add(cube.intersect(ball).subtract(rods), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n## 5.2 Inclusion–exclusion\n\nVolume is a measure, and a measure satisfies **inclusion–exclusion**:\n\n$$\n\\operatorname{vol}(A \\cup B) = \\operatorname{vol}A + \\operatorname{vol}B - \\operatorname{vol}(A \\cap B) .\n$$\n\nThe identity holds for regularised operations because they differ from the ordinary ones only by sets of zero volume. It is a check that costs three extra booleans and catches a kernel that lost or doubled material.\n\nTake a 40 × 30 × 20 mm box centred at the origin, $A$, and a cylinder of radius 12 mm and length 50 mm centred on the box's right face, $B$. The kernel measures\n\n| quantity | volume |\n|---|--:|\n| $\\operatorname{vol}A$ | 24,000 mm³ |\n| $\\operatorname{vol}B$ | 22,619.467106 mm³ |\n| $\\operatorname{vol}(A \\cap B)$ | 4,523.893421 mm³ |\n| $\\operatorname{vol}A + \\operatorname{vol}B - \\operatorname{vol}(A \\cap B)$ | 42,095.573685 mm³ |\n| $\\operatorname{vol}(A \\cup B)$ | 42,095.573685 mm³ |\n\nand the last two rows agree to every digit printed. The intersection is half of a 24 mm disc 20 mm thick, $\\pi \\cdot 12^2 \\cdot 20 / 2 = 4523.89$ mm³, because the cylinder's axis lies on the box's face and the box is 20 mm tall. The die obeys the same identity for a difference: $\\operatorname{vol}(C - R) = \\operatorname{vol}C - \\operatorname{vol}(C \\cap R)$, and $7167.02 - 3298.18 = 3868.84$ mm³, where $C$ is cube ∩ ball and $R$ the rods.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst A = j.box(mm(40), mm(30), mm(20), { center: true })\nconst B = j.cyl(mm(12), mm(50), { center: true }).moved(j.tx.move(20, 0, 0))\nreturn j.part('union').add(A.union(B), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n**Figure 5.2.** $A \\cup B$: a 40 × 30 × 20 mm box and a 24 mm cylinder 50 mm long whose axis lies on the box's right face. Its volume, 42,095.57 mm³, equals 24,000 + 22,619.47 − 4,523.89.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Inclusion–exclusion on volume\n// Return A.intersect(B) or A.subtract(B) instead and add up the volumes.\nconst A = j.box(mm(40), mm(30), mm(20), { center: true })\nconst B = j.cyl(mm(12), mm(50), { center: true }).moved(j.tx.move(20, 0, 0))\nreturn j.part('union').add(A.union(B), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\nThe same identity holds for areas of regions, and the compiler's `shape.boolean` law tests both on random inputs.\n\n## 5.3 Boundary representation and Euler–Poincaré\n\nThe kernel does not store a solid as a set of points. It stores a **boundary representation** (B-rep): the faces that bound it, each a piece of a surface; the edges where faces meet; the vertices where edges end; and the **loops** that bound each face, one outer loop and one inner loop per hole in the face. A solid has one or more **shells**, closed connected boundaries, and a **genus**, its number of through-holes.\n\nThese counts are not independent. For any valid solid the **Euler–Poincaré formula** holds (Mäntylä, 1988):\n\n$$\nV - E + F - (L - F) - 2(S - G) = 0 ,\n$$\n\nwith $V$ vertices, $E$ edges, $F$ faces, $L$ loops, $S$ shells and $G$ the genus. $L - F$ is the number of inner loops. A box has $V = 8$, $E = 12$, $F = 6$, $L = 6$, $S = 1$, $G = 0$: $8 - 12 + 6 - 0 - 2 = 0$. The compiler computes the left-hand side after every feature and reports it as `residual` in the body's topology; a nonzero residual means the kernel produced something that is not a solid.\n\nRun backwards, the formula gives the genus from counts the kernel already has. For the plate below, 170 × 100 × 5 mm with 40 clearance holes, the compiler reports $V = 88$, $E = 132$, $F = 46$, $L = 126$, $S = 1$:\n\n$$\nG = S - \\tfrac{1}{2}(V - E + F - (L - F)) = 1 - \\tfrac{1}{2}(88 - 132 + 46 - 80) = 1 + 39 = 40 ,\n$$\n\none through-hole per bore. The counts themselves follow from how OpenCascade builds a bore: a cylindrical face with a seam edge, bounded by one loop, and a circle at each end with one vertex on the seam. Each bore adds 2 vertices, 3 edges, 1 face and 3 loops (its own, and an inner loop in the top and the bottom face). The die of Figure 5.1 has $V = 30$, $E = 42$, $F = 13$, $L = 22$, $S = 1$, and the formula gives $G = 5$: three bores that cross at the centre leave five independent through-holes.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nlet pts = j.sketch('pts', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(5)) })\nfor (let i = 0; i < 8; i++) {\n  for (let k = 0; k < 5; k++) {\n    pts = pts.point(`p${i}_${k}`, [-70 + i * 20, -40 + k * 20])\n  }\n}\nreturn j.part('perf')\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(170, 100), mm(5), { id: 'body' })\n  .hole(pts, j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' }), { id: 'holes' })\n```\n\n**Figure 5.3.** A 170 × 100 × 5 mm plate with 40 M3 clearance holes, 3.5 mm each, from one hole feature. $V - E + F - (L - F) - 2(S - G) = 88 - 132 + 46 - 80 - 2(1 - 40) = 0$, so the genus is 40.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Genus from counting\n// Change the 8 × 5 grid and read V, E, F, L in the IR at level L4.\nlet pts = j.sketch('pts', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(5)) })\nfor (let i = 0; i < 8; i++) {\n  for (let k = 0; k < 5; k++) {\n    pts = pts.point(`p${i}_${k}`, [-70 + i * 20, -40 + k * 20])\n  }\n}\nreturn j.part('perf')\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(170, 100), mm(5), { id: 'body' })\n  .hole(pts, j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' }), { id: 'holes' })\n```\n\n> **Try it.** Open the sketch, [show the IR](lab:show/ir) and find the body at level L4: it prints the six counts and a ✓ for the Euler–Poincaré check.\n\n## 5.4 Operations that cannot produce a valid solid\n\nSome requests have no valid answer, and the compiler says which and by how much rather than returning a broken body.\n\nA **shell** offsets faces inwards by a wall thickness. Shell a 60 × 40 × 30 mm block by 25 mm and the inner offset of the 40 mm sides crosses itself: there is no solid with that wall. The diagnostic is `brep.invalid: shell of body1 by 25 mm gives an invalid solid`, with the fix `wall < 15 mm (half the smallest dimension)`.\n\nA **fillet** replaces an edge by a rolling-ball blend of radius $r$. On a 50 × 30 × 4 mm plate, the four vertical edges can take at most a radius of half the short side, where the two blends on each end meet. Ask for 20 mm and the compiler searches for the largest radius that works and reports it: `fillet.tooLarge: r = 20 mm is too large for 4 edges of body1; the largest that works is about 14.844 mm`. The reported value comes from a search, a little below the geometric limit of 15 mm.\n\nA **join** of solids that touch only along an edge or at a point has no regularised union with one shell: two 20 × 20 × 10 mm boxes meeting at a vertical edge stay two bodies, and the compiler warns `boolean.nonManifold: join left 2 solids that only touch along an edge or a point`, with the fix to overlap them by at least one line width. [S-04](lab:preset/S-04) overlaps its spike and paddle by 1 mm for exactly this reason.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\nconst plate = j.sketch('p', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 50, 30, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('mount')\n  .extrude(plate.profiles(), mm(4), { id: 'plate' })\n  .fillet(j.feat('plate').sideFaces().edges('|Z'), mm(14), { id: 'round' })\n```\n\n**Figure 5.4.** A 50 × 30 × 4 mm plate with its vertical edges filleted at 14 mm, in plan: near the largest radius that works. The volume is $6000 - 4(1 - \\pi/4) \\cdot 14^2 \\cdot 4 = 5327.0$ mm³, and the body still satisfies Euler–Poincaré with $V = 16$, $E = 24$, $F = 10$.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A fillet that is too large\n// expect: fillet.tooLarge\n// This asks for 20 mm. Read the diagnostic, then use the radius it reports.\nconst plate = j.sketch('p', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 50, 30, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('mount')\n  .extrude(plate.profiles(), mm(4), { id: 'plate' })\n  .fillet(j.feat('plate').sideFaces().edges('|Z'), mm(20), { id: 'round' })\n```\n\n## 5.5 Key points\n\n- Ordinary set operations leave faces and edges with no volume around them; regularised operations (Requicha) take the closure of the interior and do not.\n- Regularised sets form a Boolean algebra, so unions can be reordered and merged into one n-ary operation.\n- Volume satisfies inclusion–exclusion, $\\operatorname{vol}(A \\cup B) = \\operatorname{vol}A + \\operatorname{vol}B - \\operatorname{vol}(A \\cap B)$; the kernel matches it to every printed digit.\n- A B-rep stores faces, edges, vertices, loops and shells; a valid solid satisfies $V - E + F - (L - F) - 2(S - G) = 0$.\n- The formula gives the genus from the counts: 40 for a plate with 40 holes, 5 for the die.\n- Requests with no valid solid are diagnostics with a measured limit: `brep.invalid`, `fillet.tooLarge`, `boolean.nonManifold`.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** Compute $V$, $E$, $F$, $L$ for a box with one round through-hole, using the bore counts of Section 5.3, and check that the formula gives $G = 1$. Then compile it and compare with the IR.\n\n> **Exercise.** Verify inclusion–exclusion for the die: compute vol(cube), vol(ball), vol(cube ∪ ball) and vol(cube ∩ ball) with `.volume()` inside a script, and return the part whose volume you want to see.\n\n> **Exercise.** Two boxes that touch along an edge stay two bodies. Move one by 0.45 mm so they overlap by a line width, and read how many bodies and faces the join gives.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- Requicha, *Representations for rigid solids: theory, methods, and systems*, ACM Computing Surveys 12(4), 1980: regularised sets and the properties a solid representation must have.\n- Mäntylä, *An Introduction to Solid Modeling*, 1988: boundary representations, Euler operators and the Euler–Poincaré formula used here.\n"},{"file":"06-naming.md","text":"---\npart: II · Solids\npresets: A-01, D-03\ndocs: naming\n---\n# Naming what you made\n\nA fillet must say which edges to round and a hole which face to start from. In a parametric model those references are written once and must keep meaning the same thing after every edit, but the kernel numbers faces in the order it happened to build them, and that order changes. This chapter shows how jig names every face and edge by its history instead, how selectors resolve through those names, where a query by position means something different from a reference by history, and what the compiler reports when a reference breaks. By the end you can read any face name back to the sketch entity it came from, and choose a selector that survives the edits you expect.\n\n## 6.1 A name is a history\n\nEvery face the compiler makes gets a **persistent name** of the form `<feature>/<role>[<source>]`. The feature is the id of the feature that created the face; the role is what the face is to that feature; the source is what it was made from. The box of [A-01](lab:preset/A-01) has 19 faces. Some of them:\n\n| name | made by | from |\n|---|---|---|\n| `body/start` | the extrude `body` | its near cap, on the sketch plane |\n| `body/end` | the extrude `body` | its far cap; after the shell, the rim |\n| `body/side[base.outline.bottom]` | the extrude `body` | the bottom line of the rectangle `outline` in sketch `base` |\n| `body/side[base.outline.br]` | the extrude `body` | the rectangle's bottom-right corner arc |\n| `shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]` | the shell `shell` | the face `body/side[base.outline.bottom]`, offset inwards |\n\nA name can be read from the inside out. `shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]` is the inner face the shell offset from the side the extrude swept from the bottom edge of the outline: the inside of the front wall. Edges are named by the two faces they separate, `a ∩ b`, and a fillet or chamfer face is named by the edge it replaced: `corners/face[body/side[base.r.bottom] ∩ body/side[base.r.left]]`.\n\nThe record of which feature made which face from what is the **genealogy**. Each feature reports its faces as created, modified or deleted as it runs, and the compiler composes the reports into names. The approach, naming faces by the history of features rather than by geometry or kernel index, is Kripac's (1997).\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// select: shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]\nreturn j.part('box')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(30), wall: mm(2), r: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(\n    j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n      .rect('outline', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 'r' }).profiles(),\n    'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { direction: 'in', id: 'shell' })\n```\n\n**Figure 6.1.** The box of A-01 with `shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]` selected: the inside of the front wall, 1,512 mm² at the default 60 mm width. Its name records three steps: the sketch line, the extrude's side, the shell's offset.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Read a face's name\n// Click faces on the sheet and read their names and Why in the inspector.\nreturn j.part('box')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(30), wall: mm(2), r: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(\n    j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n      .rect('outline', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 'r' }).profiles(),\n    'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { direction: 'in', id: 'shell' })\n```\n\n> **Try it.** [Load A-01](lab:preset/A-01) and [select the inside of the front wall](lab:select/shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]). The inspector's *Why* lists the two steps of its genealogy.\n\n## 6.2 Names survive dimension changes\n\nBecause a name records history and not position, a change of dimensions leaves it alone. The block below rounds its four vertical corners and chamfers its top edges:\n\n```js\n.fillet(j.feat('body').sideFaces().edges('|Z')\n  .where(e => e.convex).expect(4), mm(3), { id: 'corners' })\n.chamfer(j.feat('body').endFace().edges(), mm(1), { id: 'lead' })\n```\n\nCompiled at `w` = 60 mm and again at `w` = 80 mm, it has 18 faces with the same 18 names, and the selectors resolve to the same edges. The volume changes from 71,671.85 to 95,651.85 mm³; the names do not. Among them are the four `corners/face[…]` faces and eight `lead/face[…]` faces, including the ones the chamfer cut across the fillets: `lead/face[corners/face[body/side[base.r.bottom] ∩ body/side[base.r.left]] ∩ body/end]`, a chamfer face named after the edge between a fillet face and the top.\n\nThe selectors themselves are written by history too. `j.feat('body').sideFaces()` means \"the side faces of the extrude `body`\", whatever their number; `.edges('|Z')` keeps the edges parallel to Z; `.where(e => e.convex)` keeps the outside ones; `.expect(4)` asserts there are four. If a later edit made it five, the compile would fail at this selector with `ref.expect`, rather than fillet five edges and fail somewhere less obvious.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// select: corners/face[body/side[base.r.bottom] ∩ body/side[base.r.left]]\nconst base = j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 'w', 'd', { center: true })\nreturn j.part('box')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(30) })\n  .extrude(base.profiles(), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .fillet(j.feat('body').sideFaces().edges('|Z')\n    .where(e => e.convex).expect(4), mm(3), { id: 'corners' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('body').endFace().edges(), mm(1), { id: 'lead' })\n```\n\n**Figure 6.2.** A block with filleted vertical corners and a chamfered top, the front-left fillet selected. At `w` = 60 mm and at 80 mm it has the same 18 face names; only their areas change.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Names that survive a change of width\n// Change w to 80 mm and Run: every face keeps its name.\nconst base = j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 'w', 'd', { center: true })\nreturn j.part('box')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(30) })\n  .extrude(base.profiles(), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .fillet(j.feat('body').sideFaces().edges('|Z')\n    .where(e => e.convex).expect(4), mm(3), { id: 'corners' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('body').endFace().edges(), mm(1), { id: 'lead' })\n```\n\n## 6.3 Queries mean positions; references mean history\n\njig has two kinds of selector, and they answer different questions.\n\nA **query** selects by geometry on every compile: `j.sel.faces('>Z')` is the face farthest along Z, `'|Z'` the edges parallel to Z, `'%cylinder'` the cylindrical faces. The language is CadQuery's, combined with `and`, `or` and `not`. A **reference** selects by history: `j.feat('plate').endFace()` is the far cap of the extrude `plate`, found through the genealogy.\n\nBefore anything is built on top of it, both pick the top of a plate. Put a boss on the plate and they part:\n\n```text\nj.sel.faces('>Z')          → boss/end     '>Z' → 1 of 13\nj.feat('plate').endFace()  → plate/end    1 by genealogy\n```\n\nNeither is wrong. A label embossed on \"the top\" should follow the query; a chamfer on \"the plate's top edges\" should follow the reference. The bug is to write one when you mean the other, and the compiler cannot tell which you meant, so it shows both in `why`.\n\nA selector also returns exactly what it names. `j.feat('plate').endFace().edges()` is every edge of the plate's top face, and after the boss is added that includes the circle where the boss meets it. In the part below, the 1 mm chamfer on those edges therefore also cuts a concave chamfer round the root of the boss, named `edge/face[boss/side[b.c] ∩ plate/end]`. To chamfer only the outside, add `.where(e => e.convex)`.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// select: plate/end\nconst plate = j.sketch('p', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 60, 40, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('mount')\n  .extrude(plate.profiles(), mm(5), { id: 'plate' })\n  .extrude(j.sketch('b', { on: j.feat('plate').endFace() })\n    .circle('c', 8).profiles(), mm(12), { id: 'boss' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('plate').endFace().edges(), mm(1), { id: 'edge' })\n```\n\n**Figure 6.3.** A 60 × 40 × 5 mm plate with a boss 16 mm across and 12 mm tall, `plate/end` selected. `j.sel.faces('>Z')` now resolves to `boss/end`; the reference `j.feat('plate').endFace()` still resolves to `plate/end`, and its edges include the boss's root circle, which the chamfer also cut.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A query and a reference part ways\n// Chamfer j.sel.faces('>Z').edges() instead and see which edges change.\nconst plate = j.sketch('p', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 60, 40, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('mount')\n  .extrude(plate.profiles(), mm(5), { id: 'plate' })\n  .extrude(j.sketch('b', { on: j.feat('plate').endFace() })\n    .circle('c', 8).profiles(), mm(12), { id: 'boss' })\n  .chamfer(j.feat('plate').endFace().edges(), mm(1), { id: 'edge' })\n```\n\n## 6.4 Splits, losses and fragile references\n\nA later feature can split a face. A groove cut across the top of a 60 × 40 × 20 mm block divides `body/end` into two pieces, and each keeps the name with a suffix: `body/end#a` and `body/end#b`, 1,020 mm² each. `why('body/end#a')` explains:\n\n```text\nbody/end#a: piece a of a face split by a later feature\nbody/end ← body: the far cap of the extrusion\n```\n\nA reference to `body/end` after the split resolves to both pieces, which is usually what a feature on \"the top\" wants. The groove's own faces are new: `groove/side[g.r.bottom]`, `groove/side[g.r.top]` and the floor `groove/start`.\n\nNames do not survive the removal of what they were made from. Misspell a sketch entity in a reference, `sideFace('r.leftt')`, or delete the entity, and the compiler warns `ref.lost: j.feat('body').sideFace('r.leftt').edges('|Z') resolved to nothing`, and the fillet that used it is skipped with a second `ref.lost`.\n\nSelectors by index are the fragile kind. `j.sel.edges('|Z').nth(0)` is whichever vertical edge the kernel lists first, and the compiler warns `ref.fragile: nth(0) depends on face order`. On a block whose left side had already been filleted, `nth(0)` picked an edge the fillet had rounded away, and a 1 mm chamfer on it failed with `feature.failed`. A reference by feature, or a query with `.expect(n)`, says what it means and fails at the selector when the model no longer has it.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// select: body/end#a\nconst groove = j.sketch('g', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('r', 'w + 2 mm', 6, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('block')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(20) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(groove.profiles(), mm(4), { id: 'groove' })\n```\n\n**Figure 6.4.** A groove 6 mm wide and 4 mm deep across a 60 × 40 × 20 mm block splits the top face in two; `body/end#a` is selected. Both pieces keep the name of the face they came from.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A face split in two\n// Make the groove narrower than the block (w - 10 mm): the top is not split.\nconst groove = j.sketch('g', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h') })\n  .rect('r', 'w + 2 mm', 6, { center: true })\nreturn j.part('block')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), h: mm(20) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd'), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(groove.profiles(), mm(4), { id: 'groove' })\n```\n\n| diagnostic | level | means |\n|---|---|---|\n| `ref.lost` | warning | a selector resolved to nothing |\n| `ref.fragile` | warning | a selector depends on the kernel's order (`nth`) |\n| `ref.expect` | error | `.expect(n)` counted a different number |\n| `ref.autoId` | info | a reference to a generated id such as `extrude1`, which changes when features are added before it |\n\n## 6.5 Key points\n\n- Every face and edge has a persistent name `<feature>/<role>[<source>]`, built from the genealogy the features report.\n- Names record history, so they survive changes of dimension: the block of Figure 6.2 keeps all 18 names from 60 to 80 mm wide.\n- A query (`'>Z'`) selects by position on every compile; a reference (`j.feat(id).endFace()`) selects by history. Choose the one that means what you mean.\n- A face split by a later feature keeps its name with `#a`, `#b`, and a reference to the original resolves to all the pieces.\n- `why` explains any reference: what it resolved to and the chain of features behind it.\n- Broken references are diagnostics: `ref.lost`, `ref.fragile` for `nth`, `ref.expect` for a wrong count; use `.expect(n)` to fail early.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** In Figure 6.3, change the chamfer's selector so that it does not touch the boss. Find two ways: one with `.where`, one with `.minus`.\n\n> **Exercise.** Add a second groove across the block of Figure 6.4, perpendicular to the first. How many pieces does `body/end` have, and what are they called?\n\n> **Exercise.** [D-03](lab:preset/D-03) fillets the floor edges of its pockets with a `where` on concave edges at the floor height. Rewrite that selector with `j.feat('pocket').edges()` and `.created('pocket')`, and check with `why` that it resolves to the same edges.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- Kripac, *A mechanism for persistently naming topological entities in history-based parametric solid models*, Computer-Aided Design 29(2), 1997: naming by feature history, the basis of jig's names.\n- [Persistent names and genealogy](lab:doc?id=naming): the naming rules as a reference page.\n"},{"file":"07-patterns.md","text":"---\npart: II · Solids\npresets: S-03, D-01, D-03\ndocs: reference\n---\n# Symmetry and patterns\n\nSix bosses round a hub, twelve holes in a grid, nineteen pockets in a coaster: most repetition in a part is a symmetry applied to one feature. This chapter treats a pattern as what it is mathematically, a group of transformations acting on a feature, and shows what that buys: the number of instances is known before any geometry exists, two patterns combine by multiplying their counts, and instances that coincide merge instead of failing. By the end you can write a pattern as a product of simpler ones, count its instances by hand, and avoid the off-by-one that open circular patterns invite.\n\n## 7.1 A pattern is a group acting on a feature\n\nA **pattern** is a set of transformations, and patterning a feature applies each of them to the feature's tool (its profile, its hole, its boss) and combines the results with the part. For a circular pattern of $n$ instances about an axis the transformations are the rotations by $k \\cdot 360°/n$ for $k = 0, \\ldots, n - 1$. They form the cyclic group $C_n$: composing two rotations of the set gives another rotation of the set, the rotation by 0° is the identity, and every rotation has an inverse in the set. The feature's copies are its **orbit** under the group.\n\n`j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 6 })` is $C_6$. Its instances are known from the pattern alone, before the part is compiled: `count` is 6, `closed` is true because the angles cover a full turn, and expanding it at the point (20, 0, 0) gives the six centres\n\n| k | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |\n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| centre | (20, 0) | (10, 17.32) | (−10, 17.32) | (−20, 0) | (−10, −17.32) | (10, −17.32) |\n\nwhich is $20(\\cos 60°k, \\sin 60°k)$. A pattern of a feature **replays** the feature: `.pattern(j.feat('boss'), …)` builds the boss's tool once more at each transformed position and joins it, so each copy is a real boss with its own named faces, `bosses[1]/boss/end` to `bosses[5]/boss/end` beside the original `boss/end`.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nreturn j.part('hub')\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(30), mm(5), { id: 'disc' })\n  .extrude(j.sketch('b', { on: j.feat('disc').endFace() })\n    .circle('c', 4, { at: [20, 0] }).profiles(), mm(6), { id: 'boss' })\n  .pattern(j.feat('boss'), j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 6 }),\n    { id: 'bosses' })\n```\n\n**Figure 7.1.** A 60 mm hub 5 mm thick with one 8 mm boss at radius 20 mm, patterned by $C_6$. Volume: the disc's 14,137.17 mm³ plus six bosses of 301.59 mm³ each, 15,946.72 mm³.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A circular pattern of a feature\n// Change count to 5 or 8; the volume grows by one boss per instance.\nreturn j.part('hub')\n  .extrude(j.region.circle(30), mm(5), { id: 'disc' })\n  .extrude(j.sketch('b', { on: j.feat('disc').endFace() })\n    .circle('c', 4, { at: [20, 0] }).profiles(), mm(6), { id: 'boss' })\n  .pattern(j.feat('boss'), j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 6 }),\n    { id: 'bosses' })\n```\n\nThe same patterns act on sketch entities. `.pattern(['h1'], …)` inside a sketch copies the circle `h1` and names the copies `h1.p1`, `h1.p2`, …, so a single profile carries every hole and one extrude cuts them all, as in the plate of [Chapter 4](lab:book?ch=4).\n\n## 7.2 Products multiply counts\n\nTwo patterns combine by `.times`: every transformation of the first composed with every transformation of the second. For groups this is the **direct product**, and its size is the product of the sizes:\n\n$$\n|G \\times H| = |G| \\cdot |H| .\n$$\n\nA row of 4 holes 15 mm apart times a column of 3 is a 4 × 3 grid of 12, and the count is 12 before anything is built. The plate below is 70 × 55 × 3 mm with 12 holes of 6 mm. Each hole is a through-hole, so the genus is 12, which the Euler–Poincaré check of [Chapter 5](lab:book?ch=5) confirms: $V = 40$, $E = 60$, $F = 22$, $L = 46$, $S = 1$ give $G = 1 - \\tfrac{1}{2}(40 - 60 + 22 - 24) = 12$.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\nconst across = j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+X'), { count: 4, step: mm(15) })\nconst deep = j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+Y'), { count: 3, step: mm(15) })\nconst holes = j.sketch('h', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 70, 55, { center: true, r: 4 })\n  .circle('c', 3, { at: [-22.5, -15] })\n  .pattern(['c'], across.times(deep))\nreturn j.part('plate').extrude(holes.profiles(), mm(3), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n**Figure 7.2.** A 70 × 55 × 3 mm plate, plan: one 6 mm hole and the product of a 4-instance row and a 3-instance column, 12 holes, genus 12. Volume $(3850 - 13.73 - 12 \\cdot 9\\pi) \\times 3 = 10{,}490.92$ mm³.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A grid as a product of two rows\n// Replace deep by j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X): how many holes now?\nconst across = j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+X'), { count: 4, step: mm(15) })\nconst deep = j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+Y'), { count: 3, step: mm(15) })\nconst holes = j.sketch('h', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .rect('r', 70, 55, { center: true, r: 4 })\n  .circle('c', 3, { at: [-22.5, -15] })\n  .pattern(['c'], across.times(deep))\nreturn j.part('plate').extrude(holes.profiles(), mm(3), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\nA **mirror** is a group of two elements, the identity and the reflection, and the reflection is an **involution**: mirroring twice is the identity. That law has a visible consequence. `mx.times(mx)`, a mirror composed with itself, has four transformations, but only two distinct ones; expanded, it reports `count 2, total 4, deduped 2` and the decision `pattern.dedupe: 2 coincident instances removed`. Coincident instances merge rather than being cut or joined twice. Mirrors across two perpendicular axes, on the other hand, do not collapse: `j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X).times(j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.Y))` is the four-element Klein group and puts a hole in each corner of the plate of Figure 4.1.\n\n## 7.3 Lattices and filters\n\nA **lattice** pattern repeats a cell over the plane. `j.pattern.hex(plane, { pitch, rings })` places cells on a triangular lattice, the arrangement whose cells are hexagons; with $r$ rings round the centre it has\n\n$$\n1 + 6 + 12 + \\ldots + 6r = 1 + 3r(r + 1)\n$$\n\ninstances: 37 for 3 rings. A lattice rarely fits a part's outline, so patterns take a **filter**, a predicate on each instance that sees its position and the part's params. Filtering the hex lattice of 10 mm pitch to centres within 20 mm of the origin keeps 19 of the 37, rings 0 to 2.\n\nThe coaster of [S-03](lab:preset/S-03) uses both. Its pockets are hexagons of circumradius `cell` (8 mm) on a lattice of pitch $\\sqrt{3}\\cdot\\text{cell} + \\text{web}$, rotated 30° so that neighbouring pockets face each other flat to flat across a web of `lw(3)`. The filter keeps a pocket only if its farthest corner stays inside the rim:\n\n```js\n.filter((c, { scope }) => Math.hypot(c.at.x, c.at.y)\n  + scope.cell.value <= scope.d.value / 2 - scope.rim.value)\n```\n\nFor a 90 mm coaster with a 4 mm rim, 19 pockets pass. The pocket cut makes 19 × 7 faces (six walls and a floor each), and the part has 136 faces in all. The filter reads the params through `scope`, so a larger diameter admits more pockets without any change to the pattern.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\nconst lattice = j.frame({ origin: j.origin, z: j.dir('+Z'),\n  x: j.dir(Math.cos(Math.PI / 6), Math.sin(Math.PI / 6), 0) })\nconst corner = k => [Math.cos(k * Math.PI / 3), Math.sin(k * Math.PI / 3)]\nconst hexagon = [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5].map(k => corner(k))\n  .map(([c, s]) => [`cell * ${c.toFixed(6)}`, `cell * ${s.toFixed(6)}`])\nconst pitch = 'cell * sqrt(3) + web'\nconst cells = j.sketch('cells', { on: j.feat('body').endFace() })\n  .polyline('cell', hexagon, { closed: true })\n  .pattern(['cell'], j.pattern.hex(lattice, { pitch, rings: 3 })\n    .filter((c, { scope }) => Math.hypot(c.at.x, c.at.y)\n      + scope.cell.value <= scope.d.value / 2 - scope.rim.value))\nreturn j.part('coaster')\n  .params({ d: mm(90), t: mm(4), depth: mm(1.6), cell: mm(8),\n    web: lw(3), rim: mm(4) })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle('d / 2'), 't', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(cells.profiles(), 'depth', { id: 'pockets' })\n```\n\n**Figure 7.3.** A 90 mm coaster in plan: a hex lattice of 37 cells filtered to the 19 whose corners stay 4 mm inside the rim, cut 1.6 mm deep. Pockets 16 mm across corners, webs of `lw(3)` = 1.35 mm.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A filtered hex lattice\n// Set d to 120 mm: the same filter admits more pockets. Try rim: mm(10).\nconst lattice = j.frame({ origin: j.origin, z: j.dir('+Z'),\n  x: j.dir(Math.cos(Math.PI / 6), Math.sin(Math.PI / 6), 0) })\nconst corner = k => [Math.cos(k * Math.PI / 3), Math.sin(k * Math.PI / 3)]\nconst hexagon = [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5].map(k => corner(k))\n  .map(([c, s]) => [`cell * ${c.toFixed(6)}`, `cell * ${s.toFixed(6)}`])\nconst pitch = 'cell * sqrt(3) + web'\nconst cells = j.sketch('cells', { on: j.feat('body').endFace() })\n  .polyline('cell', hexagon, { closed: true })\n  .pattern(['cell'], j.pattern.hex(lattice, { pitch, rings: 3 })\n    .filter((c, { scope }) => Math.hypot(c.at.x, c.at.y)\n      + scope.cell.value <= scope.d.value / 2 - scope.rim.value))\nreturn j.part('coaster')\n  .params({ d: mm(90), t: mm(4), depth: mm(1.6), cell: mm(8),\n    web: lw(3), rim: mm(4) })\n  .extrude(j.region.circle('d / 2'), 't', { id: 'body' })\n  .cut(cells.profiles(), 'depth', { id: 'pockets' })\n```\n\n> **Careful.** The pattern's `rings` bounds the lattice before filtering, so it must be large enough for the largest diameter you expect. With 3 rings at the coaster's 15.21 mm pitch, no cell centre lies more than 45.6 mm from the middle, so a larger coaster would get no new pockets; the preset computes `rings` from `d` for that reason.\n\n## 7.4 Open and closed circular patterns\n\nA circular pattern over a full turn is **closed**: its last step returns to the first instance, so the step is $360°/n$. Over a partial angle $\\theta$ it is **open**, and both ends are instances, so the step is $\\theta/(n - 1)$. Three instances over 120° are at 0°, 60° and 120°; three over 180° are at 0°, 90° and 180°.\n\nThe difference decides the pen cup of [D-01](lab:preset/D-01). Its window is cut straight through the hexagonal cup with `{ symmetric: … }`, so one cut makes two windows on opposite flats, and the cut is symmetric under a half turn. To put a window on each of the six flats, the cut must be repeated at 60° and 120°: count 3 over 120°. Written as count 3 over 180°, the instances fall at 0°, 90° and 180°. The one at 180° coincides with the original and removes nothing; the one at 90° cuts through two corners of the hexagon instead of two flats. The two scripts compile to 38,965.40 mm³ with six windows, and 41,949.97 mm³ with two windows on flats and two on corners.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// height: 320\nconst window = j.sketch('win', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .polyline('w', [\n    [0, 'sill'], ['win_w / 2', 'sill + win_w / 2'],\n    ['win_w / 2', 'h - lintel - win_w / 2'], [0, 'h - lintel'],\n    ['-win_w / 2', 'h - lintel - win_w / 2'],\n    ['-win_w / 2', 'sill + win_w / 2'],\n  ], { closed: true })\nconst hex = j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polygon('hex', 6, 'af / 2', { inscribed: false })\nreturn j.part('pen-cup')\n  .params({ af: mm(70), h: mm(100), wall: lw(4), win_w: mm(14),\n    sill: mm(12), lintel: mm(12) })\n  .extrude(hex.profiles(), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { id: 'shell' })\n  .cut(window.profiles(), { symmetric: 'af + 10 mm' }, { id: 'window' })\n  .pattern(j.feat('window'),\n    j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 3, angle: deg(120) }),\n    { id: 'windows' })\n```\n\n**Figure 7.4.** A hexagonal cup 70 mm across flats and 100 mm tall, shelled to `lw(4)`, with one through-cut repeated at 0°, 60° and 120°: six pointed windows, one per flat. The 45° points top and bottom print without support ([Chapter 8](lab:book?ch=8)).\n\n```sketch\n// title: Open and closed circular patterns\n// Change angle to deg(180) and count the windows; then try count: 6 alone.\nconst window = j.sketch('win', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .polyline('w', [\n    [0, 'sill'], ['win_w / 2', 'sill + win_w / 2'],\n    ['win_w / 2', 'h - lintel - win_w / 2'], [0, 'h - lintel'],\n    ['-win_w / 2', 'h - lintel - win_w / 2'],\n    ['-win_w / 2', 'sill + win_w / 2'],\n  ], { closed: true })\nconst hex = j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polygon('hex', 6, 'af / 2', { inscribed: false })\nreturn j.part('pen-cup')\n  .params({ af: mm(70), h: mm(100), wall: lw(4), win_w: mm(14),\n    sill: mm(12), lintel: mm(12) })\n  .extrude(hex.profiles(), 'h', { id: 'body' })\n  .shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { id: 'shell' })\n  .cut(window.profiles(), { symmetric: 'af + 10 mm' }, { id: 'window' })\n  .pattern(j.feat('window'),\n    j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 3, angle: deg(120) }),\n    { id: 'windows' })\n```\n\nThe symmetry argument also says which patterns are equivalent. The cut has the symmetry of a half turn, so $C_6$ acting on it has orbits of size 3: `count: 6` over a full turn makes the same six windows, with three of its six instances coinciding with others. Count 3 over 120° is the smaller description of the same set.\n\n## 7.5 Key points\n\n- A pattern is a group of transformations acting on a feature's tool; the copies are the feature's orbit.\n- A pattern's instance count and transforms are known before any geometry is built.\n- `.times` forms the product of two patterns, and the counts multiply: 4 × 3 = 12.\n- A mirror is an involution, so a mirror times itself collapses to two instances, and the compiler merges coincident instances with a `pattern.dedupe` decision.\n- A hex lattice of $r$ rings has $1 + 3r(r + 1)$ cells; a filter on position and params cuts it to the part's outline.\n- A closed circular pattern steps by $360°/n$; an open one by $\\theta/(n - 1)$, so count 3 over 180° means 0°, 90°, 180°.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** Write the hub's six bosses as a product of a half-turn pattern and a three-instance pattern. Which two patterns, and does the volume match Figure 7.1?\n\n> **Exercise.** In the coaster, how many pockets pass the filter for $d$ = 120 mm? Predict it from the ring geometry before compiling.\n\n> **Exercise.** [D-03](lab:preset/D-03) uses `j.pattern.grid` for its pockets. Rewrite it as a product of two linear patterns and check that the pockets' names do not change.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- [The reference: patterns](lab:reference): every pattern constructor and operation, with status.\n"},{"file":"08-printing-as-layers.md","text":"---\npart: III · Printing\npartAbout: How a solid becomes layers of plastic, and how parts that must fit together are sized for the printer that makes them.\npresets: D-01, D-02, C-02, C-03, C-05\ndocs: printing\n---\n# Printing as layers\n\nA fused-filament printer does not make a solid; it makes a stack of flat layers, each a few tenths of a millimetre thick, each resting on the one below. This chapter treats printing as the mathematics of that stack: layers as level sets of the solid, the volume they carry, the stair-step error they leave on a slope, and the rules that follow from material needing something underneath. By the end you can predict how much a layer height changes a part's surface and print time, explain why the compiler chose a build direction, and design holes and bed edges that print without support.\n\n## 8.1 Layers are level sets\n\nFix the **build direction**, the axis that points up on the printer, and call the height along it $z$. A layer of height $t$ is the slab $kt \\le z < (k + 1)t$, and the printer draws it from one **section**: the intersection of the solid with the plane $z = (k + \\tfrac{1}{2})t$, the layer's middle.
