Each section is a set of closed loops, an outer loop for each island of material and an inner loop for each hole, the level set of the height function at that $z$.\n\nSumming section area times layer height over the layers approximates the volume:\n\n$$\nV \\approx \\sum_k A((k + \\tfrac{1}{2})t)\\,t .\n$$\n\nThis is the midpoint rule for $V = \\int A(z)\\,dz$, Cavalieri's principle from [Chapter 4](lab:book?ch=4) applied one slab at a time, and its error is $O(t^2)$: for a section area with bounded second derivative it is at most $\\tfrac{t^2}{24} \\int |A''(z)|\\,dz$.\n\nThe dome below is a hemisphere of radius $R = 40$ mm, $V = \\tfrac{2}{3}\\pi R^3 = 134{,}041.3$ mm³. Sliced at three layer heights:\n\n| layer | layers | Σ area · t | difference | print time |\n|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|\n| 0.4 mm | 100 | 133,784.3 mm³ | −256.95 mm³ | 69.7 min |\n| 0.2 mm | 200 | 133,783.4 mm³ | −257.89 mm³ | 115.9 min |\n| 0.1 mm | 400 | 133,783.1 mm³ | −258.23 mm³ | 208.5 min |\n\nThe difference does not shrink with $t$, so it is not the midpoint rule's error. For a hemisphere $A(z) = \\pi(R^2 - z^2)$, $A'' = -2\\pi$, and the rule's error is $\\pi R t^2 / 12$: 0.42 mm³ at 0.2 mm, too small to see in the table except as the 1.3 mm³ drift between its first and last rows. The constant 258 mm³ comes from measuring each section as a polygon whose chords stay within $\\delta = 0.05$ mm of the true circle. An inscribed polygon with sagitta $\\delta$ loses about $\\tfrac{2}{3}\\delta$ of area per unit of perimeter, and integrating that over the dome gives\n\n$$\n\\Delta V \\approx \\tfrac{2}{3}\\,\\delta \\int_0^R 2\\pi r(z)\\,dz = \\tfrac{4}{3}\\pi\\delta \\cdot \\tfrac{\\pi R^2}{4} = \\tfrac{\\pi^2 \\delta R^2}{3} = 263\\ \\mathrm{mm^3} .\n$$\n\nThe slicer's volume is an approximation, and the compiler reports it as one: `fidelity: approx`.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst quarter = j.sketch('q', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .arc('a', { center: [0, 0], start: [40, 0], end: [0, 40] })\n  .line('l1', 'a.end', [0, 0]).line('l2', [0, 0], 'a.start')\nconst dome = j.part('dome').material(j.material('PLA'))\n  .revolve(quarter.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'dome' })\nreturn j.print('dome', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [dome] })\n  .orient('+Z').slice({ layer: mm(0.2) })\n```\n\n**Figure 8.1.** A hemisphere of radius 40 mm, sliced in 200 layers of 0.2 mm. The sum of section areas times 0.2 mm is 133,783.4 mm³, 258 mm³ short of $\\tfrac{2}{3}\\pi R^3$ because each section's circle is measured as a polygon within 0.05 mm.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Slice a dome\n// Change layer to mm(0.1) or mm(0.3) and read layers, cusp and print time.\nconst quarter = j.sketch('q', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .arc('a', { center: [0, 0], start: [40, 0], end: [0, 40] })\n  .line('l1', 'a.end', [0, 0]).line('l2', [0, 0], 'a.start')\nconst dome = j.part('dome').material(j.material('PLA'))\n  .revolve(quarter.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'dome' })\nreturn j.print('dome', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [dome] })\n  .orient('+Z').slice({ layer: mm(0.2) })\n```\n\n## 8.2 Cusp height\n\nLayers turn a slope into stairs. On a face whose normal makes angle $\\theta$ with the build direction, the step between two layers rises $t$ and runs $t\\tan\\theta$; the **cusp height**, the greatest distance from the stair to the true surface measured along the normal, is\n\n$$\nc = t\\,|\\cos\\theta| ,\n$$\n\nthe formula of Dolenc and Mäkelä (1994). A vertical wall ($\\theta = 90°$) has no cusp; a gentle slope, nearly a full layer. Faces exactly horizontal are excluded: their height is a layer boundary, and the printer draws them flat.\n\nThe compiler reports the largest cusp and the area-weighted mean. For the hemisphere the mean of $|\\cos\\theta|$ over the curved surface is exactly $\\tfrac{1}{2}$, and at 0.2 mm the mean cusp is 0.1000 mm. For a cone whose side rises at 45°, here 80 mm across at the base and cut off 36 mm up with a flat top 8 mm across, every point of the side has $\\theta = 45°$, so the cusp is uniform: $0.2 \\cos 45° = 0.1414$ mm, which is both the mean and the maximum the compiler reports. The flat top is a layer boundary and adds nothing. Halving the layer height halves every cusp; from the table in 8.1 it multiplies the print time by 1.66 going from 0.4 to 0.2 mm and by 1.80 going from 0.2 to 0.1 mm.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst side = j.sketch('q', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [40, 0], [4, 36], [0, 36]], { closed: true })\nconst cone = j.part('cone')\n  .revolve(side.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'c' })\nreturn j.print('cone', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [cone] })\n  .orient('+Z').slice({ layer: mm(0.2) })\n```\n\n**Figure 8.2.** A truncated cone 80 mm across and 36 mm tall: its side is 45° from vertical everywhere, so at 0.2 mm layers the cusp is $0.2\\cos 45° = 0.1414$ mm at every point. Its 180 sections sum to 66,793.0 mm³ against a volume of 66,953.6 mm³.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A uniform cusp\n// Make it taller ([4, 72], [0, 72]): θ nears 90° and the cusp shrinks.\nconst side = j.sketch('q', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [40, 0], [4, 36], [0, 36]], { closed: true })\nconst cone = j.part('cone')\n  .revolve(side.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'c' })\nreturn j.print('cone', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [cone] })\n  .orient('+Z').slice({ layer: mm(0.2) })\n```\n\nThe formula says where layer height matters: on surfaces facing nearly up or down. An adaptive slicer chooses $t$ per layer so that $c$ stays below a target; that needs only this formula and the faces' normals, and it is not in this build.\n\n## 8.3 Overhangs and orientation\n\nEach layer must rest on the one below. A face whose downward normal is more than about **45°** from vertical steps out more than half a line width per layer, and the outer line has nothing under it. This is a threshold that depends on the printer and the material, not a theorem, so jig keeps it in the printer profile (`maxOverhang: 45` for FDM, 30 for resin) and treats it as a calibrated number. [C-02](lab:preset/C-02) prints six ledges from 20° to 70° to find where your printer starts to droop, and [C-03](lab:preset/C-03) finds the longest flat bridge it lays. Faces past the limit are **overhangs**, and they need support: material printed under them and removed afterwards, at a cost in filament, time and surface.\n\nWhich faces overhang depends on the build direction, and choosing it is an optimisation over directions on the sphere. jig's default, `orient('auto')`, tries 26 candidates (the six faces, twelve edges and eight corners of a cube) and ranks them **lexicographically**: least support volume, then most bed contact, then least height. A later criterion only breaks ties in an earlier one, so no amount of bed contact buys a cubic millimetre of support. For the L-bracket below, a 60 × 30 × 5 mm base with a 60 × 5 × 40 mm upright, two M4 holes and an 8 mm cable hole:\n\n| direction | support | bed contact | height | print time |\n|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|\n| +Z (auto) | 0 mm³ | 1,765 mm² | 45 mm | 54.9 min |\n| −Z | 20,516 mm³ | 300 mm² | 45 mm | 73.9 min |\n| +Y (`'min-height'`) | 0 mm³ | 2,650 mm² | 30 mm | 48.0 min |\n\nAuto chose +Z, and its decision names the runner-up: `support 0 mm³ → bed contact 1765 mm² → height 45.0 mm (next: +Y, support 51 mm³)`. Upside down, 1,465 mm² of the base overhangs and the support estimate is 20,516 mm³, about as much as the part's own 20,558 mm³.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// overlays: dims,overhang,bed\nconst bracket = j.part('bracket').material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .add(j.box(60, 30, 5), { id: 'base' })\n  .add(j.box(60, 5, 40), { at: j.tx.move(0, 0, 5), id: 'upright' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hb', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(5)) })\n    .point('p1', [15, 18]).point('p2', [45, 18]),\n    j.hole.clearance('M4', { fit: 'free' }), { id: 'screws' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hc', { on: j.plane.XZ }).point('p', [30, 30]),\n    j.hole.simple(mm(8), mm(5)), { id: 'cable' })\nreturn j.print('bracket', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [bracket] })\n```\n\n**Figure 8.3.** The L-bracket in the orientation `auto` chose, +Z up: no face overhangs past 45°, so the overhang overlay hatches nothing. The cable hole through the upright is horizontal in this orientation and has become a teardrop.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Choose a build direction\n// Add .orient('-Z') or .orient('min-height') and compare support and time.\nconst bracket = j.part('bracket').material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .add(j.box(60, 30, 5), { id: 'base' })\n  .add(j.box(60, 5, 40), { at: j.tx.move(0, 0, 5), id: 'upright' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hb', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(5)) })\n    .point('p1', [15, 18]).point('p2', [45, 18]),\n    j.hole.clearance('M4', { fit: 'free' }), { id: 'screws' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hc', { on: j.plane.XZ }).point('p', [30, 30]),\n    j.hole.simple(mm(8), mm(5)), { id: 'cable' })\nreturn j.print('bracket', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [bracket] })\n```\n\n## 8.4 Teardrops and a fixpoint\n\nA round hole whose axis lies across the build direction has a ceiling that is flat at the top: the last layers of the arch overhang by nearly 90°. The standard fix is the **teardrop**: replace the top of the circle by two lines at 45° that meet in a point, so no part of the ceiling exceeds the overhang limit. jig applies it to every such hole and records it: `cable.p teardrop → teardrop (axis +Y ⟂ build direction +Z → 45° teardrop, tip toward +Z)`. The new faces are named `cable/tear.l[p]` and `cable/tear.r[p]`. [C-05](lab:preset/C-05) drills holes from 2 to 10 mm down through a plate and sideways through a wall, to compare round holes with teardrops on your printer. A teardrop already removes more material than the circle, and the clearance holes of [Chapter 9](lab:book?ch=9) leave out their hole compensation on teardrops.\n\nThis creates a loop. The holes' shapes depend on the build direction, and the build direction, chosen by support volume, depends on the holes' shapes. The compiler resolves it as a **fixpoint**: orient, re-shape the holes, orient again, until the direction and the persistent names stop changing, at most four rounds. For the bracket under `'min-height'` the loop runs twice. Turned to +Y, the cable hole is now vertical and becomes round again, and the two screw holes in the base, now horizontal, become teardrops pointing to +Y; the trace reads `fixpoint: converged in 2`.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\n// overlays: dims,overhang,bed\nconst bracket = j.part('bracket').material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .add(j.box(60, 30, 5), { id: 'base' })\n  .add(j.box(60, 5, 40), { at: j.tx.move(0, 0, 5), id: 'upright' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hb', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(5)) })\n    .point('p1', [15, 18]).point('p2', [45, 18]),\n    j.hole.clearance('M4', { fit: 'free' }), { id: 'screws' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hc', { on: j.plane.XZ }).point('p', [30, 30]),\n    j.hole.simple(mm(8), mm(5)), { id: 'cable' })\nreturn j.print('bracket', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [bracket] })\n  .orient('min-height')\n```\n\n**Figure 8.4.** The same bracket under `orient('min-height')`: printed with +Y up, 30 mm tall. Its teardrops moved: the cable hole is round, and the two screw holes in the base are teardrops pointing along +Y. The compiler needed two rounds to agree with itself.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Teardrops follow the build direction\n// Select the screw holes' faces: tear.l and tear.r appear only under +Y.\nconst bracket = j.part('bracket').material(j.material('PETG'))\n  .add(j.box(60, 30, 5), { id: 'base' })\n  .add(j.box(60, 5, 40), { at: j.tx.move(0, 0, 5), id: 'upright' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hb', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, mm(5)) })\n    .point('p1', [15, 18]).point('p2', [45, 18]),\n    j.hole.clearance('M4', { fit: 'free' }), { id: 'screws' })\n  .hole(j.sketch('hc', { on: j.plane.XZ }).point('p', [30, 30]),\n    j.hole.simple(mm(8), mm(5)), { id: 'cable' })\nreturn j.print('bracket', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [bracket] })\n  .orient('min-height')\n```\n\nThe same reasoning shapes openings in walls. The windows of the pen cup in [D-01](lab:preset/D-01) end in 45° points at top and bottom, so their ceilings print without support or a bridge.\n\n## 8.5 Bed edges\n\nThe edges that touch the bed meet two problems at once. The first layer is pressed onto the bed and spreads by about 0.2 mm, the **elephant's foot**, and the edge above it is the one most likely to overhang. A 45° chamfer of 0.4 to 0.6 mm answers both: it takes up the spread, and at exactly 45° it never exceeds the overhang limit. A fillet does the opposite. It leaves the bed tangent to it, so its lowest band faces almost straight down.\n\nOn a 40 × 30 × 10 mm block, 2 mm fillets on the bed edges overhang over 204.7 mm², the four faces `foot/face[body/side[rect.…] ∩ body/start]`, and need an estimated 12 mm³ of support; 2 mm chamfers on the same edges overhang nowhere. The desk presets end with a chamfer for this reason:\n\n```js\n.chamfer(j.feat('body').startFace().edges(), mm(0.6), { id: 'foot' })\n```\n\n```figure\n// view: front\n// overlays: dims,overhang\nconst block = j.part('block')\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(40, 30), mm(10), { id: 'body' })\n  .fillet(j.feat('body').startFace().edges(), mm(2), { id: 'foot' })\nreturn j.print('block', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [block] })\n  .orient('+Z')\n```\n\n**Figure 8.5.** A 40 × 30 × 10 mm block with 2 mm fillets on its bed edges, front elevation, overhangs hatched: the fillets' lower halves face down by more than 45°, 204.7 mm² in all. Change the fillet to a chamfer and the hatching disappears.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Fillet or chamfer on the bed\n// Change .fillet to .chamfer and read the overhang diagnostic go away.\nconst block = j.part('block')\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(40, 30), mm(10), { id: 'body' })\n  .fillet(j.feat('body').startFace().edges(), mm(2), { id: 'foot' })\nreturn j.print('block', { printer: j.printer('mk4'), parts: [block] })\n  .orient('+Z')\n```\n\n> **Try it.** [Load D-02](lab:preset/D-02), the business-card holder, and turn on *Overhang* under *Show ▾* in the stage: the back leans 15°, well inside 45°, and the only chamfer is the 0.5 mm foot.\n\n## 8.6 Key points\n\n- A layer is drawn from the solid's section at the layer's middle; the sections are level sets of height.\n- Σ area × layer height is the midpoint rule for the volume, with error $O(t^2)$; in practice the section's polygon sampling dominates, 258 mm³ for a 40 mm dome.\n- Cusp height is $c = t\\,|\\cos\\theta|$ (Dolenc and Mäkelä): zero on vertical walls, nearly $t$ on shallow slopes, $t/2$ on average over a hemisphere.\n- The 45° overhang limit is a calibrated threshold kept in the printer profile, not a law.\n- `orient('auto')` ranks 26 directions lexicographically: support, then bed contact, then height.\n- Horizontal holes become 45° teardrops; because orientation and teardrops depend on each other, the compiler iterates to a fixpoint.\n- Bed edges take a 45° chamfer, not a fillet: the chamfer absorbs elephant's foot and never overhangs.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** Using $c = t|\\cos\\theta|$, find the layer height that keeps the cusp of the dome's flattest printed band, the top 2 mm, under 0.05 mm. How many layers would the whole dome then need?\n\n> **Exercise.** Give the bracket a fillet of 3 mm along the inside edge where the upright meets the base. Does the auto orientation change? Read the decision.\n\n> **Exercise.** Write a function `orient: (part, ctx) => …` that returns `'+Y'` when the default's support volume is above 1,000 mm³ and the default otherwise, using `ctx.default(part)`.\n\n## Further reading\n\n- Dolenc and Mäkelä, *Slicing procedures for layered manufacturing techniques*, Computer-Aided Design 26(2), 1994: cusp height and adaptive layer thickness.\n- [How jig thinks about printing](lab:doc?id=printing): the defaults of this chapter as a reference page.\n"},{"file":"09-fit.md","text":"---\npart: III · Printing\npresets: C-01, C-04, D-04\ndocs: printing\n---\n# Fit and calibration\n\nA modelled 3 mm hole prints at less than 3 mm, a 3 mm pin at more, and by how much depends on the printer, the filament, the slicer and the hole's orientation. No formula predicts it, so jig does not pretend one does. This chapter shows how jig sizes mating features from named clearance classes, how a printed coupon and a least-squares fit replace the default classes with measured ones, and how tolerance stacks and print-in-place assemblies use the same gaps. By the end you can calibrate a printer from one coupon and say whether a lid will fit before printing it.\n\n## 9.1 Fit classes and modelled sizes\n\nA **fit class** names an intended relation between a hole and what goes in it, and gives it a clearance **per side**:\n\n| class | gap per side | for |\n|---|--:|---|\n| press | 0.05 mm | magnets, dowels that must not move |\n| snug | 0.10 mm | parts held by friction |\n| slide | 0.20 mm | pins that turn, lids that lift off |\n| free | 0.30 mm | loose screws |\n| printInPlace | 0.40 mm | joints printed assembled |\n\nA fit-aware size is computed from the nominal size $d$, the class's gap $g$, and a **hole compensation** $c$ for the printer's tendency to close holes up:\n\n$$\nD = d + 2g + c, \\qquad P = d - 2g + 2e ,\n$$\n\nwhere $D$ is the modelled hole, $P$ the modelled pin and $e$ the profile's XY compensation (`xyComp`). With the defaults ($c$ = 0.1 mm, $e$ = 0), `j.fit.hole(mm(3), 'slide')` is $3 + 0.4 + 0.1 = 3.5$ mm, and `j.fit.pin(mm(3), 'press')` is 2.9 mm. The hole library uses the same rule: `j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' })` drills 3.5 mm. On a hole that has become a teardrop ([Chapter 8](lab:book?ch=8)) the compensation is left out.\n\nThe defaults are common starting points for a 0.4 mm nozzle and no more. Every feature that uses them says so with the info diagnostic `fit.uncalibrated: using default fit classes and hole compensation; print j.fit.coupon() once to calibrate`, and every resolved class appears in Params as `gap.slide` and the like, with the source `default fit classes (uncalibrated)`.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nreturn j.part('peg-board')\n  .params({ pin: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(30, 20), mm(6), { id: 'body' })\n  .hole([[-8, 0], [8, 0]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'slide'), mm(6)),\n    { id: 'pins' })\n  .hole([[0, 5]], j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' }), { id: 'screw' })\n```\n\n**Figure 9.1.** A 30 × 20 × 6 mm board with two holes for 3 mm pins and one for an M3 screw, all in the slide class: each is modelled at 3.5 mm. The compile carries `fit.uncalibrated` until a profile replaces the defaults.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Fit-aware holes\n// Change 'slide' to 'press' or 'free' and read the modelled size in the IR.\nreturn j.part('peg-board')\n  .params({ pin: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(30, 20), mm(6), { id: 'body' })\n  .hole([[-8, 0], [8, 0]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'slide'), mm(6)),\n    { id: 'pins' })\n  .hole([[0, 5]], j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' }), { id: 'screw' })\n```\n\nThe battery holder of [D-04](lab:preset/D-04) sizes its pockets with `j.fit.hole('d', 'free')`: a free fit for each cell, so cells drop in and lift out.\n\n## 9.2 A coupon measures the printer\n\nCalibration starts with a print. `j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole' })` is a part like any other: a 110 × 14 × 4 mm strip with ten holes, and beside it ten separate 6 mm pins 8 mm long. The holes are 6 mm plus twice a gap stepped from 0.05 to 0.5 mm per side, so modelled at 6.1, 6.2, …, 7.0 mm. Their compensation is off: the coupon measures the raw printer.\n\n[C-01](lab:preset/C-01) is this coupon as a preset, with a proof block. Print it in the material and with the settings you will use, then measure each hole with calipers or a gauge pin, measure a pin, and try the pin in each hole, noting the fit: none, press, snug, slide or free.\n\n```figure\n// view: plan\nreturn j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole', range: [mm(0.05), mm(0.5)],\n  steps: 10 })\n```\n\n**Figure 9.2.** The pin-hole coupon in plan: ten holes modelled at 6.1 to 7.0 mm in a 4 mm strip, and ten 6 mm test pins printed beside it. Eleven bodies in all, 7,071.53 mm³.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A fit coupon\n// Narrow the range to [mm(0.1), mm(0.3)] with steps: 9 for finer classes.\nreturn j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole', range: [mm(0.05), mm(0.5)],\n  steps: 10 })\n```\n\n## 9.3 Calibration is a least-squares fit\n\n`j.fit.calibrate(rows)` turns measurements into a profile. For the holes it fits a straight line through the (modelled, measured) pairs by least squares,\n\n$$\n\\text{measured} = a \\cdot \\text{modelled} + b ,\n$$\n\nwith $a$ and $b$ the slope and intercept that minimise the sum of squared residuals, and reports $r^2$ and the residual standard deviation so you can judge whether a line describes your printer. For the classes it reads the gaps directly: for each fit, the first hole that gave it, as $(\\text{measured hole} - \\text{measured pin})/2$.\n\nThe rows below are an example written for this chapter, eight holes and one pin; they show the arithmetic, not a real printer.\n\n```js\nconst rows = [{\n  coupon: { kind: 'pin-hole', pin: 6 }, pin: 6.04,\n  holes: [[6.1, 5.93], [6.2, 6.04], [6.3, 6.13], [6.4, 6.24],\n    [6.5, 6.33], [6.6, 6.44], [6.7, 6.52], [6.8, 6.63]],\n  outcomes: ['none', 'press', 'snug', 'snug', 'slide', 'slide',\n    'free', 'free'],\n}]\n```\n\n`j.fit.calibrate(rows)` returns $a = 0.9905$, $b = -0.1061$ mm, $r^2 = 0.99924$, a residual standard deviation of 0.0072 mm, a pin 0.04 mm oversize, and the classes press 0, snug 0.045, slide 0.145 and free 0.24 mm. By the line, a 6 mm hole prints 0.16 mm small, and the line explains all but 0.08 % of the variation.\n\nTo print a hole at a target size $D$ the model must ask for $(D - b)/a$, so the compensation is $(D - b)/a - D$. For a 3 mm pin in the slide class, $D = 3 + 2 \\times 0.145 = 3.29$ mm and the compensation is $(3.29 + 0.1061)/0.9905 - 3.29 = 0.139$ mm. The profile below uses that value; a part whose holes span a range of sizes would use a function of $d$.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst rows = [{\n  coupon: { kind: 'pin-hole', pin: 6 }, pin: 6.04,\n  holes: [[6.1, 5.93], [6.2, 6.04], [6.3, 6.13], [6.4, 6.24],\n    [6.5, 6.33], [6.6, 6.44], [6.7, 6.52], [6.8, 6.63]],\n  outcomes: ['none', 'press', 'snug', 'snug', 'slide', 'slide',\n    'free', 'free'],\n}]\nconst cal = j.fit.calibrate(rows)\nconst profile = j.fit.profile('my-mk4-petg', {\n  classes: cal.classes, holeComp: mm(0.139),\n})\nreturn j.part('peg-board')\n  .params({ pin: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(30, 20), mm(6), { id: 'body' })\n  .hole([[-8, 0], [8, 0]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'slide'), mm(6)),\n    { id: 'pins' })\n  .hole([[0, 5]], j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' }), { id: 'screw' })\n  .fit(profile)\n```\n\n**Figure 9.3.** The board of Figure 9.1 with the calibrated profile attached by `.fit(profile)`: every slide hole is now modelled at 3 + 2 × 0.145 + 0.139 = 3.429 mm, the `gap.*` params come from `fit 'my-mk4-petg'`, and `fit.uncalibrated` is gone.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Calibration as code\n// Replace the rows with your own coupon's measurements.\nconst rows = [{\n  coupon: { kind: 'pin-hole', pin: 6 }, pin: 6.04,\n  holes: [[6.1, 5.93], [6.2, 6.04], [6.3, 6.13], [6.4, 6.24],\n    [6.5, 6.33], [6.6, 6.44], [6.7, 6.52], [6.8, 6.63]],\n  outcomes: ['none', 'press', 'snug', 'snug', 'slide', 'slide',\n    'free', 'free'],\n}]\nconst cal = j.fit.calibrate(rows)\nconst profile = j.fit.profile('my-mk4-petg', {\n  classes: cal.classes, holeComp: mm(0.139),\n})\nreturn j.part('peg-board')\n  .params({ pin: mm(3) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(30, 20), mm(6), { id: 'body' })\n  .hole([[-8, 0], [8, 0]], j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'slide'), mm(6)),\n    { id: 'pins' })\n  .hole([[0, 5]], j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' }), { id: 'screw' })\n  .fit(profile)\n```\n\nFit is a calibrated metric, not a law. The spec keeps it out of the law suite on purpose: a measurement made on one spool on one day is evidence about that spool, and the profile records its name so the provenance of every gap is visible in the IR.\n\n## 9.4 Tolerance stacks\n\nA fit between two parts depends on several sizes at once. A lid whose lip drops into a box fits if the box's inside width exceeds the lip's outside width; the lip's outside width is its inside width plus two walls. Each size has a tolerance, and the gap is a sum of signed terms: a **tolerance stack**.\n\n```js\nconst stack = j.stack('lid in box')\n  .add(mm(56).pm(0.15))                 // box, inside width\n  .add(mm(2).pm(0.1), { dir: -1 })      // lip wall, left\n  .add(mm(2).pm(0.1), { dir: -1 })      // lip wall, right\n  .add(mm(51.6).pm(0.15), { dir: -1 })  // lip, inside width\n```\n\nThe nominal gap is 56 − 2 − 2 − 51.6 = 0.4 mm, 0.2 mm per side. Three analyses give three answers:\n\n| analysis | gap | assumes |\n|---|---|---|\n| `worstCase()` | −0.1 to 0.9 mm | every size at its limit at once, in the worst direction |\n| `rss()` | 0.145 to 0.655 mm | independent errors whose half-ranges are 3σ, combined as $\\sqrt{\\sum t_i^2}$ |\n| `monteCarlo({ n: 20000, seed: 7 })` | mean 0.3994 mm, σ 0.0834 mm, 0.1 % to 99.9 % from 0.144 to 0.657 mm | each size normal and truncated at its limits |\n\nWorst case says the lid can jam, by 0.1 mm. That needs all four sizes at their limits in the same direction at once; the Monte Carlo run drew 20,000 such stacks and the smallest gap among them was 0.074 mm, with no sample below zero. RSS and Monte Carlo agree because the four terms are independent and nearly normal. Every sample lies inside the worst-case interval, which is the stack law `stack.soundness` that the spec uses to check the sampler.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst lip = j.sketch('lip', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 't') })\n  .rect('r', 'lip_w', 'lip_d', { center: true, r: 2 })\nconst inside = j.sketch('in', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 't + lip_h') })\n  .rect('r', 'lip_w - 3.2 mm', 'lip_d - 3.2 mm', { center: true, r: 1 })\nreturn j.part('lid')\n  .params({ inner_w: mm(56), inner_d: mm(36), t: mm(2), lip_h: mm(4),\n    wall: mm(2), gap: mm(0.2),\n    lip_w: 'inner_w - 2 * gap', lip_d: 'inner_d - 2 * gap' })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('inner_w + 2 * wall', 'inner_d + 2 * wall',\n    { r: 4 }), 't', { id: 'plate' })\n  .extrude(lip.profiles(), 'lip_h', { id: 'lip' })\n  .cut(inside.profiles(), 'lip_h', { id: 'hollow' })\n```\n\n**Figure 9.4.** A lid for a box 56 × 36 mm inside: a 2 mm plate and a 4 mm lip, 1.6 mm thick, whose outside is the box's inside less 0.2 mm per side, the slide class. In this build an expression cannot name `gap.slide` directly, so the lid states its gap as a param of the same value.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A lid with a slide fit\n// Set gap to 0.1 mm (snug) and rerun the stack with a 55.8 mm lip.\nconst lip = j.sketch('lip', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 't') })\n  .rect('r', 'lip_w', 'lip_d', { center: true, r: 2 })\nconst inside = j.sketch('in', { on: j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 't + lip_h') })\n  .rect('r', 'lip_w - 3.2 mm', 'lip_d - 3.2 mm', { center: true, r: 1 })\nreturn j.part('lid')\n  .params({ inner_w: mm(56), inner_d: mm(36), t: mm(2), lip_h: mm(4),\n    wall: mm(2), gap: mm(0.2),\n    lip_w: 'inner_w - 2 * gap', lip_d: 'inner_d - 2 * gap' })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('inner_w + 2 * wall', 'inner_d + 2 * wall',\n    { r: 4 }), 't', { id: 'plate' })\n  .extrude(lip.profiles(), 'lip_h', { id: 'lip' })\n  .cut(inside.profiles(), 'lip_h', { id: 'hollow' })\n```\n\n## 9.5 Print in place\n\nA **print-in-place** assembly is printed already assembled: a hinge, a chain, a box with its lid. Its parts must be separate solids on the bed, so every pair that will move against each other needs a gap, and the gap must survive the printer's errors. The class for it is `printInPlace`, 0.4 mm per side by default, twice the slide class because the gap is printed, not assembled.\n\n`j.assembly(…)` places parts by **joints** between named **ports**, frames that the parts carry. A revolute joint leaves one rotational degree of freedom. `.printInPlace(ids, { clearance, pose })` chooses the pose to print in and checks that the parts keep the clearance apart in it. The clamshell below is two 60 × 40 × 10 mm trays on a hinge axis along one long edge, printed open at 180°; the compiler records `h joint → revolute at 180.0° (print pose)` and `base–lid gap → 0.400 mm (minimum distance between components)`, and the assembly has 1 DOF.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nconst tray = (name, y) => j.part(name)\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(60, 40), mm(10), { id: 'body' })\n  .port('hinge', j.frame({ origin: j.pt(0, y, 10),\n    x: j.dir('+Y'), z: j.dir('+X') }))\nreturn j.assembly('clamshell')\n  .add('base', tray('base', 20), { ground: true })\n  .add('lid', tray('lid', 20.4))\n  .joint('h', j.joint.revolute({ limits: [deg(0), deg(180)] }),\n    'base:hinge', 'lid:hinge')\n  .printInPlace(['base', 'lid'], { clearance: mm(0.4), pose: { h: deg(180) } })\n```\n\n**Figure 9.5.** A clamshell of two trays joined by a revolute joint and printed open: the lid is turned 180° about the hinge axis and lies 0.4 mm from the base, the measured minimum distance between the two bodies. One degree of freedom remains, the hinge.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A print-in-place pose\n// Change the pose to deg(150) or the clearance to mm(0.2) and read the gap.\nconst tray = (name, y) => j.part(name)\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(60, 40), mm(10), { id: 'body' })\n  .port('hinge', j.frame({ origin: j.pt(0, y, 10),\n    x: j.dir('+Y'), z: j.dir('+X') }))\nreturn j.assembly('clamshell')\n  .add('base', tray('base', 20), { ground: true })\n  .add('lid', tray('lid', 20.4))\n  .joint('h', j.joint.revolute({ limits: [deg(0), deg(180)] }),\n    'base:hinge', 'lid:hinge')\n  .printInPlace(['base', 'lid'], { clearance: mm(0.4), pose: { h: deg(180) } })\n```\n\n[C-04](lab:preset/C-04) measures the class directly: ten captive rotors with gaps from 0.15 to 0.60 mm, of which the first that turns freely gives your print-in-place clearance. The library builds on the same classes: `j.lib.hinge.pin({ knuckles: 5, clearance: 'printInPlace' })` bores its knuckles to the pin diameter plus twice the gap.\n\n## 9.6 Key points\n\n- A fit class is a clearance per side: press 0.05, snug 0.10, slide 0.20, free 0.30, print-in-place 0.40 mm by default.\n- A fit-aware hole is $d + 2g + c$ and a pin $d - 2g + 2e$; an M3 slide clearance hole is 3.5 mm with the defaults.\n- The defaults are flagged `fit.uncalibrated` until a measured profile replaces them.\n- `j.fit.coupon` prints graded holes and pins; `j.fit.calibrate` fits measured = a · modelled + b by least squares and reads each class's gap from the first hole that gave it.\n- A tolerance stack has three answers: worst case (every limit at once), RSS and Monte Carlo (independent errors); every Monte Carlo sample lies inside the worst-case interval.\n- Print-in-place parts are separate bodies posed by joints; the compiler measures their minimum distance in the print pose.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** With the calibrated profile of Figure 9.3, what does `j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'free' })` model? Compute it, then compile and compare.\n\n> **Exercise.** Tighten every tolerance in the lid stack to ±0.05 mm. What do worst case and RSS give now, and is a slide fit still needed?\n\n> **Exercise.** Change the clamshell's lid offset from 20.4 to 20.2 mm with the clearance left at 0.4 mm. What does the gap decision report?\n\n## Further reading\n\n- [How jig thinks about printing](lab:doc?id=printing): the fit classes and calibration steps as a reference page.\n- [The reference: fit](lab:reference): `j.fit.*` and `j.stack` signatures.\n"},{"file":"10-searching-designs.md","text":"---\npart: IV · Design process\npartAbout: A model is a function of its parameters; searching it is choosing a point, and the compiler's structure makes that cheap.\npresets: E-01, A-06\ndocs: reference\n---\n# Searching designs\n\nA parametric part is a function from its parameters to a solid, and once a solid has a mass, a print time and a load it can carry, choosing the parameters is an optimisation. This chapter treats design that way: a design space as a product of parameter ranges, objectives and requirements as orders on the compiled points, and search as the choice of which points to compile. It also shows why the compiler's structure makes search cheap: requirements that depend only on parameters filter points before compiling, the feature cache makes revisited points nearly free, and closed forms answer some questions with no compile at all. By the end you can write a design space, state what you want from it, and read a search result.\n\n## 10.1 A design space is a product\n\nThe wall hook of [E-01](lab:preset/E-01) is three extrudes: a back plate against the wall, an arm, and an upturned tip. Written as a function of the arm's thickness $t$ and the hook's width $b$, it is a **model function** `hook(t, b)`. A **design space** gives each parameter a range and pairs the ranges with the function:\n\n```js\nj.design({\n  t: j.param.range(mm(4), mm(8), { step: mm(1), default: mm(6) }),\n  b: j.param.range(mm(15), mm(25), { step: mm(5), default: mm(20) }),\n}, p => hook(p.t.value, p.b.value))\n```\n\nThe space is the product of the ranges: with the steps given, 5 values of $t$ times 3 of $b$, 15 points. A script that returns a design compiles to its default point, so the figure below is `hook(6, 20)`: the profile is 540 mm², 20 mm wide, so 10,800 mm³ and 13.39 g of PLA. The Experiments view offers to search any script that returns a design.\n\n```figure\n// view: front\nconst hook = (t, b) => {\n  const back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 50, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', 40, t, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 12, { at: [40 - t, 0] })\n  return j.part('hook').material(j.material('PLA'))\n    .extrude(back.profiles(), b, { id: 'back' })\n    .extrude(arm.profiles(), b, { id: 'arm' })\n    .extrude(tip.profiles(), b, { id: 'tip' })\n}\nreturn j.design({\n  t: j.param.range(mm(4), mm(8), { step: mm(1), default: mm(6) }),\n  b: j.param.range(mm(15), mm(25), { step: mm(5), default: mm(20) }),\n}, p => hook(p.t.value, p.b.value))\n```\n\n**Figure 10.1.** The hook's design space at its default point, $t$ = 6 mm and $b$ = 20 mm, front elevation: a 50 mm back, a 40 mm arm and a 12 mm tip. 10,800 mm³, 13.39 g.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A design space\n// Open it, then run \"Search the design space\" in Experiments.\nconst hook = (t, b) => {\n  const back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 50, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', 40, t, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 12, { at: [40 - t, 0] })\n  return j.part('hook').material(j.material('PLA'))\n    .extrude(back.profiles(), b, { id: 'back' })\n    .extrude(arm.profiles(), b, { id: 'arm' })\n    .extrude(tip.profiles(), b, { id: 'tip' })\n}\nreturn j.design({\n  t: j.param.range(mm(4), mm(8), { step: mm(1), default: mm(6) }),\n  b: j.param.range(mm(15), mm(25), { step: mm(5), default: mm(20) }),\n}, p => hook(p.t.value, p.b.value))\n```\n\nA design space can also have discrete axes, `j.param.choice([...])` and `j.param.int(lo, hi)`, and a configuration table ([Chapter 2](lab:book?ch=2)) is one such axis already written.\n\n## 10.2 Objectives and requirements\n\nAn **objective** orders the compiled points. `j.objective.minimize('mass')` ranks by one metric. Several metrics combine in one of three ways, and they answer different questions:\n\n- **Weighted**: $\\sum_i w_i m_i$, a single score; the weights say how many grams one minute of print time is worth.\n- **Lexicographic**: compare by the first metric, and use the next only to break ties. The orientation of [Chapter 8](lab:book?ch=8) is chosen this way.\n- **Pareto**: keep every point that no other point beats on all metrics at once, and leave the choice among them to a person. In this build `j.objective.pareto` ranks lexicographically; the front is not computed yet.\n\nA **requirement** is different: it rules points out. The hook must carry 100 N at the end of its 40 mm arm. A full analysis would need a finite-element model of an anisotropic print, which jig leaves out; E-01 uses the beam proxy instead. The arm's section is $b \\times t$, its section modulus $Z = bt^2/6$, and with an allowable stress along the layers of 50 MPa the load it carries at the tip is\n\n$$\nF = \\frac{\\sigma Z}{L} = \\frac{50 \\cdot bt^2/6}{40} \\ge 100\\ \\mathrm{N} ⇔ bt^2 \\ge 480\\ \\mathrm{mm^3} .\n$$\n\nThe requirement depends only on the parameters, so it can be checked before compiling. `space.where(p => p.b.value * p.t.value ** 2 >= 480)` drops 4 of the 15 points without building them. Minimising mass over all 15 points picks $t$ = 4, $b$ = 15 at 6.99 g, which carries 50 N. Over the 11 feasible points the best is\n\n| rank | t | b | $bt^2$ | load | mass |\n|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|\n| 1 | 6 mm | 15 mm | 540 mm³ | 112.5 N | 10.044 g |\n| 2 | 5 mm | 20 mm | 500 mm³ | 104.2 N | 11.408 g |\n| 3 | 7 mm | 15 mm | 735 mm³ | 153.1 N | 11.458 g |\n\nThe winner is the thicker, narrower arm. Load grows with $t^2$ and mass with $t$, so for a beam in bending thickness is the cheaper way to buy strength; the search finds what the section modulus predicts.\n\n```figure\n// view: front\nconst hook = (t, b) => {\n  const back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 50, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', 40, t, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 12, { at: [40 - t, 0] })\n  return j.part('hook').material(j.material('PLA'))\n    .extrude(back.profiles(), b, { id: 'back' })\n    .extrude(arm.profiles(), b, { id: 'arm' })\n    .extrude(tip.profiles(), b, { id: 'tip' })\n}\nreturn hook(6, 15)\n```\n\n**Figure 10.2.** The lightest hook that carries 100 N in the grid: $t$ = 6 mm, $b$ = 15 mm, 10.044 g, with a beam-proxy capacity of 112.5 N.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Search the hook, then show the best\n// The grid runs when the script compiles: 15 points, 11 of them built.\nconst hook = (t, b) => {\n  const back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 50, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', 40, t, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 12, { at: [40 - t, 0] })\n  return j.part('hook').material(j.material('PLA'))\n    .extrude(back.profiles(), b, { id: 'back' })\n    .extrude(arm.profiles(), b, { id: 'arm' })\n    .extrude(tip.profiles(), b, { id: 'tip' })\n}\nconst space = j.design({\n  t: j.param.range(mm(4), mm(8), { step: mm(1) }),\n  b: j.param.range(mm(15), mm(25), { step: mm(5) }),\n}, p => hook(p.t.value, p.b.value))\nconst strong = space.where(p => p.b.value * p.t.value ** 2 >= 480)\nconst r = await j.search.grid(strong,\n  { objective: j.objective.minimize('mass') })\nreturn space.at(r.best.point)\n```\n\n## 10.3 Which points to compile\n\nA **grid** search compiles every combination of the steps, so its cost is the product of the step counts, and it grows exponentially with the number of parameters: five steps on six parameters is 15,625 compiles. A **random** search draws points uniformly from the ranges with a seed, so a budget fixes the cost whatever the dimension. With a budget of 12 and seed 3, the feasibility filter kept 9 points and the best was $t$ = 5.71 mm, $b$ = 16.12 mm at 10.337 g: off the grid, and not as light as the grid's best, because nine points cover the feasible boundary sparsely.\n\n**Latin hypercube sampling** (McKay, Beckman and Conover, 1979) sits between the two. It divides each parameter's range into $n$ equal intervals and draws $n$ points so that each interval of each parameter holds exactly one, which spreads a small budget evenly along every axis. In this build `j.search.lhs` draws the same uniform samples as `random`; the stratification is not implemented yet.\n\nWhatever the strategy, the compiler's cache ([Chapter 2](lab:book?ch=2)) is shared across the search. In Node, the 15 cold compiles of the full grid took 7.46 s; searching the 11 feasible points straight after took 4.7 ms, every feature found in the cache. A search that revisits points, or that varies one parameter downstream of others, pays only for what changed.\n\n```figure\n// view: front\nconst hook = (t, b) => {\n  const back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 50, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', 40, t, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 12, { at: [40 - t, 0] })\n  return j.part('hook').material(j.material('PLA'))\n    .extrude(back.profiles(), b, { id: 'back' })\n    .extrude(arm.profiles(), b, { id: 'arm' })\n    .extrude(tip.profiles(), b, { id: 'tip' })\n}\nreturn hook(5.71, 16.12)\n```\n\n**Figure 10.3.** The best of a random search with a budget of 12, rounded to $t$ = 5.71 mm and $b$ = 16.12 mm: 8,337.5 mm³, 10.34 g. It satisfies $bt^2 \\ge 480$ with 525.6 mm³ but is heavier than the grid's best, which a finer search would approach.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Random search with a budget\n// Change budget and seed; compare the best mass with the grid's 10.044 g.\nconst hook = (t, b) => {\n  const back = j.sketch('back', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 50, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const arm = j.sketch('arm', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', 40, t, { at: [0, 0] })\n  const tip = j.sketch('tip', { on: j.plane.XZ })\n    .rect('r', t, 12, { at: [40 - t, 0] })\n  return j.part('hook').material(j.material('PLA'))\n    .extrude(back.profiles(), b, { id: 'back' })\n    .extrude(arm.profiles(), b, { id: 'arm' })\n    .extrude(tip.profiles(), b, { id: 'tip' })\n}\nconst space = j.design({\n  t: j.param.range(mm(4), mm(8)),\n  b: j.param.range(mm(15), mm(25)),\n}, p => hook(p.t.value, p.b.value))\nconst strong = space.where(p => p.b.value * p.t.value ** 2 >= 480)\nconst r = await j.search.random(strong,\n  { budget: 12, seed: 3, objective: j.objective.minimize('mass') })\nreturn space.at(r.best.point)\n```\n\n## 10.4 Closed forms need no search\n\nSome questions have answers that need no search at all. The mass of an extrude is $\\rho A h$ ([Chapter 4](lab:book?ch=4)): linear in $h$. `j.stage(design)` finds such dependence and returns it as a **residual**, a function evaluated without compiling. For a 40 × 30 mm plate in PLA with $h$ in a range, it compiles at two heights and reports\n\n```text\nmass is linear in h (Cavalieri); geometry compiled at h = 4 and 5\nslope 1.488 g/mm, intercept 0 g\n```\n\nand `residual.mass(7)` is 10.416 g. The slope is $\\rho A = 1.24\\ \\mathrm{g/cm^3} \\times 1200\\ \\mathrm{mm^2} = 1.488$ g/mm, and the intercept 0 to within $2 \\times 10^{-15}$ g. The staging rule of the spec generalises this: known pure work is compiled and cached; finite unknowns, such as configurations or standard sizes, are tabulated; continuous unknowns get a residual where a closed form exists.\n\n```figure\n// view: axo\nreturn j.design({\n  h: j.param.range(mm(2), mm(10), { default: mm(4) }),\n}, p => j.part('plate').material(j.material('PLA'))\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(40, 30), p.h, { id: 'body' }))\n```\n\n**Figure 10.4.** A 40 × 30 mm plate whose height is a range from 2 to 10 mm, at its default of 4 mm. Its mass at any height is $1.488\\,h$ g, read off two compiles.\n\n```sketch\n// title: A design with a closed form\n// In the agent: j.stage(design).residual.mass(mm(7)) gives 10.416 g.\nreturn j.design({\n  h: j.param.range(mm(2), mm(10), { default: mm(4) }),\n}, p => j.part('plate').material(j.material('PLA'))\n  .extrude(j.region.rect(40, 30), p.h, { id: 'body' }))\n```\n\n## 10.5 Laws keep the search honest\n\nA search trusts thousands of compiles it never looks at, so the compiler must be right in ways no single compile shows. jig states those ways as **laws**, property tests over random inputs, each with what it pays for:\n\n| law | checks | pays for |\n|---|---|---|\n| `extrude.volume` | $V = Ah$ | the closed form for mass in staging |\n| `revolve.volume` | $V = \\thetar̄ A$ | a kernel test of revolve |\n| `brep.eulerPoincare` | $V - E + F - (L - F) - 2(S - G) = 0$ | catching an invalid solid after a boolean |\n| `compile.deterministic` | the same model hashes the same | caching, and reproducible search |\n| `shape.boolean` | inclusion–exclusion on volume | rewriting chains of booleans |\n\n`j.laws.check(j.laws.suite, { n: 8, seed: 1 })` runs each law on 8 generated inputs; in this build all five pass. Heuristics such as the 45° overhang rule or the fit classes are not laws: they are calibrated, and a law that encoded them would only test the calibration. Determinism is the law a search depends on most. Every random strategy takes a seed, so a search run twice gives the same points, the same compiles and the same best.\n\n```sketch\n// title: Run the laws in a compile\n// A failing law throws, so the compile fails with its name.\nconst results = j.laws.check(j.laws.suite, { n: 4, seed: 1 })\nconst failed = results.filter(r => !r.ok)\nif (failed.length) {\n  throw new Error(`laws failed: ${failed.map(r => r.law).join(', ')}`)\n}\nreturn j.part('passed').extrude(j.region.rect(20, 20), mm(2), { id: 'body' })\n```\n\n## 10.6 Key points\n\n- A design space pairs parameter ranges with a model function; a script that returns one compiles to its default point.\n- Objectives order compiled points: weighted sums, lexicographic tie-breaks, or Pareto fronts (not yet computed in this build).\n- A requirement that depends only on parameters filters points before compiling; the hook's 100 N becomes $bt^2 \\ge 480$ mm³.\n- For a beam in bending, load grows with $t^2$ and mass with $t$, so the search prefers the thick, narrow arm.\n- Grid search costs the product of the steps; random search costs its budget; Latin hypercube sampling spreads a budget evenly but is not stratified yet in this build.\n- The feature cache is shared across a search, so revisited points cost milliseconds.\n- Where the metric is linear in a parameter, staging returns a closed form and no search is needed.\n- Laws (`extrude.volume`, `brep.eulerPoincare`, `compile.deterministic`, …) are what make thousands of unseen compiles trustworthy.\n\n## Exercises\n\n> **Exercise.** Change the requirement to 150 N. What is the new constraint on $bt^2$, which grid point wins, and does the ranking still favour thickness?\n\n> **Exercise.** Write a weighted objective that values one gram as much as one minute of print time, for the hook in a print job. Does the best point change?\n\n> **Exercise.** Use `j.stage` on a design whose range parameter is the hook's width $b$. Is the mass linear in $b$? Is it linear in $t$, and what would `j.stage` have to do differently if it were not?\n\n## Further reading\n\n- McKay, Beckman and Conover, *A comparison of three methods for selecting values of input variables in the analysis of output from a computer code*, Technometrics 21(2), 1979: Latin hypercube sampling.\n- [E-01 Search a wall hook](lab:preset/E-01): the same search as a preset, with its results in Experiments.\n"}],"docs":{"getting-started":"---\nid: getting-started\ntitle: The first ten minutes\ngroup: Start here\nabout: Open a preset, read the drafting sheet, change a parameter, select a face and read its name, export a 3MF.\norder: 1\n---\n# The first ten minutes\n\njigcad is a workbench for **jig**, a language for describing parts to be 3D printed. A part is a short JavaScript script; the workbench compiles it with OpenCascade, draws the result as a technical drawing, and shows every number the compile produced. This page walks through one preset from opening it to exporting a file for the slicer, in the order you would do it.\n\n> **Try it.** Do each step as you read it: [open A-01](lab:preset/A-01), and keep the stage beside this page.\n\n## Open a preset from the shelf\n\nThe button at the top left names the open preset, for example *A-01 A box with walls ▾*. It opens the **shelf**: every preset, by group, each with a thumbnail compiled when it scrolls into view, its difficulty and its size. Type to filter (`box`, `first print`, `D-0`), then click a row or press Enter.\n\nA preset is a script, a page of notes and a few settings for the stage. Opening one loads all three and compiles the script. The shelf is grouped by letter: first prints (S), around the house (H), desk and storage (D), workshop and repair (W), mechanisms (M), calibration prints (C), the foundations of the language (A) and design process (E).\n\n## Read the sheet and the pills\n\nTwo pills in the header summarise the compile. For A-01 the first reads `ok · 2 features · 0 DOF · 1 body`: the script compiled, it has two features (an extrude and a shell), its sketches leave no **degree of freedom** unfixed, and it made one solid. The second reads `15.3 cm³ · 19 g`: the volume, and the mass in the part's material, PLA at 1.24 g/cm³. Click the first pill to open the feature graph, or the code at the error when there is one.\n\nThe **stage** draws the part on a drafting sheet in orthographic projection, so one scale holds for every length parallel to the sheet, and the scale bar at the bottom left is valid anywhere on it. The buttons above it choose the view: *Axo* (axonometric), *Front*, *Side* and *Plan*. Drag to orbit, Shift-drag to pan, use the wheel to zoom, and *Square up* to return to the named view. The blue dimensions are the bounding box, 60 × 40 × 30 mm here. *Show ▾* turns overlays on and off: shadows, grid, names, dims, hidden edges, overhangs, the bed, normals and sketch DOF.\n\n> **Try it.** [Look at it from the front](lab:view/stage?view=front). The front elevation shows the 30 mm height and the 60 mm width at full scale; the hatched band is the ground.\n\n## Change a parameter\n\nThe **Params** view lists every parameter. The exposed ones come first as sliders with the labels the script gave them (*outside width*, *outside depth*, *height*, *wall thickness*); below them is every parameter with its value, its expression, the layer it came from and the parameters that depend on it. A change recompiles at once.\n\n> **Try it.** [Make it 80 mm wide](lab:param/w=80). The pills change to 19.2 cm³ and 24 g. `inner_w`, defined as `w - 2 * wall`, follows to 76 mm without being touched.\n\nThen [open the trace](lab:show/trace). It lists what the compile did and how long each step took. When only `wall` changes, the extrude is marked *cached*: measured in Node on the build machine, the first compile took 512 ms and a change of wall from 2 to 2.5 mm took 334 ms, because the extrude was reused and only the shell ran again. [Chapter 2](lab:book?ch=2) explains why that is safe.\n\nTo change something that is not a parameter, edit the script in the **Code** view and press *Run* (Ctrl+Enter). A script error puts a red pill in the header and a marker at the line; the stage keeps drawing the last part that compiled.\n\n## Select a face and read its name\n\nClick a face on the sheet.
