Read it from the inside out: `base.outline.bottom` is the bottom edge of the rectangle `outline` in the sketch `base`; `body/side[…]` is the side face the extrude `body` swept from that edge; `shell/inner[…]` is the inner face the shell offset from that side. The face's area is 2,072 mm² at 80 mm wide. Change the width again and the name stays the same, which is why a fillet or a hole that refers to it keeps working. The [naming doc](lab:doc?id=naming) explains how names are made, and [Chapter 6](lab:book?ch=6) why they survive edits.\n\nWith no face selected, the panel lists every face by name; click one to select it.\n\n## Export a 3MF\n\n*Export ▾* in the header offers five formats:\n\n| item | gives | what the report says it drops or approximates |\n|---|---|---|\n| STL | a mesh of every body | units (assumed mm), names, colours, plates, settings; the mesh deviation |\n| 3MF | a mesh with units, plates, part names and settings | parametric history, persistent names, joints; the mesh deviation |\n| STEP | the exact B-rep (AP242) | persistent names, plates, slicer settings, fit provenance |\n| IR bundle | L0 to L6 as text in one .json | nothing: it is the compiler's own record |\n| Script | `script.js` with your edits | nothing |\n\nChoose **3MF** for a slicer: it keeps millimetres, so the part cannot arrive 25.4 times too large, and it keeps the plates. For A-01 at 80 mm wide the file is `box.3mf`, 36,025 bytes, and its report says the mesh stays within 0.094 mm of the true surface; the 3 mm corner arcs are where it deviates most. Where the page cannot save files, 3MF is greyed out and the text formats (STL, STEP, IR, Script) are copied to the clipboard instead.\n\n## Where next\n\n- [The jig book](lab:book?ch=1) starts from quantities and units and builds up to printing and search.\n- [How jig thinks about printing](lab:doc?id=printing): orientation, overhangs, teardrops, fit and slicing.\n- [The reference](lab:reference) lists every `j.*` name with its status.\n- [S-01 Cable clip for a desk edge](lab:preset/S-01) is the simplest part on the shelf: one sketch, one extrude.\n","naming":"---\nid: naming\ntitle: Persistent names and genealogy\ngroup: Modelling\nabout: How faces get names like body/side[base.outline.bottom], and why selectors that use them survive edits.\norder: 20\n---\n# Persistent names and genealogy\n\nEvery face and edge the compiler makes gets a **persistent name**, built from the feature that made it and the thing it was made from: `body/side[base.outline.bottom]`, `shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]`, `round/face[body/side[base.r.left] ∩ body/end]`. Selectors resolve through these names, so a fillet that refers to \"the end face of `body`\" still finds it after the part is made wider, taller or rounder. This page explains the problem names solve, how they are built, and when they break. [Chapter 6](lab:book?ch=6) works through it with figures.\n\n## The problem\n\nOpenCascade, like every boundary-representation kernel, stores a solid as lists of faces, edges and vertices, and numbers them in the order it built them. The numbers are not stable. Make a box wider and the kernel may build its faces in the same order; add a pocket, a fillet or a hole and the order changes, faces split in two, and face 7 is now a different face. A model that says \"fillet the edges of face 7\" breaks silently: it fillets the wrong edges, or none. This is the **topological naming problem**, and it is the most common way parametric models fail when edited.\n\njig avoids face numbers altogether. A face is identified by its history, which does not change when dimensions do.\n\n## How a name is built\n\nA name has the form `<feature>/<role>[<source>]`:\n\n| part | is | examples |\n|---|---|---|\n| feature | the id of the feature that created the face | `body`, `shell`, `round`, `holes` |\n| role | what the face is to that feature | `side`, `start`, `end` (extrude, revolve, sweep); `inner` (shell); `face` (fillet, chamfer); `bore`, `floor`, `tear.l`, `tear.r` (holes) |\n| source | what the face was made from | a sketch entity `base.outline.bottom`; another face; an edge as the intersection of two faces `a ∩ b`; a hole's point `p1` |\n\nSo `body/side[base.outline.bottom]` is the side face the extrude `body` swept from the bottom edge of the rectangle `outline` in the sketch `base`. `body/end` is its far cap and `body/start` its near cap. A fillet face is named by the edge it replaced, and an edge is named by the two faces it separates: `round/face[body/side[base.r.left] ∩ body/end]`. Pattern copies carry their instance: `body/side[outline.h1.p2]`. A face that a later feature splits in two keeps its name with `#a` and `#b`: a groove across the top of a block leaves `body/end#a` and `body/end#b`, 1,020 mm² each.\n\nThe record of which feature made which face from what is the **genealogy**. Each feature reports it as it runs: faces created, faces modified, faces deleted. The approach is Kripac's (1997): name by history, and resolve a reference by following the history forward from the face it originally meant.\n\n## Selectors that use names\n\nA selector is a query that the compiler resolves on every compile. Two kinds behave differently under edits:\n\n- **Feature selectors** resolve through genealogy: `j.feat('body').endFace()`, `.startFace()`, `.sideFaces()`, `.sideFace('outline.left')`, `.holes()`, `.bores()`, and `j.sel.names(kind, [names])`. They mean the same faces whatever the dimensions.\n- **Query selectors** resolve from geometry: `j.sel.faces('>Z')` is whichever face is highest along Z now. That is often what you want, and it can change meaning when the model changes.\n\nAn example. A 60 × 40 × 5 mm plate has a boss 16 mm across and 12 mm tall on top. Before the boss existed, `j.sel.faces('>Z')` was the top of the plate; now it resolves to `boss/end`, the top of the boss. `j.feat('plate').endFace()` still resolves to `plate/end`. `why` shows both:\n\n```text\nj.sel.faces('>Z')          → boss/end    '>Z' → 1 of 13\nj.feat('plate').endFace()  → plate/end   1 by genealogy\n```\n\nSelectors also return exactly what they say. `j.feat('plate').endFace().edges()` is every edge of the plate's top face, and that includes the circle where the boss meets it: a chamfer on that selection also chamfers the root of the boss. Add `.where(e => e.convex)` to keep only the outside edges.\n\n## Asking why\n\n`why` is the engine's explanation of a reference. In the workbench, select a face and read *Why* in the inspector; in a script or the agent, `prog.why(target)` takes a face name, an edge name, a feature id, a param or a selector. For `body/end#a` it answers:\n\n```text\nbody/end#a: piece a of a face split by a later feature\nbody/end ← body: the far cap of the extrusion\n```\n\n## When references break\n\nNames survive dimension changes, and they survive features added downstream. They do not survive the removal of what they were made from: rename the sketch entity `outline` and every `body/side[base.outline.…]` is gone. The compiler reports each kind of break:\n\n| diagnostic | level | when |\n|---|---|---|\n| `ref.lost` | warning | a selector resolved to nothing: `j.feat('body').sideFace('r.leftt').edges('\\|Z') resolved to nothing` |\n| `ref.fragile` | warning | a selector depends on order: `nth(0) depends on face order` |\n| `ref.expect` | error | `.expect(n)` counted something else |\n| `ref.autoId` | info | a reference to a generated feature id such as `extrude1` |\n\n`nth` deserves its warning. On a 40 × 30 × 10 mm block whose left side has been filleted, `j.sel.edges('|Z').nth(0)` picks whichever vertical edge comes first, which happened to be an edge the fillet had already rounded, and the chamfer on it failed. Prefer a feature selector, and put `.expect(n)` on any selector whose count you know, such as `.edges('|Z').expect(4)` for the four vertical edges of a box: a model that changes under it then fails loudly at the selector instead of quietly downstream.\n\n## Names in the rest of the workbench\n\nThe same names appear wherever faces do: in the inspector, in the IR at level L4, in `lab:select/<name>` links in notes and the book, and in the agent's eval scope. Exports that keep names are the IR bundle; STL, 3MF and STEP drop them, and their reports say so.\n","printing":"---\nid: printing\ntitle: How jig thinks about printing\ngroup: Printing\nabout: Orientation, overhangs, teardrops, bed edges, fit classes and calibration, slicing and cusp height, exports.\norder: 10\n---\n# How jig thinks about printing\n\nA fused-filament printer lays down a plastic line about 0.45 mm wide, one layer of about 0.2 mm at a time, each layer resting on the one below. Almost every rule in this page follows from that: material needs something under it, round holes print undersized, and curved surfaces come out as stairs. jig turns each rule into a default the compiler applies and records, so the Program says what it decided and why. The numbers below come from compiles in this build; [Chapter 8](lab:book?ch=8) and [Chapter 9](lab:book?ch=9) derive them.\n\n## The print job\n\nA part becomes printable inside a **print job**: a printer, a material, the parts, and what to do with them.\n\n```js\nreturn j.print('bracket', {\n  printer: j.printer('mk4'), material: j.material('PETG'), parts: [bracket],\n}).orient('auto').slice({ layer: mm(0.2) })\n```\n\n`j.printer` knows `generic-fdm`, `mk4` and `mini` (0.45 mm lines, 0.2 mm layers), `x1c` (0.42 mm lines) and `generic-msla` (0.05 mm), and accepts your own: `j.printer({ bed: [180, 180, 180], nozzle: mm(0.6), lw: mm(0.68) })`. The printer also resolves the symbolic units: a wall of `lw(3)` is 1.35 mm on an mk4 and 1.26 mm on an X1C. A part returned without a print job still compiles and exports; it is checked as if printed on `generic-fdm` with +Z up.\n\n## Orientation\n\nThe build direction decides which faces need support, how much touches the bed and how tall the print is. `orient('auto')`, the default, tries 26 directions (the faces, edges and corners of a cube) and ranks them **lexicographically**: least support volume first, then most bed contact, then least height. A later criterion only breaks ties in the earlier ones. The decision records the winner and the runner-up. For the L-bracket of [Chapter 8](lab:book?ch=8), a 60 × 30 × 5 mm base with a 40 mm upright:\n\n| direction | support | bed contact | height | how it was chosen |\n|---|--:|--:|--:|---|\n| +Z (auto) | 0 mm³ | 1,765 mm² | 45 mm | lexicographic winner; next was +Y with 51 mm³ of support |\n| −Z | 20,516 mm³ | 300 mm² | 45 mm | fixed; 1,465 mm² overhangs |\n| +Y | 0 mm³ | 2,650 mm² | 30 mm | `'min-height'` |\n\nPass a direction (`'+Y'`), a strategy (`'min-height'`, `'max-contact'`) or a function `(part, ctx) => dir` whose `ctx.default(part)` is the automatic choice.\n\n## Overhangs and supports\n\nA face whose downward normal is more than **45°** from vertical prints over air: each layer sticks out more than about half a line width past the one below, and the line sags. The 45° figure is a calibrated threshold, not a law: the printer profile carries it (`maxOverhang: 45`, 30° for MSLA), and a print job reports what exceeds it as `print.overhang` with an estimate of tree supports. Turn on *Overhang* under *Show ▾* to hatch those faces on the sheet, and print [C-02](lab:preset/C-02) to find your printer's own limit.\n\nDesign out overhangs where you can: a 45° point at the top of a window (D-01) needs no support; a flat ceiling does. Bridges, flat spans with support at both ends, print up to about 10 mm in PLA (`bridge: 10` in the profile); [C-03](lab:preset/C-03) measures yours.\n\n## Teardrops\n\nA round hole whose axis lies in the build plane has a ceiling that is flat at its top. jig turns every such hole into a **teardrop**: the circle plus two 45° flats meeting in a point above it, so no part of the ceiling overhangs more than 45°. The decision reads `axis +Y ⟂ build direction +Z → 45° teardrop, tip toward +Z`, and the new faces are named `<hole>/tear.l[…]` and `<hole>/tear.r[…]`.\n\nBecause the hole's shape depends on the build direction and the direction depends on the shape, the compiler iterates: orient, re-shape the holes, orient again, until nothing changes (at most four rounds). Turning the bracket to `'min-height'` converges in 2: the cable hole becomes round, because it is now vertical, and the two screw holes become teardrops.\n\n## Bed edges\n\nThe first layer is pressed onto the bed and spreads a little, the **elephant's foot**, about 0.2 mm. A 45° chamfer of 0.4 to 0.6 mm on the edges that touch the bed takes it up and needs no support. A fillet there does the opposite: it starts horizontal, so its lowest band overhangs. On a 40 × 30 × 10 mm block, 2 mm fillets on the bed edges leave 204.7 mm² past 45° and 12 mm³ of support; 2 mm chamfers leave none. The desk presets add the chamfer explicitly:\n\n```js\n.chamfer(j.feat('body').startFace().edges(), mm(0.6), { id: 'foot' })\n```\n\n## Fit classes and calibration\n\nPrinted holes come out smaller than modelled and pins larger, by amounts that depend on the printer, the material and the slicer. jig sizes mating features from **fit classes**, a clearance per side, plus a hole compensation:\n\n| class | gap per side | use |\n|---|--:|---|\n| press | 0.05 mm | magnets, dowels that must not move |\n| snug | 0.10 mm | parts that hold by friction |\n| slide | 0.20 mm | lids, pins that turn |\n| free | 0.30 mm | loose screws |\n| printInPlace | 0.40 mm | hinges printed assembled |\n\n`j.fit.hole(mm(3), 'slide')` models 3 + 2 × 0.2 + 0.1 = 3.5 mm, which is also what `j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit: 'slide' })` drills. These defaults are starting points, and every fit-aware feature says so with the info `fit.uncalibrated` until you replace them. To calibrate, print [C-01](lab:preset/C-01) or `j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole' })`: ten holes from 6.1 to 7.0 mm round a 6 mm pin, and ten loose pins. Measure the holes, note which pin fits how, and give the rows to `j.fit.calibrate`, which fits `actual = a · nominal + b` by least squares and reads a gap for each class from the first hole with that fit. Put the result in `j.fit.profile(name, { classes, holeComp })` and attach it with `.fit(profile)`. [Chapter 9](lab:book?ch=9) does this end to end. [C-04](lab:preset/C-04) finds the print-in-place gap, [C-05](lab:preset/C-05) how small your holes print, round and as teardrops, and [C-06](lab:preset/C-06) which wall thicknesses your slicer fills.\n\n## Slicing and cusp height\n\n`.slice({ layer })` cuts the oriented part into planar sections at the middle of each layer, measures each, and reports layers, filament, mass as printed and print time. Two numbers describe the result:\n\n- The sum of section area × layer height approximates the volume. For a 40 mm dome it falls 258 mm³ (0.19 %) short at every layer height from 0.1 to 0.4 mm, because each section's circles are sampled as polygons within 0.05 mm; the layer height itself contributes under 2 mm³.\n- The **cusp height**, the depth of the stair between two layers on a surface, is $c = t\\,|\\cos\\theta|$ for layer height $t$ and a face whose normal makes angle $\\theta$ with the build direction (Dolenc and Mäkelä, 1994). Vertical walls have none; shallow slopes have nearly a full layer. The dome's area-weighted mean is exactly $t/2$: 0.1 mm at 0.2 mm layers.\n\nHalving the layer height halves the cusp and roughly doubles the print time: 69.7 min at 0.4 mm, 115.9 min at 0.2 mm and 208.5 min at 0.1 mm for the dome. Adaptive layers are not in this build; G-code is not either, so the slicer of your choice does the toolpaths.\n\n## Exports\n\n*Export ▾* or `j.export.*` gives STL, 3MF, STEP, an SVG preview and the IR bundle, each with a **report** of what it kept, approximated and dropped. Send a slicer **3MF**: it keeps millimetres, plates and part names. STL drops units, so a slicer has to assume millimetres. STEP is the exact B-rep for other CAD programs. The mesh exports measure their deviation from the true surface and report it; it is largest on small arcs.\n\n## Checking a part\n\n`.use(j.rules.fdm)` on a part runs the FDM rules after every compile: overhangs past 45°, bed fit, holes under 1.0 mm, walls under 0.9 mm, horizontal holes without teardrops, first layers under 100 mm². Each finding is a diagnostic with a fix. A thin-walled cup with a 0.6 mm wall and a 0.6 mm vent hole gets `print.thinWall` and `print.thinFeature`. The rules are checks of thresholds, not guarantees: a part that passes can still warp, and one that fails can still print on a well-tuned machine.\n","reference":"---\nid: reference\ntitle: Reference\ngroup: Start here\nabout: Every j.* name in this build, by module: signature, what it does, status and a short example.\norder: 0\n---\n# Reference\n\nEvery name in the `j` namespace that this build of jigcad implements, grouped by module, with its signature, what it does, its status and a short example. Units are millimetres and radians inside the engine; a bare number in a length slot is millimetres and in an angle slot is degrees. A string in a slot is an expression over the part's params.\n\nStatus: ✓ works end to end and is tested · ◐ partial, with the limit stated · ✗ not in this build (it throws, or compiles to a `feature.unsupported` diagnostic that says so).\n\n## Scripts and bare names\n\nA preset's `script.js`, a figure in the Book and a sketch opened from it are all the **body of a function** that returns a builder: a part, sketch, assembly, print job, region, shape or design. These names are in scope:\n\n```text\nj  mm cm m um inch ft  deg rad turn  kg gram  N kN  MPa GPa\ns minute hour  degC  usd eur  lw layer nozzle bbox  data\n```\n\n`data` holds the preset's data files, parsed: `.json` as an object, `.csv` as `{ columns, rows }`, anything else as a string. `in` is a JavaScript keyword, hence `inch`. Do not redeclare these names, and avoid `m`, `s`, `N` and `layer` as local variables.\n\n```js\n// the smallest complete script: one part, one feature\nreturn j.part('plate')\n  .params({ w: mm(60), d: mm(40), t: mm(4) })\n  .extrude(j.region.rect('w', 'd'), 't', { id: 'body' })\n```\n\nBuilders are immutable: every method returns a new builder, and there is no `build()`. Every slot accepts nothing (the default), a value, a named strategy, or a lambda `(input, ctx) => out` whose `ctx.default` is the default.\n\n## Units\n\n- `mm cm m um inch ft` ✓: lengths, canonical unit mm. Example: `inch(0.25)` is 6.35 mm.\n- `deg rad turn` ✓: angles, canonical unit rad. Example: `turn(1)` is 2π rad.\n- `kg gram` ✓: mass, canonical g. Example: `gram(12)`.\n- `N kN MPa GPa` ✓: force and stress. Example: `MPa(50)`.\n- `s minute hour` ✓: time, canonical s. Example: `minute(42)`.\n- `degC` ✓: temperature difference. Example: `degC(20)`.\n- `usd eur` ✓: money; `j.units.rates.eur` is usd per eur (1.08). Example: `eur(10).to('usd')` is 10.8 usd.\n- `lw(n)` ✓: n extrusion line widths, resolved by the printer. Example: `lw(3)` is 1.35 mm on mk4.\n- `layer(n)` ✓: n layer heights, resolved by the printer. Example: `layer(5)` is 1 mm at 0.2 mm.\n- `nozzle(n)` ✓: n nozzle diameters. Example: `nozzle(1)`.\n- `bbox(n)` ✓: n times the model's bounding-box diagonal. Example: `bbox(0.05)`.\n- `j.q(text | number | ctx => Q)` ✓: parse a quantity or wrap a lambda. Example: `j.q('1/4 in + 2 mm')`.\n- `j.q.sqrt sin cos tan asin acos atan2 min max` ✓: maths on quantities; trig takes angles. Example: `j.q.sin(deg(30))` is 0.5.\n- `j.units` ✓: every unit constructor, plus `rates`. Example: `j.units.mm(3)`.\n- `j.coerce(x, kind, scope?)` ✓: a bare number to mm, degrees or plain. Example: `j.coerce(30, 'angle')` is 30°.\n- `j.Quantity` `j.Toleranced` ✓: the classes, for `instanceof`. Example: `x instanceof j.Quantity`.\n- `j.DimensionError` ✓: thrown by arithmetic on unlike dimensions. Example: `mm(2).add(deg(3))` throws.\n\nA quantity's methods:\n\n- `.value` `.dims` `.unit` ✓: canonical number, dimension exponents, display unit. Example: `mm(3).dims` is `{ L: 1 }`.\n- `.add .sub .mul .div .neg .abs` ✓: arithmetic; `mul` adds exponents. Example: `mm(2).mul(mm(3))` is 6 mm².\n- `.min .max .clamp(lo, hi) .pow .sqrt` ✓: more arithmetic. Example: `mm(4).pow(2)`.\n- `.to(unit)` `.in(unit)` ✓: convert; `in` returns a number. Example: `inch(1).in('mm')` is 25.4.\n- `.gt .gte .lt .lte .eq(b, tol?)` ✓: comparisons, dimension-checked. Example: `mm(3).gt(mm(2))`.\n- `.resolve(ctx)` ✓: fix symbolic units: `{ lw, layer, nozzle, bboxDiag }`. Example: `lw(3).resolve({ lw: mm(0.42) })`.\n- `.isSymbolic .isLazy` ✓: whether it still needs a printer. Example: `lw(2).isSymbolic` is true.\n- `.pm(t)` `.tol(lo, hi)` ✓: a toleranced value for stacks. Example: `mm(10).pm(0.1)`.\n- `.toString()` ✓: text with unit. Example: `lw(3).add(mm(0.1))` prints `3lw + 0.1mm`.\n\n## Expressions and parameters\n\n- `.params({ … })` ✓: declare params on any builder; values are quantities, numbers, booleans, expression strings or lambdas. Example: `.params({ wall: lw(3), boss: 'wall * 4' })`.\n- `.configs({ name: { … } } | j.table(…))` ✓: a finite set of overrides; with none chosen, the middle row. Example: `.configs({ S: { h: mm(30) }, L: { h: mm(55) } })`.\n- `.config(name)` ✓: choose a configuration (still a value). Example: `.config('L')`.\n- `.override({ … })` ✓: the highest-precedence layer in the script. Example: `.override({ wall: mm(2.5) })`.\n- `.expose(names, { labels, ranges, steps })` ✓: the sliders in Params. Example: `.expose(['h'], { ranges: { h: [mm(10), mm(80)] } })`.\n- `j.expr(text)` ✓: an expression value. Example: `j.expr('wall * 2 + 1 mm')`.\n- `j.ref(name)` ✓: a reference to one param. Example: `j.ref('wall')`.\n- `j.lit(text)` ✓: a string kept as text, not parsed. Example: `j.lit('w * 2')`.\n- `j.table(csv | rows, { key })` ✓: a design table for `.configs`. Example: `j.table('name,d\\nS,10\\nL,20')`.\n- `j.param.expose` ✓: the expose metadata helper. Example: `j.param.expose(['h'])`.\n- `j.Expr j.ExprError j.ParamCycleError` ✓: classes and errors. Example: `e instanceof j.ExprError`.\n\nExpressions take units after numbers (`2 mm`, `1/4 in`, `25 mm^2`, `30°`), names with dots (`gap.slide`), the functions `min max abs sqrt sin cos tan asin acos atan atan2 floor ceil round clamp hypot pow lerp` and the constant `pi`. Precedence, low to high: library default, `.params`, a config row, `.override`, the Params view. A cycle is the diagnostic `param.cycle` with its path (`wall → floor → wall`); unlike dimensions are `units.dimension` (`'w - 3': cannot subtract 1 (dimensionless) from mm (L¹)`).\n\n## Geometry\n\n- `j.pt(x, y, z?)` ✓: a point; `p.sub(q)` is a vector, `p.add(p)` throws `AffineError`. Example: `j.pt(10, 0, 5)`.\n- `j.vec(x, y, z?)` ✓: a vector: `add sub mul div dot cross norm unit angleTo`. Example: `j.vec(1, 0, 0).cross(j.vec(0, 1, 0))`.\n- `j.dir('+Z' | x, y, z)` ✓: a unit direction. Example: `j.dir('-X')`.\n- `j.origin` ✓: the point (0, 0, 0). Example: `j.origin`.\n- `j.tx.identity` ✓: the identity transform. Example: `j.tx.identity`.\n- `j.tx.move(v | x, y, z)` ✓: a translation. Example: `j.tx.move(0, 0, 5)`.\n- `j.tx.rotate(axis, angle)` ✓: a rotation about an axis. Example: `j.tx.rotate(j.axis.Z, deg(60))`.\n- `j.tx.mirror(plane | axis)` ✓: a reflection; `det` is −1. Example: `j.tx.mirror(j.plane.YZ)`.\n- `j.tx.scale(k | [3], { about })` ✓: a scaling. Example: `j.tx.scale(2)`.\n- `j.tx.align(from, to)` ✓: the motion taking one frame to another. Example: `j.tx.align(j.plane.XY, j.plane.XZ)`.\n- `j.tx.matrix(arr)` ✓: a transform from 16 numbers, column-major. Example: `j.tx.matrix(m)`.\n- `t.then(u) .inverse() .pow(n) .apply(x) .det` ✓: compose (first t, then u), invert, apply. Example: `a.then(b).apply(j.pt(1, 0, 0))`.\n- `j.plane.XY .XZ .YZ` ✓: the base planes; XZ's normal is −Y. Example: `j.plane.XZ`.\n- `j.plane.offset(p, d)` ✓: a parallel plane; `d` may be an expression. Example: `j.plane.offset(j.plane.XY, 'h')`.\n- `j.plane.angle(p, axis, a)` ✓: a plane turned about an axis. Example: `j.plane.angle(j.plane.XY, j.axis.X, deg(30))`.\n- `j.plane.through(a, b, c)` ✓: the plane through three points. Example: `j.plane.through(p, q, r)`.\n- `j.plane.on(faceSel)` ✓: the plane of a planar face. Example: `j.plane.on(j.feat('body').endFace())`.\n- `j.plane.mid(a, b)` `j.plane.normalTo(sel, { at })` ✓: mid-plane; plane normal to a curve. Example: `j.plane.mid(f1, f2)`.\n- `j.axis.X .Y .Z` ✓: the base axes. Example: `j.axis.Z`.\n- `j.axis.through(p, q)` `j.axis.of(sel)` ✓: an axis through points, or of a cylinder, edge or circle. Example: `j.axis.of(j.feat('bore').faces())`.\n- `j.frame({ origin, x, z })` ✓: a frame (planes are frames). Example: `j.frame({ origin: j.origin, x: j.dir('+Y'), z: j.dir('+X') })`.\n- `j.frame.on(sel, { flip })` ✓: the frame of a face. Example: `j.frame.on(j.feat('body').endFace())`.\n- `j.point.center(sel)` `j.point.on(sel, { at })` ✓: a point from topology. Example: `j.point.center(j.feat('boss').endFace())`.\n- `frame.offset .rotate .flip .moved .toWorld .toLocal` ✓: frame operations. Example: `j.plane.XY.offset(mm(5))`.\n- `j.Point j.Vec j.Dir j.Transform j.Frame j.Axis j.AffineError` ✓: classes and errors. Example: `p instanceof j.Point`.\n\n## Patterns\n\nA pattern is a group acting on a feature or on sketch entities. Its instance count is known before any geometry is built.\n\n- `j.pattern.linear(dir, { count, step | extent })` ✓: copies along a direction. Example: `j.pattern.linear(j.dir('+X'), { count: 4, step: mm(10) })`.\n- `j.pattern.circular(axis, { count, angle })` ✓: copies about an axis; `angle` defaults to a full turn. Example: `j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 6 })`.\n- `j.pattern.grid(dA, dB, { count: [a, b], step: [sa, sb] })` ✓: a rectangular grid. Example: `j.pattern.grid(j.dir('+X'), j.dir('+Y'), { count: [3, 2], step: [20, 20] })`.\n- `j.pattern.hex(plane, { pitch, rings | count })` ✓: a hexagonal lattice; `rings: 3` has 37 cells. Example: `j.pattern.hex(j.plane.XY, { pitch: mm(10), rings: 3 })`.\n- `j.pattern.path(sel, { count, align })` ✓: copies along a curve. Example: `j.pattern.path(edge, { count: 8 })`.\n- `j.pattern.mirror(planeOrAxis)` ✓: one reflected copy. Example: `j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.Y)`.\n- `j.pattern.points(pts)` ✓: copies at listed points. Example: `j.pattern.points([[0, 0], [20, 0]])`.\n- `p.times(q)` ✓: the product: counts multiply. Example: `mx.times(my).count` is 4.\n- `p.filter((inst, ctx) => bool)` `p.skip([i])` ✓: drop instances. Example: `hex.filter(c => Math.hypot(c.at.x, c.at.y) < 20)`.\n- `p.vary((inst, ctx) => ({ … }))` ✓: per-instance feature params. Example: `p.vary(i => ({ extent: mm(2 + i.index) }))`.\n- `p.count p.closed p.eq(q)` ✓: size, whether a full turn, equality. Example: `j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 6 }).closed`.\n- `p.expand(ctx)` `p.instances(ctx)` ✓: the instances with their transforms; coincident ones merge (`pattern.dedupe`). Example: `p.expand({ base: j.pt(20, 0, 0) })`.\n\n## Regions and primitives\n\n- `j.region.rect(w, h, { center = true, at, r })` ✓: a rectangle, optionally rounded. Example: `j.region.rect(60, 40, { r: 3 })`.\n- `j.region.circle(r, { at })` ✓: a disc. Example: `j.region.circle('d / 2')`.\n- `j.region.polygon(pts)` ✓: a polygon. Example: `j.region.polygon([[0, 0], [10, 0], [0, 10]])`.\n- `j.region.slot(a, b, width)` ✓: a stadium from a to b. Example: `j.region.slot([0, 0], [20, 0], 6)`.\n- `j.region.text(str, { size, font, align, valign, at, spacing })` ✓: text outlines; `size` is the cap height. Example: `j.region.text('PLA', { size: 6 })`.\n- `r.union(o) r.subtract(o) r.on(plane)` ✓: region booleans, and a plane to sit on. Example: `j.region.rect(20, 10).subtract(j.region.circle(2))`.\n- `r.area()` ◐ (counts straight edges and full circles only: arcs, slots and text come out too small): the region's area. Example: `j.region.circle(5).area()`.\n- `j.box(w, d, h, { center: true | 'xy' })` ✓: a box, corner at the origin by default. Example: `j.box(60, 30, 5)`.\n- `j.cyl(r, h, { center })` ✓: a cylinder along Z. Example: `j.cyl(mm(5), mm(40), { center: true })`.\n- `j.sphere(r)` `j.cone(r1, r2, h)` `j.torus(R, r)` ✓: more primitives. Example: `j.sphere(mm(13))`.\n- `shape.union .subtract .intersect .moved(tx)` ✓: exact regularised booleans. Example: `cube.intersect(ball).subtract(rods)`.\n- `shape.volume() shape.bbox()` ✓: measured on the kernel. Example: `j.sphere(10).volume()`.\n\nFonts are OFL: Atkinson Hyperlegible Bold (`sans`) and IBM Plex Mono Bold (`mono`).\n\n## Sketches and constraints\n\nA sketch with no `.constrain()` and no `.dim()` is **explicit**: its numbers are fixed and its DOF is 0. Any constraint or dimension makes it **constrained**: the numbers become starting positions. `j.sketch(id, { mode })` overrides the choice. The [solver doc](lab:doc?id=solver) explains both.\n\n- `j.sketch(id, { on, mode })` ✓: a sketch on a plane or a planar face. Example: `j.sketch('base', { on: j.plane.XY })`.\n- `.point(id, at)` ✓: a point. Example: `.point('p', [20, 6])`.\n- `.line(id, a, b)` ✓: a segment; ends may be refs. Example: `.line('l2', 'l1.end', [40, 30])`.\n- `.polyline(id, pts, { closed })` ✓: segments `id.1`, `id.2`, …. Example: `.polyline('p', pts, { closed: true })`.\n- `.arc(id, { center, start, end } | { through })` ✓: an arc. Example: `.arc('a', { center: [0, 0], start: [40, 0], end: [0, 40] })`.\n- `.circle(id, r, { at })` ✓: a circle. Example: `.circle('hole', 2.2, { at: [40, 4] })`.\n- `.ellipse(id, rx, ry, { at, angle })` ✓: an ellipse. Example: `.ellipse('e', 10, 6)`.\n- `.spline(id, pts, { degree, fit, closed })` ✓: a B-spline through or controlled by points. Example: `.spline('s', pts, { fit: 'interp' })`.\n- `.rect(id, w, h, { at, center, r })` ✓: sides `id.bottom right top left`, corners `id.bl br tr tl`. Example: `.rect('outline', 'w', 'd', { center: true, r: 'r' })`.\n- `.polygon(id, n, r, { at, inscribed })` ✓: a regular polygon; `inscribed: false` takes the apothem. Example: `.polygon('hex', 6, 'af / 2', { inscribed: false })`.\n- `.slot(id, a, b, width)` ✓: a slot: `id.l1 l2 a1 a2`. Example: `.slot('s', [0, 0], [20, 0], 6)`.\n- `.text(id, str, { size, font, at, align })` ✓: text as a fixed outline. Example: `.text('t', 'jig', { size: 5 })`.\n- `.offset(id, srcIds, d)` ✓: offset curves. Example: `.offset('o', ['p'], -1.2)`.\n- `.fillet(id, cornerRef, r)` `.chamfer(id, cornerRef, d)` ✓: round or cut a corner. Example: `.fillet('f', 'r.tr', 3)`.\n- `.trim(id, at)` ✓: split an entity at a point. Example: `.trim('l1', [20, 0])`.\n- `.mirror(srcIds, axisId)` `.pattern(srcIds, pattern)` ✓: copies `<src>.m`, `<src>.p<k>`. Example: `.pattern(['h1'], j.pattern.mirror(j.axis.X))`.\n- `.construction(...ids)` ✓: reference geometry, left out of profiles. Example: `.construction('axis')`.\n- `.project(id, sel)` `.intersect(id, sel)` ✓: curves from the model. Example: `.project('e', j.feat('body').endFace().edges())`.\n- `.import(id, region)` ✓: a region as fixed curves. Example: `.import('logo', j.region.text('A'))`.\n- `.constrain(...cs)` ✓: add constraints. Example: `.constrain(j.c.horizontal('l1'))`.\n- `.dim(target, value, { driven })` ✓: a dimension: line length, circle diameter, arc radius, `[a, b]` distance or angle, `{ h }`, `{ v }`. Example: `.dim('l1', 60)`.\n- `.solver(slot)` ✓: `'dogleg'` (default), `'lm'`, `'newton'`, `j.solver.lm({ maxIter })` or a lambda. Example: `.solver('lm')`.\n- `.strict()` ✓: under-constrained becomes an error. Example: `.strict()`.\n- `.profiles()` `.profile(pick)` `.wire(ids)` ✓: every closed region (even–odd nesting), one of them, or an open path. Example: `sk.profiles()`.\n- `j.c.coincident horizontal vertical parallel perpendicular tangent equal symmetric midpoint concentric collinear on fix distance distanceX distanceY angle radius diameter length` ✓: constraints; each takes refs and an optional `{ id }`. Example: `j.c.tangent('a1', 'l2')`.\n- `j.solver.dogleg lm newton bfgs` ✓: solver strategies. Example: `j.solver.lm({ maxIter: 200 })`.\n- `j.sketch3d(id).line .polyline .spline .helix(id, { axis, pitch, turns, r })` ✓: 3D paths for sweeps. Example: `j.sketch3d('path').helix('h', { pitch: mm(4), turns: 3, r: mm(10) })`.\n- `j.Sketch` ✓: the class. Example: `sk instanceof j.Sketch`.\n\nRefs: `'l1'`, `'l1.start'`, `'l1.end'`, `'l1.mid'`, `'c1.center'`, `'origin'`, `'X'`, `'Y'`, `[x, y]`. Diagnostics: `sketch.underConstrained` (warning, with the free entities), `sketch.redundant` (warning; the constraint is dropped), `sketch.overConstrained` (error, with the minimal conflicting set and a driven dimension to try), `sketch.ref`.\n\n## Parts and features\n\n- `j.part(name)` ✓: an empty part. Example: `j.part('box')`.\n- `.material(m) .color(c)` ✓: material for mass; display colour. Example: `.material(j.material('PETG'))`.\n- `.color(sel, c)` ◐ (recorded only): per-face colour. Example: `.color(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'red')`.\n- `.port(name, frame)` ✓: a named frame for joints. Example: `.port('hinge', j.frame({ … }))`.\n- `.fit(profile)` ✓: the fit profile for every fit-aware slot. Example: `.fit(j.fit.profile('mk4-petg', { … }))`.\n- `.set(k, v) .use(...fns) .with(fn)` ✓: raw settings, presets and rule sets, a builder function. Example: `.use(j.rules.fdm)`.\n- `.extrude(profile, extent, { op, taper, twist, herringbone, id })` ✓ (`to` uses the face centre): extent: `Q`, `{ symmetric }`, `{ two: [a, b] }`, `{ through: 'all' }`, `{ to: faceSel }`. Example: `.extrude(sk.profiles(), 'h', { id: 'body' })`.\n- `.extrude(…, { twist: deg(a) })` ✓ (not with `taper`): the end section turned by a about the sketch normal; with `herringbone: true` the middle section is turned and the end brought back. Example: `.extrude(j.region.rect(20, 10), mm(30), { twist: deg(90) })`.\n- `.cut(profile, extent, opts)` ✓: extrude with `op: 'cut'`; goes into the material. Example: `.cut(pocket.profiles(), 'h - floor', { id: 'pocket' })`.\n- `.revolve(profile, axis, angle = turn(1), opts)` ✓: the axis lies in the profile's plane. Example: `.revolve(sec.profiles(), j.axis.Z, turn(1), { id: 'ring' })`.\n- `.sweep(profile, path, { frame, id })` ✓: frame `'rmf'` (default), `'frenet'`, `'fixed'`. Example: `.sweep(c.profiles(), helix, { id: 'wire' })`.\n- `.loft([profiles], { ruled, id })` ✓: a solid through sections. Example: `.loft([a.profiles(), b.profiles()], { id: 'duct' })`.\n- `.hole(at, spec, opts)` ✓: holes at points, sketch points or circle edges. Example: `.hole(pts, j.hole.clearance('M3'), { id: 'holes' })`.\n- `.fillet(edges, r | (edge, ctx) => r, { id })` ✓: rolling-ball fillet, concave edges first. Example: `.fillet(j.feat('body').sideFaces().edges('|Z'), mm(3), { id: 'round' })`.\n- `.chamfer(edges, d, { d2, angle, id })` ✓: a chamfer. Example: `.chamfer(j.feat('body').startFace().edges(), mm(0.5), { id: 'foot' })`.\n- `.shell(openFaces, t, { direction, id })` ✓: hollow with open faces. Example: `.shell(j.feat('body').endFace(), 'wall', { id: 'shell' })`.\n- `.draft(faces, angle, { pull, neutral })` ✓: taper faces against a pull direction. Example: `.draft(j.feat('body').sideFaces(), deg(2))`.\n- `.rib(wire, t, { direction })` ✓: a wall grown from lines. Example: `.rib(sk.wire(['l1']), mm(2))`.\n- `.thicken(faces, t, { op })` ✓: a solid from faces. Example: `.thicken(j.feat('s').faces(), mm(1))`.\n- `.emboss(region, depth, { on, op, id })` ✓ (planar faces, no wrap): raised or cut text and logos on a planar face. Example: `.emboss(j.region.text('PLA', { size: 5 }), mm(0.6), { on: j.feat('body').endFace() })`.\n- `.pattern(j.feat(id) | bodySel, pattern, { onFail, id })` ✓: replay a feature's tool. Example: `.pattern(j.feat('boss'), j.pattern.circular(j.axis.Z, { count: 6 }), { id: 'bosses' })`.\n- `.mirror(targets, plane, { id })` ✓: replay a feature reflected. Example: `.mirror(j.feat('pin'), j.plane.YZ, { id: 'pins' })`.\n- `.boolean(op, targetSel, shapeOrSel)` ✓: an explicit boolean. Example: `.boolean('cut', j.sel.bodies(), j.cyl(2, 20))`.\n- `.split(bodySel, plane)` ✓: cut a body in two. Example: `.split(j.feat('body').body(), j.plane.YZ, { id: 'cut' })`.\n- `.move(sel, tx)` `.copy(sel, tx)` ✓: move or copy bodies. Example: `.copy(j.feat('body').body(), j.tx.move(50, 0, 0))`.\n- `.pushPull(planarFaces, d)` ✓: move a planar face. Example: `.pushPull(j.feat('body').endFace(), mm(5))`.\n- `.add(shape | part | libItem, { at, op, leaf, fit, id })` ✓: add a primitive, part or library feature. Example: `.add(j.box(60, 5, 40), { at: j.tx.move(0, 0, 5), id: 'upright' })`.\n- `.feature(id, (bodies, ctx) => shapes)` ✓: a custom pure feature. Example: `.feature('twin', (b) => [...b, j.box(5, 5, 5)])`.\n- `.suppress(id) .unsuppress(id) .rollback(id) .insertAfter(id, fn) .features()` ✓: edit the timeline. Example: `.insertAfter('body', b => b.chamfer(…))`.\n- `.deleteFace` `.offsetFace` `.import` ✗: direct edits and imports.\n\n`op` is `'new'`, `'join'`, `'cut'` or `'intersect'`. The first solid feature is `new`; later ones `join` when they touch a body, else `new`; the choice is an `op` decision. Give `{ id }` to anything you refer to: a generated id (`extrude1`) raises `ref.autoId`. Failures are diagnostics: `feature.failed`, `fillet.tooLarge` (with the largest radius that works), `brep.invalid` (with the wall that would work), `boolean.nonManifold`, `pattern.instanceSkipped`.\n\n## Selectors and naming\n\nFaces and edges are named by genealogy: `<feature>/<role>[<source>]`, as in `body/side[base.outline.bottom]`, `body/end`, `body/start`, `shell/inner[body/side[base.outline.bottom]]` and `round/face[body/side[base.r.left] ∩ body/end]`. A face split by a later feature keeps its name with `#a`, `#b`. The [naming doc](lab:doc?id=naming) explains why these survive edits.\n\n- `j.sel.faces(q?) .edges(q?) .vertices(q?) .bodies(q?)` ✓: a query over the whole model. Example: `j.sel.faces('>Z')`.\n- `j.sel.names(kind, names)` ✓: entities by persistent name. Example: `j.sel.names('faces', ['body/end'])`.\n- `j.feat(id)` ✓: a feature's own topology: `.faces(q) .edges(q) .body() .endFace() .startFace() .sideFaces() .sideFace('l1') .holes() .bores() .capFaces()`. Example: `j.feat('body').endFace().edges()`.\n- `j.on(componentId, sel)` ✓: a selector inside an assembly component. Example: `j.on('lid', j.sel.faces('<Z'))`.\n- `.of(target) .where(pred) .type(t) .radius(r) .near(pt)` ✓: restrict. Example: `.where(e => e.convex)`.\n- `.adjacent(sel) .tangentChain() .loop()` ✓: grow along topology. Example: `.tangentChain()`.\n- `.largest() .smallest() .sortBy(fn) .first() .last()` ✓: pick by measure. Example: `j.sel.faces('%plane').largest()`.\n- `.nth(i)` ✓ (raises `ref.fragile`): pick by index. Example: `j.sel.edges().nth(3)`.\n- `.and(o) .or(o) .minus(o) .not()` ✓: set algebra. Example: `a.or(b)`.\n- `.faces() .edges() .vertices()` ✓: navigate. Example: `j.feat('body').endFace().edges()`.\n- `.created(featId) .modified(featId)` ✓: by history. Example: `j.sel.faces().created('pocket')`.\n- `.bed()` ✓: faces touching the bed in the chosen orientation. Example: `j.sel.faces().bed()`.\n- `.expect(n | [min, max])` ✓: a count assertion; a mismatch is the error `ref.expect`. Example: `.edges('|Z').expect(4)`.\n- `j.isSelector(x)` ✓: test. Example: `j.isSelector(j.sel.faces())`.\n\nThe query language is CadQuery's: `+Z` (normal points +Z), `|Z` (parallel), `#Z` (perpendicular), `>Z` and `<Z` (farthest and nearest along Z), `%plane %cylinder %line %circle`, any axis or `(x,y,z)`, combined with `and`, `or`, `not`. Predicates in `where`: edges have `convex`, `concave`, `smooth`, `length`, `radius`, `dir`, `center`; faces have `normal`, `area`, `center`, `radius`, `axis`.\n\n## Holes\n\n- `j.hole.simple(d, depth | 'through')` ✓: a plain bore at `d`. Example: `j.hole.simple(j.fit.hole('pin', 'slide'), mm(6))`.\n- `j.hole.counterbore(d, cbD, cbDepth, depth)` ✓: a bore with a counterbore. Example: `j.hole.counterbore(mm(3.4), mm(6.5), mm(3))`.\n- `j.hole.countersink(d, csD, angle, depth)` ✓: a bore with a countersink. Example: `j.hole.countersink(mm(3.4), mm(7), deg(90))`.\n- `j.hole.countersink('M3', { fit, depth })` ✓: ISO 10642 head, fit-aware. Example: `j.hole.countersink('M3', { fit: 'slide' })`.\n- `j.hole.clearance('M3', { fit, depth })` ✓: d + 2·gap + hole compensation: M3 slide is 3.5 mm. Example: `j.hole.clearance('M4', { fit: 'free' })`.\n- `j.hole.tapped('M3', { depth, method })` ✓: `'self-tap'` drills d − 0.1 mm; `'printed'` models an ISO internal thread with core Ø = D1 + 2·gap, starting 0.5 mm below the mouth; `depth: 'through'` runs it to the far side. Example: `j.hole.tapped('M3', { depth: mm(8), method: 'printed' })`.\n- `j.hole.insert('M3x5.7')` ✓: a heat-set insert hole, Ø4.0 × 6.7 mm. Example: `j.hole.insert('M3x5.7')`.\n- `j.hole.nutTrap('M3', { kind, from })` ✓: a hex pocket and a bolt hole. Example: `j.hole.nutTrap('M3', { from: 'side' })`.\n\nA hole whose axis is perpendicular to the build direction becomes a 45° teardrop, tip up, recorded as a `teardrop` decision. Face names: `<id>/bore[<point>]`, `floor`, `cbore`, `cbFloor`, `csink`, `tear.l`, `tear.r`, `nut.*`.\n\n## Library\n\n- `j.lib.thread('M8x1.25' | 'M8' | 'Tr40x3' | { d, pitch }, { length, internal, profile, fit, starts, hand, through })` ✓ (1–2 s cold, then cached): an ISO 68, trapezoid or buttress thread along the frame's z; `internal: true` or `op: 'cut'` cuts it for nuts and lids. The fit class moves the radii, so an M8 slide thread is modelled at 7.6 mm major and 6.247 mm minor. Faces `<id>/core`, `flank.lo`, `flank.hi`, `crest`, `start`, `end`. Example: `j.lib.thread('M8x1.25', { length: mm(12) })`.\n- `j.lib.gear.spur({ module, teeth, pressure = 20°, shift = 0, backlash = 'fit', width, bore })` ✓: exact involutes from the ISO 53 rack, a trochoidal root and real undercut below about 17 teeth; `backlash` thins each tooth by the fit class; ports `axis` and `pitch`. Example: `j.lib.gear.spur({ module: mm(1.5), teeth: 20, width: mm(6) })`.\n- `j.lib.gear.helical({ …, helix = 30°, hand })` ✓ (module is the transverse module): a helical gear. Example: `j.lib.gear.rack({ module: 1, teeth: 20 })`.\n- `j.lib.gear.bevel` ✗: bevel gears.\n- `j.lib.hinge.pin({ knuckles = 5, length = 30, d = 3, clearance = 'printInPlace', wall = 1.6, plates })` ✓: a print-in-place pin hinge along the frame's z; add with `{ leaf: 'a' | 'b' }` to two parts, or `{ leaf: 'both' }` for both leaves as separate bodies of one part; `plates: { width, thickness }` adds flat leaves. Example: `.add(j.lib.hinge.pin({ length: mm(30), plates: { width: mm(20), thickness: mm(2) } }), { leaf: 'both' })`.\n- `j.lib.hinge.living({ length, thickness = 0.4, gap = 1, overlap = 2, material = 'PP' })` ✓: a living hinge; warns `print.layerStrength` when the material's allowable strain is under 5 %. Example: `j.lib.hinge.living({ length: mm(30) })`.\n- `j.lib.dovetail({ width, depth, angle, fit, length })` ✓: tail with `op: 'join'`, socket with `op: 'cut'`. Example: `j.lib.dovetail({ width: mm(10), depth: mm(5) })`.\n- `j.lib.insert('M3x5.7').boss({ wall, height })` ✓: a boss with an insert hole. Example: `j.lib.insert('M3x5.7').boss({ wall: lw(3) })`.\n- `j.lib.magnet({ d, h })` `j.lib.dowel({ d, l, fit })` ✓: a press-fit pocket; a dowel. Example: `j.lib.magnet({ d: mm(6), h: mm(3) })`.\n- `j.lib.snap.cantilever({ length = 12, thickness = 1.6, deflection = 1, width = 6, material = 'PETG', angle = 30°, retention = 90° })` ✓: a snap hook along the frame's z with an insertion ramp and a retention face, strain-checked (`print.snapStrain`); its info gives strain and the deflection and insertion forces in N. Example: `j.lib.snap.cantilever({ length: mm(12), thickness: mm(1.6), deflection: mm(1) })`.\n- `j.lib.screw(std, 'M3x8')` `j.lib.nut(std, 'M3')` `j.lib.washer` `j.lib.pin({ d, l })` ✓: simple hardware parts. Example: `j.lib.screw('ISO 7380', 'M3x8')`.\n- `j.lib.snap.annular` `j.lib.snap.torsion` `j.lib.bearing` ✗.\n\n## Fit\n\n- `j.fit.classes(obj?)` ✓: the clearance classes per side; with no argument, the defaults. Example: `j.fit.classes()`.\n- `j.fit.profile(name, { classes, holeComp, xyComp, elephantFoot })` ✓: a calibrated profile. Example: `j.fit.profile('mk4-petg', { classes: { slide: mm(0.135) } })`.\n- `j.fit.gap(cls)` ✓: the gap per side of a class. Example: `j.fit.gap('printInPlace')` is 0.4 mm.\n- `j.fit.hole(d, cls)` ✓: modelled hole: d + 2·gap + hole compensation. Example: `j.fit.hole(mm(3), 'slide')` is 3.5 mm.\n- `j.fit.pin(d, cls)` ✓: modelled pin: d − 2·gap + 2·xyComp. Example: `j.fit.pin(mm(3), 'press')` is 2.9 mm.\n- `j.fit.coupon({ kind, range, steps, pin })` ✓: a printable test part with graded gaps. Example: `j.fit.coupon({ kind: 'pin-hole', steps: 10 })`.\n- `j.fit.calibrate(rows)` ✓: least squares `actual = a·nominal + b`, and classes from the outcomes. Example: `j.fit.calibrate([{ pin: 6.04, holes, outcomes }])`.\n- `j.stack(name).add(q.pm(t), { dir })` ✓: a tolerance stack: `.worstCase() .rss() .monteCarlo({ n, seed, dist })`. Example: `j.stack('gap').add(mm(56).pm(0.15))`.\n\nDefault classes, per side: press 0.05, snug 0.10, slide 0.20, free 0.30, printInPlace 0.40 mm; hole compensation 0.1 mm. Resolved values are params named `gap.<class>`, with provenance. Until a profile is calibrated, a fit-aware feature raises the info `fit.uncalibrated`.\n\n## Inspect and materials\n\n- `j.material(name)` ✓: PLA, PETG, ABS, ASA, PP, TPU-95A, PA-CF, resin-standard. An **explicit** sketch is a drawing: its numbers are coordinates and nothing moves. A **constrained** sketch is a system of equations: its numbers are a first guess, and the solver moves the geometry until every constraint and dimension holds. This page explains how the compiler chooses, how it counts what is still free, what it does with constraints that repeat or contradict each other, and how a drag on the sheet works. [Chapter 3](lab:book?ch=3) derives the same results with figures.\n\n## Explicit or constrained\n\nA sketch with no `.constrain()` and no `.dim()` is explicit. Every entity made by `point`, `line`, `polyline`, `arc`, `circle`, `ellipse`, `spline`, `text` and `import` is fixed where its numbers put it, so the sketch has no degrees of freedom and needs no solving. Most presets are written this way, with every coordinate an expression of the params:\n\n```js\nj.sketch('outline', { on: j.plane.XY })\n  .polyline('p', [[0, 0], [60, 0], [60, 8], [8, 8], [8, 40], [0, 40]],\n    { closed: true })\n```\n\nAdd one constraint or one dimension and the whole sketch becomes constrained: the same six points are now starting positions. The choice is a decision in the Program (`sketch.mode → constrained: constraints or dimensions given: coordinates are starting positions`), and `j.sketch(id, { mode: 'explicit' | 'constrained' })` overrides it. Helpers that take sizes (`rect`, `polygon`, `slot`, `fillet`, `chamfer`) add constraints for the sizes they are given in both modes, so a rectangle keeps its width while its neighbours move.\n\nWhy two modes: a drawing written as coordinates is exact and needs no solver, but it cannot be dragged and does not say which sizes matter. A constrained sketch says which sizes matter (this leg is 8 mm, these lines are parallel) and leaves the rest to the solver, at the price of having to say enough.\n\n## Unknowns, equations and DOF\n\nThe solver turns the sketch into numbers. Each entity has parameters: a point has 2 (x, y), a line 4 (both ends), a circle 3 (centre and radius), an arc 5. Each constraint is one or more equations in those parameters: a coincidence is 2 equations, horizontal is 1, a length is 1, fixing a line is 4. Let $n$ be the number of parameters and $J$ the Jacobian of all equations with respect to them. The **degrees of freedom** are\n\n$$\n\\mathrm{DOF} = n - \\operatorname{rank} J .\n$$\n\nThe rank, not the number of equations, is what counts, because some equations can follow from others. The L-shaped outline above, constrained, has 6 lines and so $n = 24$. Its 6 corner coincidences give 12 equations. Fixing the first corner, making the lines alternately horizontal and vertical and giving the bottom a length of 60 mm adds 9 more: rank 21, DOF 3. Those three are the height, the thickness of the foot and the thickness of the upright, and the warning `sketch.underConstrained: 3 DOF remain` lists the entities that can still move. Three more dimensions (`p.6` 40 mm, `p.2` 8 mm, `p.5` 8 mm) bring the rank to 24 and the status to `fully`.\n\nUnder-constrained is a warning, not an error: the sketch still solves, with the free parameters left where they started. `.strict()` makes it an error, for sketches that must not drift.\n\n## Redundant and conflicting\n\nTwo things can go wrong with an equation that adds no rank.\n\nA **redundant** constraint agrees with the others. In the fully constrained outline, `parallel(p.1, p.3)` already follows from `horizontal(p.1)` and `horizontal(p.3)`. The compiler finds that the new row lies in the span of the existing ones, drops it and warns: `sketch.redundant: parallel(p.1, p.3) is implied by horizontal×2; dropped`. The geometry is unchanged.\n\nA **conflicting** constraint disagrees. Give the middle line `p.3` a length of 50 mm when the others already make it 60 − 8 = 52 mm, and no point satisfies all of them. Reporting \"over-constrained\" is not enough: the person needs to know which constraints to look at. It finds a **minimal conflicting set**, a set of constraints that cannot all hold but any one of which can be removed to fix it, by a deletion filter: drop each constraint in turn and keep it out if the rest still conflict. The diagnostic names six:\n\n```text\nsketch.overConstrained: no solution; minimal conflicting set:\nvertical(p.2) · vertical(p.4) · vertical(p.6) · length(p.1) = 60 mm\n· length(p.5) = 8 mm · length(p.3) = 50 mm\nfix: drop one of the 6; e.g. make dim(p.3) driven (it measures 52 mm)\n```\n\nA **driven** dimension, `.dim('p.3', null, { driven: true })`, measures instead of constraining: it reports 52 mm and adds no equation.\n\n## The solver\n\nThe compiler first splits the system into **clusters**, the connected components of the graph whose nodes are parameters and whose edges are equations, and solves each on its own. Two rectangles that share nothing are two clusters; a drag on one never touches the other.\n\nEach cluster is solved by Powell's **DogLeg** method: a step that blends Gauss–Newton (the minimum-norm solution of the linearised equations, through a rank-revealing QR factorisation) with steepest descent, inside a trust region. If DogLeg does not converge, Levenberg–Marquardt takes over. The result is recorded per cluster: `outline/cluster0 solver → dogleg (default; converged in 0 iterations, residual 0e+0)`. Zero iterations means the starting positions already satisfied every equation, which is common when the coordinates were drawn to size. `.solver('lm')`, `.solver('newton')` or `j.solver.lm({ maxIter: 200 })` choose another method, and a lambda `(cluster, ctx) => ctx.default` can choose per cluster.\n\n## Dragging\n\nA sketch with free DOF can be dragged on the sheet: open a sketch-only program, or select a sketch in the graph, and the free points are drawn in blue. Dragging one sends a **drag** to the engine: move this ref to this point, keep every constraint, and change as little else as possible. The engine re-solves only the dragged point's cluster, starting from the current solution.\n\nIn the under-constrained outline (3 DOF), dragging the top of the upright, `p.4.end`, to (14, 46) moves the upright to x = 14 mm and the top to y = 46 mm. The first line, fixed and dimensioned, stays where it was, and the foot keeps its 8 mm height because nothing pulled it. The sketch still has 3 DOF afterwards: a drag moves the geometry; it adds no constraints. To keep a dragged size, write it as a dimension.\n\nA drag changes only what the sheet shows; it never feeds a compile. Compiles have their own continuity: a constrained sketch whose text is unchanged starts from its last solution in the same worker (a **warm start**), so moving a parameter keeps the solver on the same branch, the same flip of an arc or side of a line, instead of jumping to another solution. An edited sketch, an explicit sketch and a fresh worker solve cold, from the coordinates in the script.\n\n## When the solver gives up\n\n`sketch.unsolved` means the cluster did not converge from the starting positions. A constrained sketch whose guesses are far from any solution, or whose constraints have two solutions and the guess sits between them (a tangent arc that could flip), is the usual cause. Draw the starting coordinates closer to the intended shape, or try `.solver('lm')`. `sketch.ref` means a constraint names something that does not exist, such as `p.l1` for a polyline whose lines are `p.1`, `p.2`, ….\n"}}
